# Squared-logarithmic randomized k-server on arbitrary metrics

Coordinate `EN-114-000-0007-2-2026-PR-T4-UK-EX-M1-B-AL-H-0238` | `PM-110.1` | status ACTIVE | original slug `Squared-logarithmic-randomized-k-server-on-arbitrary-metrics-September-24-2026` | date 2026-09-24 | derived from the openai/math preprint Squared-logarithmic-randomized-k-server-on-arbitrary-metrics-September-24-2026, modified (Apache-2.0). Publication status: DRAFT.

<!-- PM:BEGIN pm.node.v2 -->
```json
{
  "abstract_verbatim": "We prove that randomized <i>k</i>-server has competitive ratio $`O((\\log(k+1))^2)`$ on every metric space against oblivious request sequences, matching the known worst-case lower bound. For each metric and initial configuration, one policy works for all finite request sequences, including on infinite and unbounded spaces. When the initial server positions are distinct, no additive term is needed.",
```

<a id="pm-s1"></a>
## 1. Abstract and result contract

> We prove that randomized <i>k</i>-server has competitive ratio $`O((\log(k+1))^2)`$ on every metric space against oblivious request sequences, matching the known worst-case lower bound. For each metric and initial configuration, one policy works for all finite request sequences, including on infinite and unbounded spaces. When the initial server positions are distinct, no additive term is needed.

- **claim-main-theorem** (theorem, [source](#stmt-thm-main)): There is an absolute constant $C<\infty$ such that, for every integer $k\ge2$, every metric space $(X,d)$ with at least $k+1$ points, and every $s\in X^k$, there exist a randomized online policy $A$ and a finite $B\ge0$ satisfying $$\label{eq:main} \mathbb E\,\mathop{\mathrm{cost}}_{A,s}(\sigma) \le C\bigl(\log(k+1)\bigr)^2\mathop{\mathrm{OPT}}_{X,d,k,s}(\sigma)+B$$ for every finite request sequence $\sigma$. The constant $C$ is independent of every property of the metric. The number $B$ may depend on $(X,d),k,s$, but not on the sequence or its length. The same policy serves all finite sequences and horizons. If the initial positions are distinct, one may take $B=0$.
  - Explanation: Randomized k-server has a squared-logarithmic competitive ratio on every metric against oblivious requests. One policy serves all finite sequences, and the additive term disappears when the initial positions are distinct.
- **claim-lazy-simulation** (bound, [source](#stmt-lem-lazy-simulation)): Suppose a causal sequence of simulated configurations $C_0,C_1,\ldots,C_H$ contains the request $r_t$ at each time $t\ge1$. From any actual start $A_0$, a strongly lazy policy serves the same requests and satisfies, pathwise, $$\mathop{\mathrm{cost}}(A_0;r_1,\ldots,r_H) \le \mathsf M(A_0,C_0)+\sum_{t=1}^H\mathsf M(C_{t-1},C_t).$$ The conclusion also holds if the matching distances on the right are replaced by any larger specified movement costs.
  - Explanation: Any causal sequence of simulated configurations that contains each request can be followed by a strongly lazy policy. Its cost is bounded pathwise by the matching distances between successive simulated configurations.
- **claim-finite-law-estimate** (bound, [source](#stmt-prop-finite-law)): There is an absolute $a<\infty$ with the following property. For every finite metric $Y$ of positive diameter, every $k\ge2$, and every start $s\in Y^k$, there is a finite $D_0=D_0(Y,k,s)$ such that, for every finite horizon $H$ and every probability law on $Y^H$, there is a causal strongly lazy policy satisfying $$\label{eq:finite-law} \mathbb E\,\mathop{\mathrm{cost}}\le a\ell^2\mathbb E\,\mathop{\mathrm{OPT}}+D_0 .$$ The policy may depend on the law and horizon. The number $D_0$ does not.
  - Explanation: For a known finite input law on a finite metric there is a causal strongly lazy policy with a squared-logarithmic ratio. Its additive term does not depend on the law or the horizon.
- **claim-ball-mass-telescope** (bound, [source](#stmt-lem-scale-logarithms)): Fix $0<a<b$, a nonnegative measure $\mu$, and an offset $c_0\ge0$. On a consecutive finite interval of scale indices suppose $$c\le A_j:=c_0+\mu(B(x,ar_j)) \le B_j:=c_0+\mu(B(x,br_j))\le K, \qquad c>0.$$ If $p\ge1$ is an integer with $\tau^p a\ge b$, then $$\sum_j\log(B_j/A_j)\le p\log(K/c).$$
  - Explanation: Sums of logarithms of ratios of nested ball masses over geometric scales telescope to a single logarithm of the extreme masses.
- **claim-dominated-allocation** (theorem, [source](#stmt-thm-allocation)): There are absolute constants $C_0,C_1$ and $\tau_0$ with the following property. For $\tau\ge\tau_0$, the observed histories determine amounts $q_v(t)$ satisfying $$\label{eq:allocation-constraints} q_{\mathrm{root}}=k,\qquad 0\le q_v\le C_0m_v,\qquad \sum_{i\prec v}q_i\le q_v,\qquad q_{x_t}\ge1.$$ Moreover, with $\ell=1+\log(k+1)$, $$\label{eq:allocation-bound} V:=\mathbb E\sum_{t\ge1}\sum_{v\ne\mathrm{root}} r_{\|v\|-1}\|q_v(t)-q_v(t-1)\| \le C_1\ell P+D_{\mathcal T}.$$ Here sums stop at the chosen finite horizon. The finite constant $D_{\mathcal T}$ depends only on the fixed tree, radii, and $k$; it can be chosen independently of the input law, horizon, and number of histories. No initial allocation is prescribed.
  - Explanation: On a separated tree, observed histories determine subtree amounts that dominate the serving requirement, are bounded by posterior expected counts, allow parking at internal vertices, and move at a cost of logarithmic factor times the comparator movement.
- **claim-rank-functions** (construction, [source](#stmt-lem-rank-functions)): There are absolute constants $p_*,s_*,\epsilon_*>0$, with $p_*<s_*<1$, and functions $l^\beta:[0,1]\to[0,1]$ for $3\le\beta\le3+\epsilon_*$ such that:* 1. *$l^\beta$ is convex and nonincreasing, equals $1-\beta p$ on $[0,p_*]$, is positive on $[0,s_*)$, and is zero on $[s_*,1]$;* 2. *$l^\beta$ is affine and nonincreasing in $\beta$, and the square roots $\sqrt{l^\beta}$ have a common absolute Lipschitz constant;* 3. *if $g(p)=-\partial_\beta l^\beta(p)$, then $$\label{eq:rank-derivative} \frac1{96}l^\beta(p)\le g(p)\le l^\beta(p) \qquad(p\in[p_*/4,1]).$$* *One may take $p_*=1/24$, $s_*=5/8$, and $\epsilon_*=1/100$.
  - Explanation: Explicit convex nonincreasing rank functions with an affine initial piece, a cutoff and a controlled parameter derivative provide compatible lower bounds for subtree amounts.
- **claim-superadditivity** (bound, [source](#stmt-lem-superadditivity)): For every internal vertex $v$ and every allowed $\beta$, $$\label{eq:rank-superadditivity} \sum_{i\prec v}L_i^\beta\le L_v^\beta,\qquad \sum_{i\prec v}M_i\le M_v\le m_v.$$ No independence between child counts is required.
  - Explanation: By a majorization argument, the sum of the children's rank sums is at most the parent's, with no independence between child counts.
- **claim-rank-tracking** (bound, [source](#stmt-lem-rank-tracking)): Let $p_t,p_t^-\in[0,1]$ obey $\mathbb E[p_t^-\mid\mathcal F_{t-1}]=p_{t-1}$, and let $\mathcal E=\mathbb E\sum_t\|p_t-p_t^-\|$. If $f\ge0$ has an absolutely bounded Lipschitz constant for $\sqrt f$, then for $0<\xi<1/2$ there is a held estimate $\widehat f_t$ with $$\label{eq:rank-relative-tracker} (1+\xi)^{-1}f(p_t)\le\widehat f_t\le(1+\xi)f(p_t),\qquad \mathbb E\sum_t\|\widehat f_t-\widehat f_{t-1}\| \le\frac{C}{\xi}(\mathcal E+1).$$ In particular zeros are tracked exactly. A held estimate $\widehat p_t$ refreshed when $\|p_t-\widehat p_{t-1}\|>\delta$ has expected number of refreshes at most $\delta^{-2}(\mathcal E+1)$.
  - Explanation: A held estimate tracks a function of a rank probability within a relative tolerance while paying only for drift, via an affine potential evaluated before any refresh is decided.
- **claim-parameter-gaps** (bound, [source](#stmt-lem-parameter-gaps)): The data above satisfy, for an absolute constant $C$ and a finite $D_v^{\mathrm{data}}$ depending only on the fixed star and $k$, $$\label{eq:coarse-data-budget} \mathbb E\sum_t C_{v,t}\le C E_v+D_v^{\mathrm{data}}.$$ The constant is uniform in the law and horizon. Every active child obeys $$\label{eq:parameter-gap} 0\le h_i\le C\ell,\qquad \beta_i-\beta_v\ge\frac{c_g h_i}{\ell}$$ for an absolute $c_g>0$. If a dominant index and sides are present, then $0<u<1/4$, and with $H=1+\log(1/u)$ every side satisfies $h_i\ge cH$ for an absolute $c>0$. In addition, $n_i+B_i+\sup_\beta f_i^\beta\le C a_i$ at active children, and all these quantities vanish at inactive children.
  - Explanation: Coarse variation charges are bounded by the drift budget plus a finite constant. Every active child has a parameter gap proportional to its relative size.
- **claim-caps** (bound, [source](#stmt-lem-caps)): Given a sufficiently small absolute $\epsilon_b>0$, the remaining absolute tolerances can be chosen so that there are caps $d_i=n_i+b_i$, zero on inactive children, with $$\label{eq:cap-feasibility} \begin{aligned} f_i^{\beta_i}\le b_i\le f_i^{\beta_v} &&(i\text{ regular}),\\ T:=f_o^{\beta_v}\le b_o\le(1+\epsilon_b/\ell)T, \qquad c_DT\le D\le C_DT &&(o\text{ dominant}). \end{aligned}$$ Here $c_D,C_D>0$ are absolute, fixed independently of $\epsilon_b$ and the fine tolerances; in particular $D=0$ if and only if $T=0$. At every child $d_i\le C M_i\le C m_i$. Their variation away from wholesale changes satisfies $$\label{eq:fine-cap-budget} \mathbb E\sum_{t\notin\mathcal W_v}\sum_i\|d_i(t)-d_i(t-1)\| \le C\ell E_v+D_v^{\mathrm{cap}},$$ where $D_v^{\mathrm{cap}}$ is uniform in the law and horizon. At wholesale changes the old plus new caps are bounded by $C$ times the old plus new active totals.
  - Explanation: Fine child caps are tracked separately from the coarse data. They sit between the flex contributions at the two parameters and are dominated by the posterior counts.
- **claim-denominator-estimates** (bound, [source](#stmt-lem-denominators)): For every nonsingleton domain above, $$\label{eq:denominator-estimates} \int_0^\infty\frac{dy}{F_i(y)}\le C, \qquad \sum_{i\in I}\alpha_i F_i\bigl(\log(1/\alpha_i)\bigr) \le C\sum_{i\in I}\alpha_i h_i$$ for every $\alpha$ in its unit simplex. A summand at $\alpha_i=0$ means its continuous extension, which is zero. All constants are uniform as $u$ tends to zero.
  - Explanation: The reciprocal denominators of the proportion potentials have uniformly bounded integrals, and a weighted sum is controlled by the weighted parameters, uniformly as the dominant side proportion shrinks.
- **claim-simplex-update** (construction, [source](#stmt-lem-alpha-tracker)): Choose $C_\alpha$ sufficiently large after fixing $c_t$. For any old vector $\alpha^0$ in the same simplex, a global minimizer $$\label{eq:alpha-minimization} \alpha^1\in\mathop{\mathrm{arg\,min}}_\alpha \left\{G^\alpha(\alpha;B)+S\\|\alpha-\alpha^0\\|_1\right\}$$ satisfies <a href="#eq:alpha-feasibility" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:alpha-feasibility">[eq:alpha-feasibility]</a> and $$\label{eq:alpha-update-payment} S\\|\alpha^1-\alpha^0\\|_1 \le G^\alpha(\alpha^0;B)-G^\alpha(\alpha^1;B).$$ The same assertions hold with the automatic singleton rule.
  - Explanation: A penalized global minimizer over the simplex satisfies the removal feasibility constraints, and its movement is paid by the decrease of the proportion potential.
- **claim-side-update** (construction, [source](#stmt-lem-side-tracker)): Choose the absolute multiplier $C'_w$ sufficiently large after fixing the small constants. For every old vector $w^0$ in the side domain, a global minimizer $$\label{eq:side-minimization} w^1\in\mathop{\mathrm{arg\,min}}_w\left\{G^w(w;B,D)+D\\|w-w^0\\|_1\right\}$$ satisfies <a href="#eq:side-feasibility" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:side-feasibility">[eq:side-feasibility]</a>, and $$\label{eq:side-update-payment} D\\|w^1-w^0\\|_1\le G^w(w^0;B,D)-G^w(w^1;B,D).$$ This holds for arbitrary nonnegative $B,D$ with $S=\sum_iB_i$, without a requirement that they arise from ranks.
  - Explanation: A penalized minimizer over the bounded side domain always satisfies the upper feasibility inequality and the lower one when the core sum is comparable to the dominant flex mass; movement is paid by the potential.
- **claim-adaptive-proportion-movement** (bound, [source](#stmt-lem-adaptive-proportions)): There is a causal update schedule satisfying <a href="#eq:alpha-feasibility" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:alpha-feasibility">[eq:alpha-feasibility]</a> and <a href="#eq:side-feasibility" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:side-feasibility">[eq:side-feasibility]</a> after every step, such that $$\label{eq:proportion-movement} \mathbb E\sum_{t\text{ not wholesale}} \left(S_t\\|\alpha_t-\alpha_{t-1}\\|_1 +D_t\\|w_t-w_{t-1}\\|_1\right) \le C\ell E_v+D'_v.$$ Vectors are extended by zero to the fixed child set. The side term is zero without a dominant child. The simplex term includes the automatic singleton state; an empty active domain contributes zero. The finite constant $D'_v$ is uniform in the law and horizon.
  - Explanation: A causal update schedule keeps both feasibility conditions after every step, and its total weighted movement away from wholesale steps is bounded by the drift budget plus a finite constant.
- **claim-output-feasibility** (bound, [source](#stmt-lem-output-feasibility)): Suppose $q_v\ge L_v^{\beta_v}$. The selected output rule satisfies $$L_i^{\beta_i}\le q_i\le d_i\le C M_i\le C m_i, \qquad \sum_iq_i\le q_v,$$ with an absolute constant $C$. In particular every child output is nonnegative. The conclusions include empty active sets, empty side sets, and vanishing values of $S,D,T$.
  - Explanation: Both output rules keep each child above its rank lower bound and below its cap, and the children's total stays within the parent amount, including all degenerate cases.
- **claim-star-movement** (bound, [source](#stmt-lem-star-movement)): For the outputs above and a fixed star $v$, there is a finite $D_v^{\mathrm{out}}$, depending only on its fixed finite structure and $k$, such that $$\mathbb E\sum_t\sum_{i\text{ child of }v}\|q_i(t)-q_i(t-1)\| \le C_{\mathrm{par}}\mathbb E\sum_t\|q_v(t)-q_v(t-1)\| +C\ell E_v+D_v^{\mathrm{out}}. \label{eq:star-output-movement}$$ Here $C_{\mathrm{par}}$ and $C$ are absolute, independent of $k$, degree, depth, and $\tau$. In fact the output estimates below allow $C_{\mathrm{par}}=1$.
  - Explanation: Total movement of the children's outputs on a star is bounded by the parent's movement plus a logarithmic multiple of the local drift budget and a finite constant.
- **claim-pilot-compact** (theorem, [source](#stmt-lem-pilot-compact)): For every nonnegative measure $\mu$ on the finite space $Y$, the minimum in <a href="#eq:pilot-potential" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:pilot-potential">[eq:pilot-potential]</a> exists. For fixed $g$, the function $\mu\mapsto\mathcal L_r(\mu,g)$ is concave, and $$0\le\mathcal L_r(\mu,g)\le2r\mu(Y). \label{eq:pilot-size}$$ Every feasible integrand satisfies $$0\le F_z^g\le514,\qquad r\mathop{\mathrm{Lip}}(F_z^g)\le\Lambda:=L+1026/\sigma. \label{eq:pilot-global-bounds}$$ Moreover, the positive supports of the functions $b_s$ with $z_s>0$ are pairwise disjoint.
  - Explanation: The pilot potential is a minimum over a compact feasible set of linear functionals of the measure. The minimum exists, the potential is concave in the measure, and the integrands have uniform bounds.
- **claim-pilot-local** (theorem, [source](#stmt-lem-pilot-local)): Assume $h\le\delta$, and let $z$ be any minimizer in <a href="#eq:pilot-potential" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:pilot-potential">[eq:pilot-potential]</a>. Then the following assertions hold.* 1. *A center $s$ with $\sigma r/4\le d(s,x)\le Rr/2$ has $z_s=0$.* 2. *There is a unique positive center $s$ with $d(s,x)<\sigma r/4$. Its weight $z_s$ and normalized displacement $w=d(s,x)/r$ satisfy $$z_s\ge\tfrac34,\qquad 1-z_s\le\frac{512\gamma}{\sigma^2}t+514h, \qquad w\le(5+L)(h+t). \label{eq:pilot-local-structure}$$* 3. *If $d(x,y)<\sigma r/8$, then $$_+ \le C_{\mathrm{loc}}(h+t)\frac{d(x,y)}r, \label{eq:pilot-local-slope}$$ where one admissible constant is $$C_{\mathrm{loc}} =(L+1024/\sigma)(514+8/\sigma) +\frac{1024(5+L)}{\sigma^2}. \label{eq:pilot-local-constant}$$
  - Explanation: When mass concentrates near the request, any minimizer has no sites at intermediate distance and one dominant nearby site. Its displacement is controlled, giving a small positive slope at the request.
- **claim-pilot-drift** (bound, [source](#stmt-lem-pilot-drift)): Fix a template and constants as in <a href="#eq:pilot-concentration-constants" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:pilot-concentration-constants">[eq:pilot-concentration-constants]</a>. At each radius let $g_j$ be any function with $0\le g_j\le1$ and $r_j\mathop{\mathrm{Lip}}(g_j)\le L$. For measures satisfying <a href="#eq:pilot-mover" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:pilot-mover">[eq:pilot-mover]</a>, $$\sum_{j=1}^J [\mathcal L_{r_j}(\mu^+,g_j) -\mathcal L_{r_j}(\mu^-,g_j)]_+ \le C_{\mathrm{dr}}\ell e_x. \label{eq:pilot-drift}$$ The constant is independent of $Y$, $k$, $J$, and $\tau\ge2$. For example, set $$\begin{aligned} p_0&=\left\lceil\log_2(D_1/\gamma)\right\rceil,\nonumber\\ C_{\mathrm{dr}} &=\frac{2056}{\gamma}+\frac{8224}{\sigma} +\frac{2\Lambda p_0}{\delta\log2} +2C_{\mathrm{loc}} \left(\frac{p_0}{\log2}+2\gamma\right). \label{eq:pilot-drift-constant} \end{aligned}$$
  - Explanation: Summed over all scales, the positive drift of the pilot potentials caused by a mover is bounded by a constant times the logarithmic factor times the mover distance.
- **claim-pilot-edit-budget** (bound, [source](#stmt-cor-pilot-edits)): Let templates be indexed by a finite or countable set $I$, with fixed weights $\omega_\alpha\ge0$. Each template has fixed admissible parameters and the drift constant $C_{\mathrm{dr},\alpha}$ of Lemma <a href="#lem:pilot-drift" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:pilot-drift">19</a>. Assume $$A:=\sum_{\alpha\in I}\omega_\alpha<\infty, \qquad B:=\sum_{\alpha\in I}\omega_\alpha C_{\mathrm{dr},\alpha}<\infty.$$ At each step, filter and drift the counting measure while keeping the previous functions $g_{\alpha,j}$ fixed. Then edit these functions using the new history, retaining their template bounds. Suppose the weighted total potential drop in the edit stage is at least a nonnegative charge $c_t$, taking $c_t=0$ on steps without edits. Then $$\mathbb E\sum_t c_t \le B\ell Q+2kA\sum_{j=1}^Jr_j \le B\ell Q+\frac{2kr_0A}{\tau-1}. \label{eq:pilot-edit-budget}$$ In particular, if $A,B$ are absolute constants, the multiplicative budget is $O(\ell Q)$ and the additive bound is independent of the input law and horizon.
  - Explanation: Edits funded by potential decreases across a summably weighted family of templates have expected total charge of logarithmic order times the comparator cost plus a law-independent term.
- **claim-embedded-cost** (theorem, [source](#stmt-prop-embedded-cost)): There are finite alphabets $\mathcal A_1,\ldots,\mathcal A_J$, depending only on $Y,k$ and the fixed constants, and online random maps $$\xi_t:Y\longrightarrow\mathcal A_1\times\cdots\times\mathcal A_J$$ with the following properties. Each coordinate cell at level $j$ has diameter at most $40r_j$ and an anchor within $20r_j$ of all its points. Thus $\xi_t$ is injective. Give the full alphabet tree the leaf distance $r_{j-1}$ when the first differing coordinate is $j$. For the selected hidden members $X_t^1,\ldots,X_t^k$, $$\label{eq:embedded-cost} P:=\mathbb E\sum_t\sum_{a=1}^k d_{\mathcal T}\bigl(\xi_{t-1}(X_{t-1}^a),\xi_t(X_t^a)\bigr) \le C\ell Q+D_{\rm part}.$$ Here $C$ is absolute and $D_{\rm part}<\infty$ is independent of the input law and its horizon. A coordinate label common to successive maps has a fixed anchor, except for the explicit heavy-ball reuse below, when its anchor moves by at most $40r_j$.
  - Explanation: Online injective maps into a fixed finite-alphabet tree have cells of controlled diameter and anchors, and the hidden comparator's expected tree movement is bounded by a logarithmic multiple of its true cost plus a uniform constant.
- **claim-heavy-edits** (bound, [source](#stmt-lem-heavy-edits)): The expected sum of $r_j$ times overall level-key changes caused by heavy edits, evaluated at the hidden members’ old physical positions with the base structures held fixed, is at most $C\ell Q+D_H''$.
  - Explanation: Key changes caused by heavy-ball edits cost a logarithmic multiple of the comparator cost plus a finite constant.
- **claim-roster-count** (bound, [source](#stmt-lem-roster-count)): In a subset of $Y$ of diameter at most $20r$, there are at most $A$ chronological entries of age less than the positive integer $A$. In particular the bounds are $K$ and $K^2$ at the two age cutoffs, regardless of how many entries have already retired.
  - Explanation: A small region can contain only a limited number of chronological roster entries below any given age.
- **claim-fixed-alphabet** (construction, [source](#stmt-lem-fixed-alphabet)): The construction can use fixed finite label pools at each level, independent of the input law and horizon. Labels can be assigned so that distinct current records have distinct labels, including shadowed records, and a freshly chosen label was absent throughout the preceding step and all earlier intermediate states of the current step. The sole exception is the prescribed heavy-ball reuse. Every point with a given current label is within $20r$ of its anchor; its cell has diameter at most $40r$. A label common to consecutive maps has the same anchor or is a reused heavy label with anchor displacement at most $40r$.
  - Explanation: Finite recyclable label pools independent of the law and horizon suffice, with bounded cells and anchors that move only under the prescribed heavy-label reuse.
- **claim-first-hit** (bound, [source](#stmt-lem-first-hit)): Fix a true input, a roster at a fixed step, and points $x,y$ with $d=d(x,y)<r/4$. The probability that the earliest present ball covering either point covers exactly one is at most $Chd/r$. The expectation is over the partition tape.
  - Explanation: The probability that the earliest covering ball separates two nearby points is proportional to their distance over the scale, times a tier coefficient.
- **claim-tier-edits** (bound, [source](#stmt-lem-tier-edits)): The expected sum of $r_j$ times overall level-key changes due to roster edits, evaluated at the old physical positions in the stated edit order, is at most $C\ell Q+D_L'$.
  - Explanation: Key changes due to roster edits cost a logarithmic multiple of the comparator cost plus a finite constant, using a short auxiliary roster to pay for insertions.
- **claim-park-variation** (bound, [source](#stmt-lem-park-variation)): At every step, $$\label{eq:park-variation} \sum_v r_{\|v\|}\lvert\Delta p_v^{\circ}\rvert \le \left(1+\frac1\tau\right) \sum_{v\ne\mathrm{root}}r_{\|v\|-1}\lvert\Delta q_v\rvert.$$ If $q_{t,v}>0$, there is a point of $Y$ whose current key string has prefix $v$. Such a point is called a validity witness for $v$. If $\|v\|=j\ge1$, every validity witness is within $20r_j$ of the anchor of the last key of $v$.
  - Explanation: The weighted variation of parked mass is bounded by the weighted movement of subtree amounts, and every positively allocated vertex has a validity witness near its anchor.
- **claim-anchor-travel** (bound, [source](#stmt-prop-anchor-travel)): There is an absolute constant $C$ and a finite constant $D_{\rm anchor}$, independent of the input law and horizon, such that $$\label{eq:anchor-travel-bound} T_{\rm anchor}\le C\bigl(V+\ell P+\ell Q\bigr)+D_{\rm anchor}.$$ Consequently the expected cost of moving the new parked masses with their retained anchors is at most $40T_{\rm anchor}$. No multiplicative constant in this statement depends on the number of levels or on $k$.
  - Explanation: Travel of parked mass caused by retained anchors is bounded by the allocation movement, tree embedding cost and comparator cost, with no multiplicative dependence on the number of levels.
- **claim-key-size-refresh** (bound, [source](#stmt-lem-key-size-refresh)): If $R_{t,j,\lambda}$ denotes a refresh, then $$\label{eq:key-size-refresh-budget} \mathbb E\sum_{t,j,\lambda\in\Lambda_j^H} r_j\bigl(\widehat m_{t-1,j,\lambda} +\widehat m_{t,j,\lambda}\bigr)\mathbf 1_{R_{t,j,\lambda}} \le C\ell P+D_{\rm size},$$ where $C$ is absolute and $D_{\rm size}$ depends only on the fixed metric, scales, $k$, and label pools.
  - Explanation: Refreshes of held key sizes are paid through a Bregman-type tangent potential, giving a logarithmic multiple of the tree embedding cost plus a finite constant.
- **claim-park-coefficients** (bound, [source](#stmt-lem-park-coefficients)): Fix any completed fractional state and any $y\in Y$. At each level whose heavy-center set has a center within $7r_j$ of $y$, let $\lambda_j$ be its label. Such a center is unique. Then $$\label{eq:pointwise-park-coefficients} \sum_{\substack{j:\ d(y,a_{j,\lambda_j})\le7r_j}} \frac{b_{j,\lambda_j}}{\widehat m_{j,\lambda_j}} \le C\ell,$$ with an absolute $C$, independent of the state and the number of levels.
  - Explanation: At any state and point, the sum over levels of parked mass over held size near the point is of logarithmic order, via dyadic grouping and a scale telescope.
- **claim-rounding-star** (construction, [source](#stmt-lem-rounding-star)): Let $a,b\in[0,\infty)^d$, and let $X\in\mathbb Z_{\ge0}^d$ be a random vector with $$X_i\in\{\lfloor a_i\rfloor,\lceil a_i\rceil\}, \qquad \mathbb EX_i=a_i.$$ Put $M=\sum_iX_i$. Let $N$ be any random integer, arbitrarily correlated with $X$, such that $$N\in\left\{\left\lfloor\sum_i b_i\right\rfloor, \left\lceil\sum_i b_i\right\rceil\right\}, \qquad \mathbb EN=\sum_i b_i.$$ There is a randomized kernel from the finite set of states $(X,N)$ to vectors $X'$ such that, in every state and every outcome of the kernel, $$X'_i\in\{\lfloor b_i\rfloor,\lceil b_i\rceil\}, \qquad \sum_iX'_i=N.$$ Its coordinate means, averaged over both the original state and the kernel, satisfy $$\mathbb EX'_i=b_i.$$ With $$T=\sum_i\|a_i-b_i\|, \qquad I=\mathbb E\|N-M\|,$$ the kernel can be chosen to satisfy $$\label{eq:rounding-star-bound} \mathbb E\\|X'-X\\|_1\le32T+11I.$$ Moreover, for any balanced random total $N$ with the displayed mean, a balanced vector with total $N$ and coordinate means $b_i$ exists without specifying an old vector $X$.
  - Explanation: A finite randomized kernel rounds a star to integers meeting a prescribed random total in every outcome, with the right coordinate means and a bounded expected movement.
- **claim-rounding-realization** (bound, [source](#stmt-prop-rounding)): Assume the fractional allocations and anchors constructed in the preceding sections, with $\tau\ge22$. They admit a causal rounding satisfying <a href="#eq:rounding-invariants" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:rounding-invariants">[eq:rounding-invariants]</a> for which $$\label{eq:rounding-global} N_{\rm rnd}:=\mathbb E\sum_{t=1}^H\sum_{v\ne{\rm root}} r_{\|v\|-1}\|n_v(t)-n_v(t-1)\|\le132V.$$ Placing the integer parks at their current anchors gives simulated configurations $S_t$ of exactly $k$ members containing each current request. There is a matching of consecutive configurations with expected total cost at most $$\label{eq:rounding-realization} 120N_{\rm rnd}+40T_{\rm anchor}.$$ Consequently their expected movement is at most $C(V+\ell P+\ell Q)+D$ for an absolute $C$ and a finite additive term $D$ depending only on the fixed finite structure.
  - Explanation: A causal rounding with bounded tree variation exists, and placing integer parks at their anchors gives configurations serving each request whose matching movement is bounded linearly.
- **claim-episode-amplification** (bound, [source](#stmt-lem-amplification)): Let $Y$ be a finite metric space, let $k\ge2$, and let $s=(s_1,\ldots,s_k)\in Y^k$ have distinct entries. For every finite-support probability law $\lambda$ on finite request sequences in $Y$, there is a causal randomized policy $A_\lambda$ starting from $s$ such that $$\mathbb E_{\sigma\sim\lambda}\mathbb E\bigl[\mathop{\mathrm{cost}}_{A_\lambda,s}(\sigma)\bigr] \le \alpha\, \mathbb E_{\sigma\sim\lambda}\bigl[\mathop{\mathrm{OPT}}_s(\sigma)\bigr]. \label{eq:additive-free-law}$$ The policy can be strongly lazy. The constant $c_{\mathrm{tr}}$ in <a href="#eq:transfer-alpha" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:transfer-alpha">[eq:transfer-alpha]</a> is the same for all $Y$, $s$, and $\lambda$.
  - Explanation: Repeating forward, reverse and reset episodes amortizes the finite additive term, giving a purely multiplicative bound for any finite law when starting points are distinct.
- **claim-finite-minimax** (conditional_result, [source](#stmt-lem-finite-minimax)): For every finite collection $\sigma_1,\ldots,\sigma_m$ of finite request sequences in $X$, there is a behavioral policy $p$ from $s$ satisfying $$\mathbb E\mathop{\mathrm{cost}}_{p,s}(\sigma_i)\le\alpha\mathop{\mathrm{OPT}}_s(\sigma_i) \qquad(1\le i\le m). \label{eq:finite-family-bound}$$ Its behavioral choices can be defined on all decision histories in $X$.
  - Explanation: By separation in a finite convex set and a mixed-to-behavioral conversion, one behavioral policy satisfies the multiplicative bound on any finite collection of sequences.
- **claim-compact-policy** (theorem, [source](#stmt-lem-compact-policy)): For every metric space $X$ and every distinct tuple $s\in X^k$, there is a single randomized causal policy $A_s$ such that $$\mathbb E\mathop{\mathrm{cost}}_{A_s,s}(\sigma)\le\alpha\mathop{\mathrm{OPT}}_s(\sigma) \qquad\text{for every finite request sequence }\sigma\text{ in }X. \label{eq:all-inputs-distinct}$$ For each fixed input its decisions and cost are measurable functions of its internal randomness. Neither its rule nor its randomization depends on an eventual horizon.
  - Explanation: Product compactness over all decision histories and the finite intersection property give a single policy meeting the multiplicative bound on every finite request sequence, on any metric space.

```json
      "exact_statement": "There are absolute constants $p_*,s_*,\\epsilon_*>0$, with $p_*<s_*<1$, and functions $l^\\beta:[0,1]\\to[0,1]$ for $3\\le\\beta\\le3+\\epsilon_*$ such that:*\n\n1.  *$l^\\beta$ is convex and nonincreasing, equals $1-\\beta p$ on $[0,p_*]$, is positive on $[0,s_*)$, and is zero on $[s_*,1]$;*\n\n2.  *$l^\\beta$ is affine and nonincreasing in $\\beta$, and the square roots $\\sqrt{l^\\beta}$ have a common absolute Lipschitz constant;*\n\n\n3.  *if $g(p)=-\\partial_\\beta l^\\beta(p)$, then $$\\label{eq:rank-derivative}\n     \\frac1{96}l^\\beta(p)\\le g(p)\\le l^\\beta(p)\n           \\qquad(p\\in[p_*/4,1]).$$*\n\n*One may take $p_*=1/24$, $s_*=5/8$, and $\\epsilon_*=1/100$.",
      "explanation": "Explicit convex nonincreasing rank functions with an affine initial piece, a cutoff and a controlled parameter derivative provide compatible lower bounds for subtree amounts.",

        "anchor": "stmt-lem-rank-tracking",
        "exact_quote": "Let $p_t,p_t^-\\in[0,1]$ obey $\\mathbb E[p_t^-\\mid\\mathcal F_{t-1}]=p_{t-1}$, and let $\\mathcal E=\\mathbb E\\sum_t|p_t-p_t^-|$.         "exact_quote": "The data above satisfy, for an absolute constant $C$ and a finite $D_v^{\\mathrm{data}}$ depending only on the fixed star and $k$, $$\\label{eq:coarse-data-budget}\n             \\mathbb E\\sum_t C_{v,t}\\le C E_v+D_v^{\\mathrm{data}}.$$ The constant is uniform in the law and horizon.       "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-lem-pilot-compact",
        "exact_quote": "For every nonnegative measure $\\mu$ on the finite space $Y$, the minimum in <a href=\"#eq:pilot-potential\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"eq:pilot-potential\">[eq:pilot-potential]</a> exists. For fixed $g$, the function $\\mu\\mapsto\\mathcal L_r(\\mu,g)$ is concave, and $$0\\le\\mathcal L_r(\\mu,g)\\le2r\\mu(Y).\n \\label{eq:pilot-size}$$ Every feasible integrand satisfies $$0\\le F_z^g\\le514,\\qquad\n r\\mathop{\\mathrm{Lip}}(F_z^g)\\le\\Lambda:=L+1026/\\sigma.\n \\label{eq:pilot-global-bounds}$$ Moreover, the positive supports of the functions $b_s$ with $z_s>0$ are pairwise disjoint.",

        "anchor": "stmt-lem-pilot-local",
        "exact_quote": "Assume $h\\le\\delta$, and let $z$ be any minimizer in <a href=\"#eq:pilot-potential\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"eq:pilot-potential\">[eq:pilot-potential]</a>. Then the following assertions hold.*\n\n1.  *A center $s$ with $\\sigma r/4\\le d(s,x)\\le Rr/2$ has $z_s=0$.*\n\n\n2.  *There is a unique positive center $s$ with $d(s,x)<\\sigma r/4$. Its weight $z_s$ and normalized displacement $w=d(s,x)/r$ satisfy $$z_s\\ge\\tfrac34,\\qquad\n           1-z_s\\le\\frac{512\\gamma}{\\sigma^2}t+514h,\n           \\qquad w\\le(5+L)(h+t).\n           \\label{eq:pilot-local-structure}$$*\n\n\n3.  *If $d(x,y)<\\sigma r/8$, then $$_+\n              \\le C_{\\mathrm{loc}}(h+t)\\frac{d(x,y)}r,\n           \\label{eq:pilot-local-slope}$$ where one admissible constant is $$C_{\\mathrm{loc}}\n           =(L+1024/\\sigma)(514+8/\\sigma)\n               +\\frac{1024(5+L)}{\\sigma^2}.\n           \\label{eq:pilot-local-constant}$$",
        "source_id": "PM-110.1"
      },

      "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-lem-pilot-drift",
        "exact_quote": "Fix a template and constants as in <a href=\"#eq:pilot-concentration-constants\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"eq:pilot-concentration-constants\">[eq:pilot-concentration-constants]</a>. At each radius let $g_j$ be any function with $0\\le g_j\\le1$ and $r_j\\mathop{\\mathrm{Lip}}(g_j)\\le L$. For measures satisfying <a href=\"#eq:pilot-mover\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"eq:pilot-mover\">[eq:pilot-mover]</a>, $$\\sum_{j=1}^J\n [\\mathcal L_{r_j}(\\mu^+,g_j)\n        -\\mathcal L_{r_j}(\\mu^-,g_j)]_+\n \\le C_{\\mathrm{dr}}\\ell e_x.\n \\label{eq:pilot-drift}$$ The constant is independent of $Y$, $k$, $J$, and $\\tau\\ge2$. For example, set $$\\begin{aligned}\n p_0&=\\left\\lceil\\log_2(D_1/\\gamma)\\right\\rceil,\\nonumber\\\\\n C_{\\mathrm{dr}}\n   &=\\frac{2056}{\\gamma}+\\frac{8224}{\\sigma}\n       +\\frac{2\\Lambda p_0}{\\delta\\log2}\n       +2C_{\\mathrm{loc}}\n                   \\left(\\frac{p_0}{\\log2}+2\\gamma\\right).\n \\label{eq:pilot-drift-constant}\n\\end{aligned}$$",
        "source_id": "PM-110.1"
      },
      "evidence": [
        {
          "anchor": "stmt-lem-pilot-drift",
          "exact_quote": "Fix a template and constants as in <a href=\"#eq:pilot-concentration-constants\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"eq:pilot-concentration-constants\">[eq:pilot-concentration-constants]</a>. At each radius let $g_j$ be any function with $0\\le g_j\\le1$ and $r_j\\mathop{\\mathrm{Lip}}(g_j)\\le L$. For measures satisfying <a href=\"#eq:pilot-mover\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"eq:pilot-mover\">[eq:pilot-mover]</a>, $$\\sum_{j=1}^J\n [\\mathcal L_{r_j}(\\mu^+,g_j)\n        -\\mathcal L_{r_j}(\\mu^-,g_j)]_+\n \\le C_{\\mathrm{dr}}\\ell e_x.\n \\label{eq:pilot-drift}$$ The constant is independent of $Y$, $k$, $J$, and $\\tau\\ge2$. For example, set $$\\begin{aligned}\n p_0&=\\left\\lceil\\log_2(D_1/\\gamma)\\right\\rceil,\\nonumber\\\\\n C_{\\mathrm{dr}}\n   &=\\frac{2056}{\\gamma}+\\frac{8224}{\\sigma}\n       +\\frac{2\\Lambda p_0}{\\delta\\log2}\n       +2C_{\\mathrm{loc}}\n                   \\left(\\frac{p_0}{\\log2}+2\\gamma\\right).\n \\label{eq:pilot-drift-constant}\n\\end{aligned}$$",

      "exact_statement": "Fix a template and constants as in <a href=\"#eq:pilot-concentration-constants\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"eq:pilot-concentration-constants\">[eq:pilot-concentration-constants]</a>. For example, set $$\\begin{aligned}\n p_0&=\\left\\lceil\\log_2(D_1/\\gamma)\\right\\rceil,\\nonumber\\\\\n C_{\\mathrm{dr}}\n   &=\\frac{2056}{\\gamma}+\\frac{8224}{\\sigma}\n       +\\frac{2\\Lambda p_0}{\\delta\\log2}\n       +2C_{\\mathrm{loc}}\n                   \\left(\\frac{p_0}{\\log2}+2\\gamma\\right).\n \\label{eq:pilot-drift-constant}\n\\end{aligned}$$",

    {
      "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-cor-pilot-edits",
        "exact_quote": "Let templates be indexed by a finite or countable set $I$, with fixed weights $\\omega_\\alpha\\ge0$. Each template has fixed admissible parameters and the drift constant $C_{\\mathrm{dr},\\alpha}$ of Lemma <a href=\"#lem:pilot-drift\" data-reference-type=\"ref\" data-reference=\"lem:pilot-drift\">19</a>. Assume $$A:=\\sum_{\\alpha\\in I}\\omega_\\alpha<\\infty,\n \\qquad\n B:=\\sum_{\\alpha\\in I}\\omega_\\alpha C_{\\mathrm{dr},\\alpha}<\\infty.$$ At each step, filter and drift the counting measure while keeping the previous functions $g_{\\alpha,j}$ fixed. Then edit these functions using the new history, retaining their template bounds. Suppose the weighted total potential drop in the edit stage is at least a nonnegative charge $c_t$, taking $c_t=0$ on steps without edits.         "anchor": "stmt-prop-embedded-cost",
        "exact_quote": "There are finite alphabets $\\mathcal A_1,\\ldots,\\mathcal A_J$, depending only on $Y,k$ and the fixed constants, and online random maps $$\\xi_t:Y\\longrightarrow\\mathcal A_1\\times\\cdots\\times\\mathcal A_J$$ with the following properties. Each coordinate cell at level $j$ has diameter at most $40r_j$ and an anchor within $20r_j$ of all its points. Thus $\\xi_t$ is injective. Give the full alphabet tree the leaf distance $r_{j-1}$ when the first differing coordinate is $j$. For the selected hidden members $X_t^1,\\ldots,X_t^k$, $$\\label{eq:embedded-cost}\n P:=\\mathbb E\\sum_t\\sum_{a=1}^k\n d_{\\mathcal T}\\bigl(\\xi_{t-1}(X_{t-1}^a),\\xi_t(X_t^a)\\bigr)\n \\le C\\ell Q+D_{\\rm part}.$$ Here $C$ is absolute and $D_{\\rm part}<\\infty$ is independent of the input law and its horizon.       "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-lem-fixed-alphabet",
        "exact_quote": "The construction can use fixed finite label pools at each level, independent of the input law and horizon. Labels can be assigned so that distinct current records have distinct labels, including shadowed records, and a freshly chosen label was absent throughout the preceding step and all earlier intermediate states of the current step. The sole exception is the prescribed heavy-ball reuse. Every point with a given current label is within $20r$ of its anchor; its cell has diameter at most $40r$.         "anchor": "stmt-lem-park-coefficients",
        "exact_quote": "Fix any completed fractional state and any $y\\in Y$. At each level whose heavy-center set has a center within $7r_j$ of $y$, let $\\lambda_j$ be its label. Such a center is unique.         "source_id": "PM-110.1"
      },
```

<a id="pm-s2"></a>
## 2. Definitions, validity and bounds

- **configuration** ([source](#pm-h-11)): A configuration is a multiset of $k$ points, with each repeated point counted separately.
- **logarithmic shorthand** ([source](#pm-h-11)): We use the shorthand $$\ell=1+\log(k+1).$$
- **expected optimum** ([source](#pm-h-12)): Set $$Q=\mathbb E\,\mathop{\mathrm{OPT}}.$$
- **template** ([source](#pm-h-37)): A *template* consists of fixed numbers $0<\sigma\le1$, $R\ge256$, and $L\ge0$.
- **parked mass** ([source](#pm-h-50)): For every tree vertex, including the root and leaves, define its parked mass by $$\label{eq:park-definition} p_{t,v}^{\circ}=q_{t,v}-\sum_{i\text{ child of }v}q_{t,i}.$$

| id | quantity | relation | value | regime |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| q-main-ratio | competitive ratio factor | ≤ | $C\bigl(\log(k+1)\bigr)^2$ | absolute constant C independent of the metric |
| q-additive-distinct | additive term B | = | $0$ | any metric space |
| q-rank-p-star | affine threshold of rank functions | = | $1/24$ | admissible choice |
| q-rank-s-star | cutoff of rank functions | = | $5/8$ | admissible choice |
| q-rank-epsilon-star | parameter slack of rank functions | = | $1/100$ | admissible choice |
| q-size-cutoff | size cutoff constant | = | $10^{-4}$ | absolute constant |
| q-pilot-delta | concentration shell tolerance | ≤ | $1/4112$ | may be decreased for applications |
| q-size-tracker-ratio | refresh tolerance ratio | = | $6/5$ | fixed absolute value |
| q-rounding-star-loss | expected l1 movement of the star rounding kernel | ≤ | $32T+11I$ | T and I as defined in the lemma |
| q-rounding-tree-variation | expected weighted tree variation of the rounding | ≤ | $132V$ | separation factor at least a fixed threshold |
| q-rounding-realization | expected matching cost of simulated configurations | ≤ | $120N_{\rm rnd}+40T_{\rm anchor}$ | separation factor at least a fixed threshold |
| q-separation-threshold | separation factor in the rounding proposition | ≥ | $22$ | absolute threshold |

<a id="pm-s3"></a>
## 3. Proof roadmap

- **step-lazy-simulation** [claim](#stmt-lem-lazy-simulation) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-lazy-simulation): Minimum matchings composed along the simulated trajectory -> pathwise cost bound for a strongly lazy policy
- **step-ball-telescope** [claim](#stmt-lem-scale-logarithms) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-scale-logarithms): Shift index by a fixed offset so interior terms cancel -> single logarithm bound over scales
- **step-rank-functions** [claim](#stmt-lem-rank-functions) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-rank-functions): Explicit piecewise formulas with matched values and derivatives -> rank functions
- **step-superadditivity** [claim](#stmt-lem-superadditivity) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-superadditivity): Majorization via convex hinges -> parent bound dominates children
- **step-rank-tracking** [claim](#stmt-lem-rank-tracking) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-rank-tracking): Affine potential with reference resets -> held estimates with drift-only cost
- **step-parameter-gaps** [claim](#stmt-lem-parameter-gaps) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-parameter-gaps): Amortization of refresh charges and size comparison -> coarse budget and parameter gaps
- **step-caps** [claim](#stmt-lem-caps) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-caps): Interpolated endpoint trackers with small tolerance -> fine caps
- **step-denominators** [claim](#stmt-lem-denominators) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-denominators): Direct integration and elementary exponential inequalities -> uniform denominator estimates
- **step-simplex-update** [claim](#stmt-lem-alpha-tracker) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-alpha-tracker): Global minimization and exchange argument -> feasible simplex update
- **step-side-update** [claim](#stmt-lem-side-tracker) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-side-tracker): Global minimization over the bounded side domain -> feasible side vector
- **step-adaptive-proportions** [claim](#stmt-lem-adaptive-proportions) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-adaptive-proportions): Telescoping potentials with filtering before drift and refresh charges -> proportion movement bound
- **step-output-feasibility** [claim](#stmt-lem-output-feasibility) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-output-feasibility): Case analysis of the two removal rules -> outputs within bounds
- **step-star-movement** [claim](#stmt-lem-star-movement) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-star-movement): Hole vector comparison and switching potential -> star movement bound
- **step-dominated-allocation** [claim](#stmt-thm-allocation) / [proof](#pm-h-35): Sum star estimates over the tree and absorb the parent term using the separation factor -> allocation theorem
- **step-pilot-compact** [claim](#stmt-lem-pilot-compact) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-pilot-compact): Compactness of the feasible set and minimum of linear functionals -> existence, concavity and bounds
- **step-pilot-local** [claim](#stmt-lem-pilot-local) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-pilot-local): Competitor sites by deleting, inserting and relocating -> local structure and slope
- **step-pilot-drift** [claim](#stmt-lem-pilot-drift) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-pilot-drift): Split scales into small, bad and good, with telescoped ball masses -> drift bound
- **step-pilot-edits** [claim](#stmt-cor-pilot-edits) / [proof](#proof-stmt-cor-pilot-edits): Concavity under filtering and bounded drift, summed over steps -> edit budget
- **step-heavy-edits** [claim](#stmt-lem-heavy-edits) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-heavy-edits): Auxiliary ramp potential plus annulus mass paid by pilot decrease -> heavy edit cost
- **step-roster-count** [claim](#stmt-lem-roster-count) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-roster-count): Earliest entry counting argument -> local entry bound
- **step-fixed-alphabet** [claim](#stmt-lem-fixed-alphabet) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-fixed-alphabet): Reserved label pools with a cooldown rule -> finite alphabets and anchors
- **step-first-hit** [claim](#stmt-lem-first-hit) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-first-hit): Radius density integration and chronological independence -> separation probability
- **step-tier-edits** [claim](#stmt-lem-tier-edits) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-tier-edits): Noncoverage potential, retirement probability, short roster pilot gain -> tier edit cost
- **step-embedded-cost** [claim](#stmt-prop-embedded-cost) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-tier-edits-2): Map points to key strings and charge edits plus final disagreements -> embedded comparator cost
- **step-park-variation** [claim](#stmt-lem-park-variation) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-park-variation): Differences of the park definition and witness argument -> park variation bound
- **step-key-size-refresh** [claim](#stmt-lem-key-size-refresh) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-key-size-refresh): Tangent gap potential for the entropy-type function -> refresh budget
- **step-park-coefficients** [claim](#stmt-lem-park-coefficients) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-park-coefficients): Dyadic grouping and fixed-offset scale telescope -> pointwise coefficient bound
- **step-anchor-travel** [claim](#stmt-prop-anchor-travel) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-park-coefficients-2): Normalized affine ramp potential decomposed into filtering, drift, refresh and update substeps -> anchor travel bound
- **step-rounding-star** [claim](#stmt-lem-rounding-star) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-rounding-star): Clip, fix the total, then repair means by exchange minimization -> balanced rounding kernel
- **step-rounding-realization** [claim](#stmt-prop-rounding) / [proof](#proof-stmt-prop-rounding): Top-down rounding, tree matching, anchor distance estimates -> rounding and realization bounds
- **step-finite-law** [claim](#stmt-prop-finite-law) / [proof](#proof-stmt-prop-rounding-2): Combine partition, allocation, travel and rounding budgets, then lazy simulation -> finite distributional estimate
- **step-amplification** [claim](#stmt-lem-amplification) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-amplification): Episodes with reverse and reset words, random episode extraction -> additive-free policy per law
- **step-finite-minimax** [claim](#stmt-lem-finite-minimax) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-finite-minimax): Separation in a compact convex set and conversion to behavioral choices -> policy for a finite family
- **step-compact-policy** [claim](#stmt-lem-compact-policy) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-compact-policy): Product compactness and finite intersection property -> single policy for all finite inputs
- **step-main-theorem** [claim](#stmt-thm-main) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-compact-policy-2): Compare to a distinct start and follow by lazy simulation -> main theorem

<a id="pm-s4"></a>
## 4. Engineering Triptych

### Panel 1 - epistemic contract

- **inv-lazy-pathwise-bound**: For any causal sequence of simulated configurations that contains each request, a strongly lazy policy started from an arbitrary actual configuration serves the same requests, and its pathwise cost is at most the initial matching mismatch plus the sum of the successive matching distances between simulated configurations.
- **inv-strongly-lazy-moves**: A strongly lazy policy makes no move when a server already occupies the request, and otherwise moves exactly one server onto the request.

### Panel 2 - negative knowledge

- **anti-noncausal-simulation**: Feeding the lazy-simulation procedure a sequence of simulated configurations that is chosen using knowledge of future requests and presenting the result as an online policy. -> The pathwise inequality still holds, but the resulting procedure is not causal, so it is not an online algorithm.
- **anti-harness-as-main-theorem**: Reading the finite harness as a computable implementation of the randomized competitive policy of the main theorem. -> The harness only checks a deterministic pathwise cost bound relative to a given simulated trajectory; it does not produce randomized decisions, and it says nothing about the competitive ratio against the offline optimum.

### Panel 3 - reference implementation and oracle

Applicability: `algorithmic`. The paper's main theorem is existential and non-constructive, but the lazy-simulation lemma has an explicit finite proof procedure (minimum matching, then move one matched server onto the request) whose pathwise cost bound can be checked exhaustively on small inputs. Only this finite step is exercised.

```python
# INPUTS: k, H, actual start A0 (k ints), simulated configurations C0..CH (k ints each), requests r1..rH; points are integers on the line with distance |x-y|.
# PRECONDITIONS: each Ct (t>=1) contains the request rt; k small enough to enumerate all permutations.
# OUTPUTS: first line 'cost bound' (lazy policy cost, matching bound); then H lines 'j pos' (moved server index and destination) or '-1 0' for no move.
# TIME: O(H * k! * k) by brute-force enumeration of permutations.
# SPACE: O(H * k) for the stored configurations.
# ARITHMETIC: exact integer arithmetic.
# FAILURE: malformed input raises an exception and gives a nonzero exit status.
import sys
import itertools


def main():
    tok = sys.stdin.read().split()
    k = int(tok[0])
    H = int(tok[1])
    p = 2
    A = [int(x) for x in tok[p:p + k]]
    p += k
    C = []
    for t in range(H + 1):
        C.append([int(x) for x in tok[p:p + k]])
        p += k
    R = [int(x) for x in tok[p:p + H]]

    def mm(X, Y):
        return min(sum(abs(X[i] - Y[pi[i]]) for i in range(k)) for pi in itertools.permutations(range(k)))

    bound = mm(A, C[0])
    bound += sum(mm(C[t - 1], C[t]) for t in range(1, H + 1))
    cost = 0
    out = []
    for t in range(1, H + 1):
        r = R[t - 1]
        if r in A:
            out.append("-1 0")
            continue
        best = None
        bp = None
        for pi in itertools.permutations(range(k)):
            s = sum(abs(A[a] - C[t][pi[a]]) for a in range(k))
            if best is None or s < best:
                best = s
                bp = pi
        i = C[t].index(r)
        j = bp.index(i)
        cost += abs(A[j] - r)
        A[j] = r
        out.append("%d %d" % (j, r))
    print(cost, bound)
    for line in out:
        print(line)


main()
```

## 5. Domain engineering contracts

| domain | applies | strength | maturity | basis | why |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| software | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The result is an existence theorem for a policy, with no interface, procedure or implementable decision rule that a program could execute, so there is nothing to wrap as a library. |
| games_simulation | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | Nothing in the paper concerns agents, game dynamics or simulation; requests are a fixed sequence chosen independently of the policy, which is not an interactive game loop. |
| tools | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | No checker, analyzer or utility is described; the paper proves a ratio bound and does not provide a measurable artifact a tool could compute or verify. |
| algorithms | True | weak | adaptable | inferred | Only the lazy-simulation reduction is an explicit finite construction: it turns any causal sequence of simulated configurations covering the requests into a one-server-per-request policy with pathwise cost bounded by matching distances. The main policy itself is non-constructive. |
| data_structures | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The prefix tree of label strings is a proof device with fixed finite pools, not a proposed container with operations and costs that could be implemented and benchmarked. |
| hardware_accelerators | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | No arithmetic kernel, data path or parallel structure is specified, and the policy is not claimed to be computable at all, so an accelerator has nothing to implement. |
| crypto_security | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The guarantee holds only against an oblivious request sequence fixed independently of the policy's coins; it offers no secrecy, hardness or adaptive-attack statement. |
| machine_learning | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | There is no learning from data, training objective or generalization claim; the policy may depend on a known input law, which is a proof device rather than a learned model. |
| optimisation_operations_research | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The theorem is a competitive-analysis guarantee on expected cost relative to the offline optimum, not an optimisation model or solver; no instance-level procedure for dispatch or placement is given. |
| distributed_systems | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | Replica or worker placement could be modelled by k servers on a symmetric metric, but the paper itself provides no protocol, messaging or failure model, so this stays an unsupported analogy. |
| other | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The existence of one policy on an arbitrary, possibly infinite metric rests on product compactness and a choice principle, so it is a purely mathematical contribution with no further engineering reading. |

<a id="pm-s6"></a>
## 6. Applicability limits and search

- **limit-oblivious-adversary** (hypothesis): The competitive guarantee is proved only for requests fixed independently of the policy's random choices. It is not stated for opponents that adapt to the policy's moves. Blocked use: ["Citing the bound against an adversary that reacts to the policy's random choices or its server positions", 'Using the guarantee as a security or robustness claim against an adaptive attacker']
- **limit-metric-and-size** (hypothesis): The main theorem requires a genuine metric space with at least k+1 points and k at least 2. Costs that are asymmetric or break the triangle inequality, and networks with at most k points, are outside the stated regime. Blocked use: ['Applying the bound to directed networks with asymmetric costs', 'Applying the bound to a space with at most k points']
- **limit-additive-term-repeated-starts** (edge_case): The additive term B vanishes only when the initial server positions are distinct. With repeated starting positions a finite additive term that depends on the metric, k and the start may be needed. Blocked use: ['Assuming a purely multiplicative ratio when several servers start at the same point', 'Treating B as independent of the metric and the starting tuple in the repeated-start case']
- **limit-no-computational-claim** (nonconstructive): The paper makes no computational-efficiency claim for the policy. Its existence rests on product compactness over all decision histories, so the decision probabilities are not given by a procedure. Blocked use: ['Deploying the policy as a real-time dispatcher or cache manager that needs per-step computation', 'Expecting an explicit policy table or a complexity bound from the compactness argument']
- **limit-lazy-simulation-causality** (hypothesis): The lazy-simulation step yields an online policy only when the simulated configuration sequence is causal. Otherwise it is only a pathwise comparison of offline services. Blocked use: ['Feeding it a simulated trajectory built from future requests and calling the result an online algorithm']
- **limit-finite-law-dependence** (formal_scope): In the finite distributional estimate the policy may depend on the input law and horizon, and only the additive constant is uniform. This is a stage of the proof, not a law-independent policy. Blocked use: ['Using the finite-law policy without knowing the input law', 'Reading the intermediate estimate as the final single-policy statement']
- **limit-rounding-separation** (hypothesis): The rounding and realization bounds are stated under a separation factor of at least a fixed threshold, together with the fractional allocations and anchors of the preceding sections. Blocked use: ['Using the rounding bound with a smaller separation factor', 'Using it without the preceding allocation and anchor constructions']

<a id="pm-formal"></a>
### Formal statement (Lean 4)

Corpus status: formalized in the source corpus (toolchain `leanprover/lean4:v4.34.1`, Mathlib `d13f23b723b8a846827a245b89c10fc7d3f11612`). Not re-checked here; the statements give the exact quantifiers and hypotheses, not a proof.

Scope note of family 110 (`lean/docs/110.md`, verbatim):

> The following describes the scope of the Lean formalization related to the following accompanying paper(s):
>
> - [Squared-logarithmic randomized $k$-server on arbitrary metrics](../../preprints/Squared-logarithmic-randomized-k-server-on-arbitrary-metrics-September-24-2026/Squared-logarithmic-randomized-k-server-on-arbitrary-metrics-September-24-2026.pdf)
> - [Uniform computation of the squared-logarithmic $k$-server bound](../../preprints/Uniform-computation-of-the-squared-logarithmic-k-server-bound-September-24-2026/Uniform-computation-of-the-squared-logarithmic-k-server-bound-September-24-2026.pdf)
>
> ## Scope
>
> The formalization proves an $O((\log(k+1))^2)$ competitive ratio for randomized $k$-server against oblivious finite request sequences. For every $k\ge2$, every metric space containing at least $k+1$ points, and every initial configuration, one policy works for all request sequences, including in infinite and unbounded spaces.
>
> The bound permits a configuration-dependent additive constant, which is zero when the initial server positions are distinct. The universal multiplicative constant is independent of the metric and $k$.
>
> The formalization constructs one uniform randomized bit algorithm for $k$-server on finite rational metrics, for $2\le k<n$. Its expected movement on every oblivious finite request sequence is at most an absolute multiple of $(\log(k+1))^2$ times the offline optimum, plus a finite instance-dependent additive constant.
>
> Preprocessing is polynomial in the encoded input length, and each request is processed in time polynomial in that length and the binary length of the request counter. Every processed request returns a legal server index. The additive movement constant is not claimed to be polynomially bounded.

Comparator source `KServer.lean` (2224 bytes, sha256 `e6ae7349a69c21a8466e6c8e02fe5572dba7ea9eaece17133041d1c2495daa6f`), verbatim:

```lean
import Mathlib

namespace OAI

noncomputable section

open scoped BigOperators

namespace KServer

universe u

abbrev Configuration (k : ℕ) (X : Type u) := Fin k → X

def LabelDistribution (k : ℕ) :=
  {probability : Fin k → ℝ //
    (∀ label, 0 ≤ probability label) ∧ ∑ label, probability label = 1}

abbrev Policy (k : ℕ) (X : Type u) :=
  List (X × Fin k) → X → LabelDistribution k

def serve {k : ℕ} {X : Type u} (s : Configuration k X)
    (label : Fin k) (request : X) : Configuration k X :=
  Function.update s label request

def serviceCost {k : ℕ} {X : Type u} [MetricSpace X]
    (s : Configuration k X) : (requests : List X) → (Fin requests.length → Fin k) → ℝ
  | [], _ => 0
  | request :: requests, labels =>
      dist (s (labels 0)) request +
        serviceCost (serve s (labels 0) request) requests (fun index => labels index.succ)

def pathProbability {k : ℕ} {X : Type u} (A : Policy k X)
    (history : List (X × Fin k)) :
    (requests : List X) → (Fin requests.length → Fin k) → ℝ
  | [], _ => 1
  | request :: requests, labels =>
      (A history request).val (labels 0) *
        pathProbability A (history ++ [(request, labels 0)]) requests
          (fun index => labels index.succ)

def expectedCost {k : ℕ} {X : Type u} [MetricSpace X]
    (A : Policy k X) (s : Configuration k X) (requests : List X) : ℝ :=
  ∑ labels : Fin requests.length → Fin k,
    pathProbability A [] requests labels * serviceCost s requests labels

def optimalCost {k : ℕ} {X : Type u} [MetricSpace X]
    (s : Configuration k X) (requests : List X) : ℝ :=
  sInf (Set.range (serviceCost s requests))

def MainStatement : Prop :=
  ∃ C : ℝ, 0 < C ∧ ∀ (k : ℕ), 2 ≤ k →
    ∀ (X : Type u) (_ : MetricSpace X),
      (∃ embedding : Fin (k + 1) → X, Function.Injective embedding) →
      ∀ s : Configuration k X,
        ∃ A : Policy k X, ∃ B : ℝ,
          0 ≤ B ∧ (Function.Injective s → B = 0) ∧
          ∀ requests : List X,
            expectedCost A s requests ≤
              C * (Real.log (k + 1)) ^ 2 * optimalCost s requests + B

theorem main_theorem : MainStatement.{u} := by
  sorry

end KServer

end

end OAI
```

<a id="pm-s7"></a>
## 7. Complete mathematical paper


We prove that randomized <i>k</i>-server has competitive ratio $`O((\log(k+1))^2)`$ on every metric space against oblivious request sequences, matching the known worst-case lower bound. For each metric and initial configuration, one policy works for all finite request sequences, including on infinite and unbounded spaces. When the initial server positions are distinct, no additive term is needed.

<a id="sec:introduction"></a>
### Introduction

The $k$-server problem asks how to move $k$ servers in a metric space when requests arrive one at a time. A request is a point that must be occupied by a server; the cost is the distance moved. The offline optimum knows all future requests, whereas an online policy must decide from the revealed prefix. We determine the sharp worst-case order of the randomized competitive ratio.

More precisely, let $(X,d)$ be a metric space and let $s=(s_1,\ldots,s_k)\in X^k$ be the initial positions of $k$ labeled servers. Repeated positions are allowed. To serve $r_t$, a policy chooses one server and moves it to $r_t$, leaving all others fixed. A server already at $r_t$ can be chosen at zero cost. For a finite request sequence $\sigma$, let $\mathop{\mathrm{cost}}_{A,s}(\sigma)$ be a policy’s total movement and let $\mathop{\mathrm{OPT}}_{X,d,k,s}(\sigma)$ be the minimum such cost with full knowledge of $\sigma$. Requests are fixed independently of the policy’s random choices, the usual oblivious-adversary model.

<a id="stmt-thm-main"></a>
<div id="thm:main" class="theorem">

**Theorem 1**. *There is an absolute constant $C<\infty$ such that, for every integer $k\ge2$, every metric space $(X,d)$ with at least $k+1$ points, and every $s\in X^k$, there exist a randomized online policy $A$ and a finite $B\ge0$ satisfying <a id="eq:main"></a>$$\label{eq:main}
 \mathbb E\,\mathop{\mathrm{cost}}_{A,s}(\sigma)
 \le C\bigl(\log(k+1)\bigr)^2\mathop{\mathrm{OPT}}_{X,d,k,s}(\sigma)+B$$ for every finite request sequence $\sigma$. The constant $C$ is independent of every property of the metric. The number $B$ may depend on $(X,d),k,s$, but not on the sequence or its length. The same policy serves all finite sequences and horizons. If the initial positions are distinct, one may take $B=0$.*

</div>

There is no finiteness, boundedness, or discreteness assumption on $X$, and no computational-efficiency claim for the policy. Throughout, logarithms are natural.

<a id="pm-h-3"></a>
#### Context and previous work

Manasse, McGeoch, and Sleator introduced the $k$-server problem as a common framework for online service problems (Manasse, McGeoch, and Sleator 1988, 1990) <a id="pm-cite-1"></a>[[MMS88](#ref-MMS88), [MMS90](#ref-MMS90)]. They proved the deterministic lower bound $k$ on every metric with more than $k$ points and conjectured that it is attainable. Koutsoupias and Papadimitriou established the $(2k-1)$-competitive work function algorithm (Koutsoupias and Papadimitriou 1995) <a id="pm-cite-2"></a>[[KP95](#ref-KP95)]. A recent preprint of Coester, Koutsoupias, and Zbysiński announces a $k$-competitive analysis of that algorithm (Coester, Koutsoupias, and Zbysiński 2026) <a id="pm-cite-3"></a>[[CKZ26](#ref-CKZ26)]. The present paper concerns the different asymptotic behavior made possible by randomization against oblivious requests.

Paging, the case of a uniform metric, gave the basic reason to expect such an improvement. Fiat, Karp, Luby, McGeoch, Sleator, and Young proved a harmonic lower bound and a $2H_k$-competitive randomized marking algorithm (Fiat et al. 1991) <a id="pm-cite-4"></a>[[FKLMSY91](#ref-FKLMSY91)]; McGeoch and Sleator attained the exact bound $H_k=\sum_{i=1}^k i^{-1}$ (McGeoch and Sleator 1991) <a id="pm-cite-5"></a>[[MS91](#ref-MS91)]. For weighted paging, Bansal, Buchbinder, and Naor obtained the optimal order $O(\log k)$ through a fractional primal-dual algorithm and online rounding (Bansal, Buchbinder, and Naor 2012) <a id="pm-cite-6"></a>[[BBN12](#ref-BBN12)]. These results formed part of the evidence for the folklore randomized $k$-server conjecture, which predicted $O(\log k)$ on every metric.

That conjecture was disproved by Bubeck, Coester, and Rabani (Bubeck, Coester, and Rabani 2023, Theorem 11 and Proposition 8) <a id="pm-cite-7"></a>[[BCR23](#ref-BCR23)]: there exist $(k+1)$-point metrics requiring competitive ratio $\Omega(\log^2 k)$, even allowing a fixed additive constant. Their result must be distinguished from a lower bound on every metric. The latter developed through the decomposition and metric Ramsey methods of Karloff, Rabani, and Ravid (Karloff, Rabani, and Ravid 1994) <a id="pm-cite-8"></a>[[KRR94](#ref-KRR94)], Blum, Karloff, Rabani, and Saks (Blum et al. 2000) <a id="pm-cite-9"></a>[[BKRS00](#ref-BKRS00)], Bartal, Bollobás, and Mendel (Bartal, Bollobás, and Mendel 2006) <a id="pm-cite-10"></a>[[BBM06](#ref-BBM06)], and Bartal, Linial, Mendel, and Naor (Bartal et al. 2005) <a id="pm-cite-11"></a>[[BLMN05](#ref-BLMN05)]; Bubeck, Coester, and Rabani ultimately obtained the sharp universal order $\Omega(\log k)$ (Bubeck, Coester, and Rabani 2023, Corollary 20) <a id="pm-cite-12"></a>[[BCR23](#ref-BCR23)]. Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a> matches the larger, worst-case lower bound. It establishes the sharp squared-logarithmic order after the negative resolution of the original logarithmic conjecture.

The main upper-bound approach passes through hierarchically separated trees. Bartal introduced probabilistic approximation by these metrics (Bartal 1996, 1998) <a id="pm-cite-13"></a>[[Bartal96](#ref-Bartal96), [Bartal98](#ref-Bartal98)], and Fakcharoenphol, Rao, and Talwar obtained expected distortion $O(\log n)$ for $n$-point metrics (Fakcharoenphol, Rao, and Talwar 2004) <a id="pm-cite-14"></a>[[FRT04](#ref-FRT04)]. Recursive allocation methods were developed by Coté, Meyerson, and Poplawski (Coté, Meyerson, and Poplawski 2008) <a id="pm-cite-15"></a>[[CMP08](#ref-CMP08)] and by Bansal, Buchbinder, and Naor (Bansal, Buchbinder, and Naor 2010) <a id="pm-cite-16"></a>[[BBN10](#ref-BBN10)]. Bansal, Buchbinder, Mądry, and Naor then gave the first polylogarithmic bound for arbitrary finite metrics, $O(\log^2 k\,\log^3 n\,\log\log n)$ (Bansal et al. 2015). Bubeck, Cohen, Lee, Lee, and Mądry achieved $O(\log^2 k)$ on HSTs using multiscale entropic regularization (Bubeck et al. 2018) <a id="pm-cite-17"></a>[[BCLLM18](#ref-BCLLM18)]. The static embedding reduction yields $O(\log^2 k\log n)$ on general finite metrics. Its dependence on $n$ does not give a bound in $k$ alone. Coester and Cosson recently made the HST and finite-metric bounds polynomial-time, using a total number of random bits independent of the request length (Coester and Cosson 2026, Theorem 1 and Corollary 2) <a id="pm-cite-18"></a>[[CoesterCosson2026](#ref-CoesterCosson2026)]. Their general finite-metric ratio retains the factor $\log n$.

Changing the embedding as requests arrive is also an established direction. The dynamic construction of (Bubeck et al. 2018, sec. 4) <a id="pm-cite-19"></a>[[BCLLM18](#ref-BCLLM18)] gives $O(\log^3 k\log(1+\Delta))$ on finite metrics, where $\Delta$ is the ratio of the largest distance to the smallest positive distance. Lee’s evolving, fusible HST framework used heavy-ball nets to merge clusters near concentrated mass and introduced a fission potential intended to pay for later splits (Lee 2018, secs. 3.1.2–3.2 and 5.3) <a id="pm-cite-20"></a>[[Lee18](#ref-Lee18)]. Its general competitive-ratio claim was withdrawn after a gap in Section 5.3.2 (Lee, n.d.). Heavy-ball protection and accounting for changing partitions are methodological antecedents of our construction. Here the partition schedule is driven by the posterior measure of a hidden comparator; it produces a fixed tree of label strings. We prove the required edit and retained-anchor travel estimates in Sections <a href="#sec:partitions" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:partitions">7</a> and <a href="#sec:travel" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:travel">8</a>, without invoking a theorem from the withdrawn argument.

Subtree-rank coordinates, internal supermeasures, and fractional-to-integral transfer already play central roles in (Bansal et al. 2015; Bubeck et al. 2018) <a id="pm-cite-21"></a>[[BBMN15](#ref-BBMN15), [BCLLM18](#ref-BCLLM18)]. The use of local ball-mass ratios and their telescoping across scales has a geometric predecessor in measured descent (Krauthgamer et al. 2005, Lemma 1.7 and Section 1.3) <a id="pm-cite-22"></a>[[KLMN05](#ref-KLMN05)]. The distributional estimates are converted into a single policy by finite minimax and product compactness.

<a id="pm-h-4"></a>
#### Main ingredients

We first establish a finite distributional bound. Given a known law of requests, select one offline optimum for each input and use only its posterior distributions conditional on the revealed prefix. This does not reveal the realized future.

The construction starts with partitions of the finite metric at several distance scales. A point’s complete tuple of cell labels identifies a leaf of a fixed tree. The hidden optimum then gives posterior distributions of the server counts below each tree vertex. We use those distributions to allocate fractional server mass on the tree, represent that mass by points of the original metric, and round it to exactly $k$ servers. Changes of the partitions and of the representatives must be charged along with movement of mass through the tree. One logarithm comes from the partition estimate and one from the allocation estimate; rounding and realization preserve their product.

For exposition we prove the reusable allocation theorem first, then construct its partition input. The four components are as follows.

<a id="pm-h-5"></a>
###### Allocation from subtree count distributions.

On a fixed separated tree, we maintain fractional subtree amounts with movement $O(\log(k+1))$ times that of the hidden comparator. The amounts dominate the serving requirement and are bounded above by a constant multiple of the posterior expected counts. They may park at internal vertices, keeping unused mass above children while their posterior proportions change. The proof uses convex rank functions and slowly varying proportions. A separate rule for a nearly dominant child chooses the allocations to the other children first and gives that child the remaining parent amount, subject to its cap. This fits all children within their rank bounds without an additional logarithm. The resulting allocation theorem applies to general observed hidden trajectories, independently of the later metric embedding.

<a id="pm-h-6"></a>
###### A compact variational potential.

The partition analysis uses a finite-dimensional minimum over separated auxiliary sites with positive-part capacity constraints. Concentration forces a nearly full site near the request. A feasible relocation of that site makes the optimal integrand have small positive slope there. Ball-mass ratios and inner moments then telescope over scales, giving a single logarithm in the measure-motion budget. All geometric radii in this argument are fixed numerical multiples of the scale.

<a id="pm-h-7"></a>
###### Partitions adapted to local mass ratios.

Heavy balls protect highly concentrated requests. Other requests use chronological rosters in doubling qualification tiers. Their first-hit loss depends on a local mass ratio rather than on one worst-case logarithm at every level. Random retirements keep the rosters bounded; a separate short-lived auxiliary roster pays the retirement errors. The resulting finite label sets are fixed independently of the request horizon.

<a id="pm-h-8"></a>
###### Moving representatives and rounding.

Fractional mass parked at a tree vertex is represented by an anchor in the original metric. Retained anchors can move even when no mass crosses a tree edge. A normalized affine potential and a pointwise sum of parked-mass coefficients pay this travel without multiplying the tree movement by another logarithm. A dynamic balanced-rounding lemma, allowing correlated parent and child states, incurs only an absolute loss.

These components give two logarithms in total. The final step removes the finite-metric additive term by independent episodes and reset blocks, then uses finite-dimensional separation and compactness. This yields a single policy on all finite inputs of an arbitrary metric, including unbounded spaces. A matching simulation handles repeated initial positions.

<a id="pm-h-9"></a>
#### Organization

Section <a href="#sec:setup" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:setup">2</a> fixes the probabilistic conventions and the movement accounting. Sections <a href="#sec:allocation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:allocation">3</a>, <a href="#sec:proportions" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:proportions">4</a>, and <a href="#sec:allocation-finish" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:allocation-finish">5</a> prove the tree allocation theorem. Section <a href="#sec:pilot" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:pilot">6</a> develops the variational potential, and Section <a href="#sec:partitions" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:partitions">7</a> constructs the changing partitions. Sections <a href="#sec:travel" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:travel">8</a> and <a href="#sec:rounding" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:rounding">9</a> control realization and rounding. Section <a href="#sec:transfer" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:transfer">10</a> proves the uniform-policy conclusion.

The geometric and algorithmic estimates are proved below. The final passage to arbitrary spaces uses the standard compactness theorem identified in Section <a href="#sec:transfer" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:transfer">10</a>; the worst-case lower bound is the result of Bubeck, Coester, and Rabani cited above.

<a id="sec:setup"></a>
### Finite laws, posterior motion, and the proof architecture

We first allow the policy to know a finite input law. This permits conditional distributions of an offline optimum, while still forbidding the policy from observing the realized future. The estimate obtained in this setting must have an additive term uniform in the law and horizon. Section <a href="#sec:transfer" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:transfer">10</a> uses precisely this uniformity to construct one policy on an arbitrary metric.

<a id="pm-h-11"></a>
#### Matching and lazy simulation

A configuration is a multiset of $k$ points, with each repeated point counted separately. For two configurations $A=(a_1,\ldots,a_k)$ and $C=(c_1,\ldots,c_k)$, put $$\mathsf M(A,C)=\min_{\pi\in S_k}\sum_{i=1}^k d(a_i,c_{\pi(i)}).$$ This distance ignores the labels when matching configurations; an actual server retains its label when it moves. A *simulated* configuration may move several members between requests. Its movement cost is the matching distance between its successive configurations. A policy is *strongly lazy* if it does nothing when a server already occupies the request, and otherwise moves exactly one server to it.

The classical cost-nonincreasing lazy reduction for the same starting configuration is due to Manasse, McGeoch, and Sleator (Manasse, McGeoch, and Sleator 1990, Lemma 1, p. 211) <a id="pm-cite-23"></a>[[MMS90](#ref-MMS90)]. The matching-potential formulation below also permits an arbitrary initial mismatch.

<a id="stmt-lem-lazy-simulation"></a>
<div id="lem:lazy-simulation" class="lemma">

**Lemma 2** (Lazy simulation). *Suppose a causal sequence of simulated configurations $C_0,C_1,\ldots,C_H$ contains the request $r_t$ at each time $t\ge1$. From any actual start $A_0$, a strongly lazy policy serves the same requests and satisfies, pathwise, $$\mathop{\mathrm{cost}}(A_0;r_1,\ldots,r_H)
 \le \mathsf M(A_0,C_0)+\sum_{t=1}^H\mathsf M(C_{t-1},C_t).$$ The conclusion also holds if the matching distances on the right are replaced by any larger specified movement costs.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-lazy-simulation"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-lazy-simulation</summary>

*Proof.* After $C_t$ is revealed, take a minimum matching from $A_{t-1}$ to $C_t$. If $A_{t-1}$ does not contain $r_t$, move the actual server matched to a chosen occurrence of $r_t$ in $C_t$ to that point. Keeping the same matching after the move shows that $$\text{actual cost at }t+\mathsf M(A_t,C_t)
 \le \mathsf M(A_{t-1},C_t).$$ If the request was already covered, make no move and the inequality holds with equality. Composing minimum matchings gives $$\mathsf M(A_{t-1},C_t)
 \le \mathsf M(A_{t-1},C_{t-1})
       +\mathsf M(C_{t-1},C_t).$$ Summing and discarding the nonnegative final matching distance proves the claim. Minimum matchings exist because there are only $k!$ permutations, even with repeated positions. Fixed orders resolve all ties, so this procedure is causal. ◻

</details>

The same pathwise inequality applies to any prescribed simulated trajectory. Causality is needed to obtain an online policy from it; when comparing two offline optima, only the pathwise inequality is used.

We use the shorthand $$\ell=1+\log(k+1).$$ All unnamed multiplicative constants below are absolute. Constants may depend on numerical parameters only after those parameters have been fixed once and for all, independently of $k$ and the metric.

<a id="stmt-prop-finite-law"></a>
<div id="prop:finite-law" class="proposition">

**Proposition 3** (Finite distributional estimate). *There is an absolute $a<\infty$ with the following property. For every finite metric $Y$ of positive diameter, every $k\ge2$, and every start $s\in Y^k$, there is a finite $D_0=D_0(Y,k,s)$ such that, for every finite horizon $H$ and every probability law on $Y^H$, there is a causal strongly lazy policy satisfying <a id="eq:finite-law"></a>$$\label{eq:finite-law}
             \mathbb E\,\mathop{\mathrm{cost}}\le a\ell^2\mathbb E\,\mathop{\mathrm{OPT}}+D_0 .$$ The policy may depend on the law and horizon. The number $D_0$ does not.*

</div>

Sections <a href="#sec:allocation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:allocation">3</a>–<a href="#sec:rounding" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:rounding">9</a> prove this proposition. An additive term in those sections is always bounded by a fixed finite function of $Y,k,s$ and the finite structures constructed from them. It never depends on the law, horizon, number of histories, or number of updates. We will verify this distinction again when assembling the estimates.
