<a id="pm-h-12"></a>
#### Hidden comparators and the two parts of a posterior update

Fix the data of Proposition <a href="#prop:finite-law" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:finite-law">3</a>. For each input, choose one optimum trajectory of the $k$ labeled servers, resolving ties by a fixed order. Such a choice exists: each request has only $k$ possible server labels, so a length-$H$ input has only $k^H$ candidate trajectories. The chosen optimum is a hidden random variable determined by the input. Set $$Q=\mathbb E\,\mathop{\mathrm{OPT}}.$$ The policy uses conditional laws of this trajectory, not its hidden realization.

Let $\mathcal F_t$ contain the request prefix through time $t$ and the independent random tape used to construct the partitions. It is convenient to include that entire tape from time zero. The rounding procedure will use a separate independent tape that is excluded from $\mathcal F_t$. Thus conditioning on $\mathcal F_t$ does not alter the true-metric comparator posterior beyond conditioning on the request prefix.

For any old hidden quantity $Z_{t-1}$, a step has two logically different parts: $$\mathcal L(Z_{t-1}\mid\mathcal F_{t-1})
 \xrightarrow{\mathrm{filtering}}
 \mathcal L(Z_{t-1}\mid\mathcal F_t)
 \xrightarrow{\mathrm{drift}}
 \mathcal L(Z_t\mid\mathcal F_t).$$ Here $\mathcal L$ denotes a probability law, not the pilot potential introduced later. During filtering, an affine functional with coefficients held from time $t-1$ has zero conditional expected change. A concave functional has nonpositive conditional expected change. During drift, the joint conditional law of $(Z_{t-1},Z_t)$ provides a coupling. For embedded quantities, the old variable uses the old map and the new variable uses the new map.

All signed filtering estimates are applied over every step, with old parameters held fixed, *before* selecting resets or partition edits. In particular, no argument conditions a martingale estimate on the later event that a reset is necessary.

For probability estimates concerning random radii or retirements, we use a different conditioning: fix the true input and its comparator trajectory, and average over the partition tape. The relevant insertion schedules will then be deterministic. This does not assert independence after conditioning on the entire tape in $\mathcal F_t$.

At a fixed finite horizon all relevant random outcomes can be taken finite. A radius draw need only specify which subset of the finite space is its ball; a roster lifetime has a finite maximum age. Only finitely many insertions can occur. More explicitly, preallocate at most $H$ insertion slots per level and structure, discretize each radius at all pairwise distances in $Y$, and preallocate each entry’s truncated lifetime. This gives a finite-valued relevant tape, even before the centers of the inserted balls are known. Any unused portion of an ambient infinite tape is independent and can be ignored; all fractional states and selected rounding kernels below depend only on this finite projection and the request prefix. Realizing a kernel also uses the current rounded state and the independent rounding randomness. Conditional laws, minimizing choices, and randomized rounding kernels can therefore be specified history by history. Arbitrary continuations are assigned to zero-probability histories.

<a id="pm-h-13"></a>
#### The true-metric measure update

Write $x=r_t$, and designate the server moved by the chosen optimum at this request, including when its movement is zero. Let $\mu^{\mathrm{old}}$ be the expected comparator counting measure at time $t-1$, conditional on $\mathcal F_{t-1}$. At the new conditioning $\mathcal F_t$, let $\mu^-$ and $\mu^+$ be its counting measures before and after the move, and let $\nu$ be the probability law of the designated mover’s old position. Then <a id="eq:measure-update"></a>$$\label{eq:measure-update}
 \mu^+=\mu^--\nu+\delta_x,\qquad
 \nu\le\mu^-,\qquad \nu(Y)=1,\qquad
 e_x=\int_Y d(x,y)\,d\nu(y).$$ Both counting measures have total mass $k$, the measure $\mu^+$ has at least one unit at $x$, and $$\mathbb E[\mu^-\mid\mathcal F_{t-1}]=\mu^{\mathrm{old}},
 \qquad
 \mathbb E\sum_{t=1}^H e_{r_t}=Q.$$ The expected counting measure of the stationary members is $\mu^+-\delta_x=\mu^--\nu$, hence is bounded by $\mu^+$. The separate treatment of this stationary measure and the designated mover will matter in the partition accounting.

<a id="pm-h-14"></a>
#### Scales and a logarithmic telescope

Balls are closed. Set $r_0=\mathop{\mathrm{diam}}Y$ and $$r_j=r_0\tau^{-j},\quad 1\le j\le J,\qquad
       40r_J<\min_{p\ne q}d(p,q).$$ The separation factor $\tau\ge2$ is a sufficiently large absolute constant. Its required lower bounds come from tree allocation and rounding; neither bound depends on $k,Y,J$. Once it is fixed, $J$ depends only on $Y$.

<a id="stmt-lem-scale-logarithms"></a>
<div id="lem:scale-logarithms" class="lemma">

**Lemma 4** (Ball-mass telescope). *Fix $0<a<b$, a nonnegative measure $\mu$, and an offset $c_0\ge0$. On a consecutive finite interval of scale indices suppose $$c\le A_j:=c_0+\mu(B(x,ar_j))
 \le B_j:=c_0+\mu(B(x,br_j))\le K,
 \qquad c>0.$$ If $p\ge1$ is an integer with $\tau^p a\ge b$, then $$\sum_j\log(B_j/A_j)\le p\log(K/c).$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-scale-logarithms"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-scale-logarithms</summary>

*Proof.* Extend $A_j$ before the coarsest index of the interval by the value $K$. Ball inclusion gives $B_j\le A_{j-p}$ whenever the shifted index remains in the interval, and the same inequality follows from $B_j\le K$ at the coarse boundary. Thus the sum is at most $\sum_j(\log A_{j-p}-\log A_j)$. All interior terms cancel, leaving at most $p$ coarse-end terms minus at most $p$ fine-end terms. Each resulting difference is at most $\log(K/c)$. ◻

</details>

In every application, $a,b,c$ are fixed positive constants and $K=O(k+1)$, so the bound is $O(\ell)$. The offset permits its use with $1+\mu(B)$ as well as with positive ball masses themselves. Geometric sums of radii, by contrast, cost an absolute multiple of their largest radius and introduce no logarithm.

<a id="pm-h-15"></a>
#### Where the two logarithms occur

The allocation theorem begins with a fixed finite tree and a hidden trajectory of $k$ members on its leaves. It constructs subtree amounts $q_v$ satisfying $$q_{\mathrm{root}}=k,\qquad
 \sum_{i\text{ child of }v}q_i\le q_v,\qquad
                    q_v\le C_0m_v,$$ where $m_v$ is the posterior expected hidden subtree count. The inequality in conservation allows *parking* at internal vertices: $$p_v^\circ=q_v-\sum_iq_i .$$ All parks together have total mass $k$. This freedom is useful: the required leaf still receives at least one unit, but unused mass need not follow rapidly changing posterior proportions down the tree. Figure <a href="#fig:parked-tree" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="fig:parked-tree">1</a> illustrates this split and the separate movement of its representative.

The construction order is important. Partition schedules use only the true-metric posteriors and chronological ages. They are independent of the later allocation and rounding. Their bounded simultaneous occupancy gives a finite set of labels, chosen independently of the horizon. Full strings of level labels then define a fixed finite prefix tree. We apply the allocation theorem to that tree, control the moving representatives using the domination $q_v\le C_0m_v$, and round with a further independent tape. Finally Lemma <a href="#lem:lazy-simulation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:lazy-simulation">2</a> returns to the original one-server movement model.

<figure id="fig:parked-tree">
<embed src="figures/parked_tree.pdf" />
<figcaption>A subtree amount is split between its children and a park at its own vertex. The park is included as one more child target during rounding. A fixed tree vertex can have a moving representative in the original metric, so its parked mass requires a separate travel estimate. The picture records conservation and does not assert that the original metric is a Euclidean tree.</figcaption>
</figure>

<a id="pm-figsrc-fig-parked-tree"></a>
**Figure source and visual witness** (`fig:parked-tree`)

The source figure is an external graphic; no image payload is embedded.


Let $P$ be expected movement of the mapped hidden comparator on tree leaves, and put $$V=\mathbb E\sum_t\sum_{v\ne\mathrm{root}}
                     r_{|v|-1}|\Delta q_v|.$$ The main estimates are $$\begin{aligned}
 P&\le C\ell Q+B_P,\\
 V&\le C\ell P+B_V,\\
 \mathbb E\,\mathop{\mathrm{cost}}_{\mathrm{sim}}
   &\le C(V+\ell P+\ell Q)+B_T.
\end{aligned}$$ Their additive terms have the uniform meaning specified above. Figure <a href="#fig:budget-flow" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="fig:budget-flow">2</a> displays their dependence.

<figure id="fig:budget-flow">
<embed src="figures/budget_flow.pdf" />
<figcaption>Movement accounting. The partitions and the tree allocation each contribute one logarithm. Rounding has an absolute loss. Moving the representatives costs $O(V+ℓP+ℓQ)$, so it preserves the final squared logarithm. All additive terms shown are uniform in the finite input law and horizon.</figcaption>
</figure>

<a id="pm-figsrc-fig-budget-flow"></a>
**Figure source and visual witness** (`fig:budget-flow`)

The source figure is an external graphic; no image payload is embedded.


<a id="sec:allocation"></a>
### Allocation on a separated tree: rank data

We first solve an allocation problem on a fixed finite tree. The allocation uses conditional distributions of hidden subtree counts. Convex functions of their rank probabilities give compatible lower bounds on the amounts allocated to subtrees. A small, size-dependent change in the functions provides the slack needed to move those amounts slowly.

Let $\mathcal T$ be a finite rooted tree whose leaves have depth $J$. Write $|v|$ for depth and $i\prec v$ when $i$ is a child of $v$. Fix $r_j=r_0\tau^{-j}$, where $r_0>0$ and $\tau\ge2$. Write $d_T$ for the leaf metric: distinct leaves with last common ancestor of depth $j$ have distance $r_j$. At time $t$ there are $k$ labeled hidden members at leaves, and a leaf $x_t$ known to $\mathcal F_t$ is required. Assume that, conditional on $\mathcal F_t$, at least one hidden member is at $x_t$ with probability one. Let $N_v(t)$ be the number in the subtree of $v$, and put $m_v(t)=\mathbb E[N_v(t)\mid\mathcal F_t]$. The expected sum of the labeled members’ successive traveled distances is denoted by $P$; it includes their movement from time zero. All conditional distributions below are available from the observed history.

<a id="stmt-thm-allocation"></a>
<div id="thm:allocation" class="theorem">

**Theorem 5** (Dominated tree allocation). *There are absolute constants $C_0,C_1$ and $\tau_0$ with the following property. For $\tau\ge\tau_0$, the observed histories determine amounts $q_v(t)$ satisfying <a id="eq:allocation-constraints"></a>$$\label{eq:allocation-constraints}
 q_{\mathrm{root}}=k,\qquad
 0\le q_v\le C_0m_v,\qquad
 \sum_{i\prec v}q_i\le q_v,\qquad q_{x_t}\ge1.$$ Moreover, with $\ell=1+\log(k+1)$, <a id="eq:allocation-bound"></a>$$\label{eq:allocation-bound}
 V:=\mathbb E\sum_{t\ge1}\sum_{v\ne\mathrm{root}}
       r_{|v|-1}|q_v(t)-q_v(t-1)|
 \le C_1\ell P+D_{\mathcal T}.$$ Here sums stop at the chosen finite horizon. The finite constant $D_{\mathcal T}$ depends only on the fixed tree, radii, and $k$; it can be chosen independently of the input law, horizon, and number of histories. No initial allocation is prescribed.*

</div>

The proof occupies Sections <a href="#sec:allocation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:allocation">3</a>–<a href="#sec:allocation-finish" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:allocation-finish">5</a>.

At a vertex $v$, the local task is to distribute its amount $q_v$ among the children without violating their serving lower bounds. We construct child allowances $d_i$, then remove at least $(\sum_i d_i-q_v)_+$ from them; additional removals leave mass parked at $v$. The removal choices must remain stable under posterior fluctuations. Section <a href="#sec:proportions" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:proportions">4</a> constructs these choices, and Section <a href="#sec:allocation-finish" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:allocation-finish">5</a> proves that the resulting allocations meet their lower bounds and move slowly.

This section supplies the rank lower bounds and the allowances. It uses coarse estimates to control changes of the removal rule’s parameters, and finer estimates for the final allowances. Only the coarse variation will be multiplied by logarithmic potential slopes; keeping these two budgets separate preserves the single factor of $\ell$ in the theorem.

<a id="eq:rank-drift-tree"></a>
Rank coordinates for subtree occupancies also occur in the assignment polytope of (Bubeck et al. 2018, sec. 3.2) <a id="pm-cite-24"></a>[[BCLLM18](#ref-BCLLM18)]. Here they are probabilities of the hidden comparator’s occupancies, conditional on the observed history. For $1\le a\le k$, define the rank probabilities and their filtered values by $$p_{va}(t)=\Pr(N_v(t)<a\mid\mathcal F_t),\qquad
 p^-_{va}(t)=\Pr(N_v(t-1)<a\mid\mathcal F_t).$$ Thus $\mathbb E[p^-_{va}(t)\mid\mathcal F_{t-1}]=p_{va}(t-1)$. The drift of this coordinate is $\delta_t p_{va}=p_{va}(t)-p^-_{va}(t)$, rather than its entire change between successive observations. For the star at an internal vertex $v$, put <a id="eq:star-drift"></a>$$\label{eq:star-drift}
 E_v=\mathbb E\sum_{t\ge1}\left(
        \sum_{i\prec v}\sum_{a=1}^k|\delta_t p_{ia}|
             +\sum_{a=1}^k|\delta_t p_{va}|\right).$$ These local budgets satisfy $$\label{eq:rank-drift-tree}
             \sum_{v\text{ internal}}r_{|v|}E_v\le6P.$$ Indeed, for two integer counts $n,n'\in\{0,\ldots,k\}$, $$\sum_{a=1}^k|\mathbf 1_{\{n<a\}}-\mathbf 1_{\{n'<a\}}|=|n-n'|.$$ Use the coupling of successive hidden configurations and conditional expectation to bound each rank drift by the corresponding indicator change. A nonroot vertex of depth $d$ receives weight at most $r_{d-1}+r_d$ when all stars are summed. If one labeled member moves between leaves with last common ancestor of depth $j$, its indicators change on the two paths strictly below that ancestor. Its total charge is at most $$2\sum_{d=j+1}^J(r_{d-1}+r_d)
 \le\frac{2(1+\tau^{-1})}{1-\tau^{-1}}r_j\le6r_j.$$ The root count is constant. Summing over the members proves <a href="#eq:rank-drift-tree" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:rank-drift-tree">[eq:rank-drift-tree]</a>.

<a id="pm-h-17"></a>
#### Explicit rank functions and compatible lower bounds

<a id="stmt-lem-rank-functions"></a>
<div id="lem:rank-functions" class="lemma">

**Lemma 6** (Rank functions). *There are absolute constants $p_*,s_*,\epsilon_*>0$, with $p_*<s_*<1$, and functions $l^\beta:[0,1]\to[0,1]$ for $3\le\beta\le3+\epsilon_*$ such that:*

1.  *$l^\beta$ is convex and nonincreasing, equals $1-\beta p$ on $[0,p_*]$, is positive on $[0,s_*)$, and is zero on $[s_*,1]$;*

2.  *$l^\beta$ is affine and nonincreasing in $\beta$, and the square roots $\sqrt{l^\beta}$ have a common absolute Lipschitz constant;*

<a id="eq:rank-derivative"></a>
3.  *if $g(p)=-\partial_\beta l^\beta(p)$, then $$\label{eq:rank-derivative}
     \frac1{96}l^\beta(p)\le g(p)\le l^\beta(p)
           \qquad(p\in[p_*/4,1]).$$*

*One may take $p_*=1/24$, $s_*=5/8$, and $\epsilon_*=1/100$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-rank-functions"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-rank-functions</summary>

*Proof.* Set $t_*=s_*-p_*=7/12$ and $$A_*=\frac{18}{7},\qquad c_*'=\frac6{49},\qquad
 d_*'=\frac{1152}{343},\qquad b_*'=c_*'+d_*'t_*=\frac{102}{49}.$$ For $0\le z\le1/100$ define <a id="eq:explicit-rank"></a>$$\label{eq:explicit-rank}
 l^{3+z}(p)=
 \begin{cases}
  1-(3+z)p,&0\le p\le p_*,\\
  (s_*-p)^2\bigl[A_*-z(c_*'+d_*'(p-p_*))\bigr],
                                     &p_*\le p\le s_*,\\
  0,&s_*\le p\le1.
 \end{cases}$$ The identities $$A_*t_*^2=1-3p_*,\quad 2A_*t_*=3,\quad
 c_*'t_*^2=p_*,\quad -2c_*'t_*+d_*'t_*^2=1$$ show that both values and first derivatives match at $p_*$. They also show that $g$ is $p$ on the first interval and $(s_*-p)^2(c_*'+d_*'(p-p_*))$ on the second.

Writing $x=s_*-p$, the middle expression for $l^{3+z}$ is $$(A_*-zb_*')x^2+zd_*'x^3.$$ Its two coefficients are nonnegative, with $A_*-zb_*'>2$. Consequently it is positive before the cutoff, has nonpositive $p$-derivative, and has positive second derivative. Its value and first derivative are zero at the cutoff. Together with the matching at $p_*$, this proves global convexity and monotonicity. On the first interval the function is bounded away from zero, so its square root has bounded derivative. On the middle interval its square root is $x\sqrt{A_*-zb_*'+zd_*'x}$, which also has bounded derivative, uniformly in $z$. The matching and cutoff give the global Lipschitz bound.

For $p\in[p_*/4,p_*]$, the quotient $g(p)/l^{3+z}(p)=p/(1-(3+z)p)$ lies between $1/96$ and $1$. On the middle interval the quotient is $$\frac{b_*'-d_*'x}{A_*-zb_*'+zd_*'x},\qquad 0<x\le t_*.$$ It is at least $c_*'/A_*=1/21$ and at most $b_*'/(A_*-(1/100)b_*')<1$. Beyond the cutoff both functions vanish. This proves <a href="#eq:rank-derivative" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:rank-derivative">[eq:rank-derivative]</a>. ◻

</details>

Fix also the broader size function $$l^{\mathrm{siz}}(p)=\frac{(3/4-p)_+^2}{(3/4)^2},\qquad
 L_v^\beta=\sum_{a=1}^k l^\beta(p_{va}),\qquad
 M_v=\sum_{a=1}^k l^{\mathrm{siz}}(p_{va}).$$ Its square root is Lipschitz. Both kinds of functions vanish at one and have value one at zero.

The next comparison is a majorization argument in the sense of Karamata (Karamata 1932) <a id="pm-cite-25"></a>[[Karamata32](#ref-Karamata32)]. We include the convex-hinge proof to specify the direction of the comparison and the role of the padded coordinates.

<a id="stmt-lem-superadditivity"></a>
<div id="lem:superadditivity" class="lemma">

**Lemma 7** (Superadditivity). *For every internal vertex $v$ and every allowed $\beta$, <a id="eq:rank-superadditivity"></a>$$\label{eq:rank-superadditivity}
 \sum_{i\prec v}L_i^\beta\le L_v^\beta,\qquad
 \sum_{i\prec v}M_i\le M_v\le m_v.$$ No independence between child counts is required.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-superadditivity"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-superadditivity</summary>

*Proof.* Suppose there are $d$ children. Pool their $dk$ rank probabilities and sort them increasingly, obtaining a vector $x$. Let $y$ be the parent’s $k$ increasingly ordered rank probabilities followed by $(d-1)k$ ones. Their total sums agree and equal $dk-m_v$. If the first $n$ coordinates of $x$ contain $n_i$ coordinates from child $i$, those coordinates can be chosen as the first $n_i$ of that child. Since $N_v=\sum_iN_i$, conditional expectation gives $$\sum_{b=1}^n x_b
 =\sum_i\mathbb E[(n_i-N_i)_+\mid\mathcal F_t]
 \ge\mathbb E[(n-N_v)_+\mid\mathcal F_t]
 =\sum_{b=1}^n y_b.$$ The last identity also holds for $n>k$, precisely because of the padding. For every real $b$, an increasing vector $z$ satisfies $$\sum_a(b-z_a)_+
       =\max_{0\le n\le dk}\left(nb-\sum_{a=1}^n z_a\right).$$ Thus the sum of every convex hinge $(b-p)_+$ on $x$ is no larger than its sum on $y$. Affine functions have equal sums. Every convex piecewise linear function is an affine function plus a nonnegative linear combination of these hinges. Approximating a continuous convex function by its linear interpolants proves the same comparison for that function. Apply it to $l^\beta$ and $l^{\mathrm{siz}}$; the padded ones contribute zero. Finally convexity and the endpoint values give $l^{\mathrm{siz}}(p)\le1-p$, and $\sum_a(1-p_{va})=m_v$. ◻

</details>

<a id="eq:vertex-beta"></a>
From now on set $c_*=10^{-4}$; thus $100c_*<l^{\mathrm{siz}}(s_*)=1/36$. Choose an absolute $c_\beta>0$ so small that $c_\beta(1+\log(1/c_*))\le\epsilon_*/2$, and define $$\label{eq:vertex-beta}
 \beta_v=3+\frac{c_\beta}{\ell}
                \log\frac{k}{\max(M_v,c_*)}.$$ It belongs to $[3,3+\epsilon_*/2]$, because $M_v\le k$ and $\log(k/c_*)/\ell\le1+\log(1/c_*)$. The invariant propagated down the tree will be <a id="eq:allocation-lower-bound"></a>$$\label{eq:allocation-lower-bound}
                         q_v\ge L_v^{\beta_v}.$$ Superadditivity gives compatible child lower bounds at the parent’s parameter $\beta_v$. Since $M_i\le M_v$, the child’s own parameter satisfies $\beta_i\ge\beta_v$, and its required bound is no larger. The resulting difference is the slack available for choosing slowly varying child amounts. We will estimate this difference in terms of the relative child sizes. At the root all ranks are zero, $M_{\mathrm{root}}=k$, and the lower bound is $k$. At a required leaf $p_{v1}=0$, so the lower bound is at least one.

<a id="pm-h-18"></a>
#### A rank tracker with a bounded affine potential

The input to a star may fluctuate substantially when information is revealed. The following tracker charges only drift, using signed filtering changes before it decides whether to refresh.

<a id="stmt-lem-rank-tracking"></a>
<div id="lem:rank-tracking" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:rank-relative-tracker"></a>
**Lemma 8** (Rank tracking). *Let $p_t,p_t^-\in[0,1]$ obey $\mathbb E[p_t^-\mid\mathcal F_{t-1}]=p_{t-1}$, and let $\mathcal E=\mathbb E\sum_t|p_t-p_t^-|$. If $f\ge0$ has an absolutely bounded Lipschitz constant for $\sqrt f$, then for $0<\xi<1/2$ there is a held estimate $\widehat f_t$ with $$\label{eq:rank-relative-tracker}
 (1+\xi)^{-1}f(p_t)\le\widehat f_t\le(1+\xi)f(p_t),\qquad
 \mathbb E\sum_t|\widehat f_t-\widehat f_{t-1}|
       \le\frac{C}{\xi}(\mathcal E+1).$$ In particular zeros are tracked exactly. A held estimate $\widehat p_t$ refreshed when $|p_t-\widehat p_{t-1}|>\delta$ has expected number of refreshes at most $\delta^{-2}(\mathcal E+1)$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-rank-tracking"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-rank-tracking</summary>

*Proof.* Initialize a reference $p^\circ=p_0$ and hold $f(p^\circ)$ until the displayed factor comparison fails, then replace $p^\circ$ by $p_t$. With the reference held, use $$\Phi(p;p^\circ)=p(1-p)+(p-p^\circ)^2
              =(1-2p^\circ)p+(p^\circ)^2.$$ This is affine in $p$, lies in $[0,1]$, and has slope of absolute value at most one. At every step, first evaluate its change from $p_{t-1}$ to $p_t^-$ and then from $p_t^-$ to $p_t$, holding the old reference. The former change has conditional expectation zero, and the latter is at most $|p_t-p_t^-|$. A reference reset then decreases $\Phi$ by exactly $(p_t-p^\circ)^2$. Telescoping gives <a id="eq:rank-reset-credit"></a>$$\label{eq:rank-reset-credit}
        \mathbb E\sum_{\text{resets}}(p_t-p^\circ)^2\le\mathcal E+1.$$

For two nonnegative values $a,b$ failing factor $1+\xi$ comparison, $$(\sqrt a-\sqrt b)^2
 =|a-b|\frac{|\sqrt a-\sqrt b|}{\sqrt a+\sqrt b}
 \ge \frac{\xi}{5}|a-b|.$$ The inequality includes the case in which exactly one value is zero. The Lipschitz bound for $\sqrt f$ therefore implies $(p_t-p^\circ)^2\ge c\xi|f(p_t)-f(p^\circ)|$ at every reset, proving <a href="#eq:rank-relative-tracker" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:rank-relative-tracker">[eq:rank-relative-tracker]</a>. The same potential proves the absolute-threshold claim, since every reset then releases more than $\delta^2$. Filtering was evaluated at every step before the reset test; no expectation was restricted to a selected reset event. ◻

</details>

<a id="eq:core-decomposition"></a>
<a id="eq:inactive-rank-vanishing"></a>
Apply the absolute tracker with threshold $p_*/8$ to every child rank. A rank is called *core* when its held estimate is at most $p_*/2$. After the update, every core rank has $p\le5p_*/8$, every noncore rank has $p>3p_*/8$, and every rank with $p<p_*/4$ is core. Let $K_t$ count the changed core flags in this star at step $t$, and set <a id="eq:core-data"></a>$$\label{eq:core-data}
 n_i=\#\{a:\text{$a$ is core at $i$}\},\quad
 B_i=\sum_{a\text{ core at }i}p_{ia},\quad
 S=\sum_iB_i,\quad
 f_i^\beta=\sum_{a\text{ noncore at }i}l^\beta(p_{ia}).$$ We call $f_i^\beta$ the *noncore*, or *flex*, contribution. The core ranks lie in the affine part of every rank function, so $$\label{eq:core-decomposition}
 L_i^\beta=n_i-\beta B_i+f_i^\beta,\qquad
       n_i+\sup_\beta f_i^\beta\le C M_i.$$ For the second bound, a core rank contributes at least $l^{\mathrm{siz}}(5p_*/8)>0$ to $M_i$. Any positive $l^\beta(p)$ has $p<s_*$, where $l^{\mathrm{siz}}(p)\ge1/36$ and $l^\beta(p)\le1$. These termwise bounds prove the assertion with an absolute constant. In particular, $$\label{eq:inactive-rank-vanishing}
 M_i\le10c_*\quad\Longrightarrow\quad
 n_i=0\ \text{ and }\ L_i^\beta=f_i^\beta=0\ \text{for all $\beta$}.$$ Also, if the old flags are held while the ranks are filtered and drifted, the resulting old-flag sum always satisfies $B_i\le n_i^{\mathrm{old}}$, even if those old flags no longer describe small current probabilities. This elementary bound will be used at wholesale changes.

<a id="pm-h-19"></a>
#### Coarse data, active epochs, and parameter gaps

We now describe a schedule local to a star. Its estimates are computed from the final, post-filtering and post-drift, ranks on each step. All elementary term trackers persist throughout the entire process, including times when a child is inactive and times when the schedule below is restarted.

<a id="eq:active-size-accuracy"></a>
Choose a sufficiently small absolute size tolerance $\delta_s>0$, for example $10^{-3}$. Track every term of $M_i$ by Lemma <a href="#lem:rank-tracking" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:rank-tracking">8</a> with this tolerance, and let $\widehat M_i$ be their sum. A child is either inactive, with $a_i=0$, or active. Turn an inactive child on when $\widehat M_i\ge5c_*$, setting $a_i=\widehat M_i$. Turn an active child off when $\widehat M_i\le2c_*$. Otherwise, during an active period hold $a_i$ until factor $1+\delta_s$ comparison with $\widehat M_i$ fails, then replace $a_i$ by $\widehat M_i$. Initialize with the same rules, using either admissible state in the overlap. These choices ensure $$\label{eq:active-size-accuracy}
 \begin{gathered}
 0.9M_i\le a_i\le1.1M_i\quad\text{at active children},\qquad
 M_i\ge c_*\quad\text{if active},\\
 M_i\ge10c_*\quad\Longrightarrow\quad\text{active}.
 \end{gathered}$$ By <a href="#eq:inactive-rank-vanishing" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:inactive-rank-vanishing">[eq:inactive-rank-vanishing]</a>, an inactive child has no core ranks or positive rank lower bounds.

<a id="eq:beta-upper-error"></a>
We also hold an accurate estimate of $X_v=\max(M_v,c_*)$. For this purpose take a possibly smaller absolute tolerance $\delta_p$, track the parent size terms, and clip their sum below at $c_*$, obtaining $\widehat X_v$. Hold $H_v$ equal to its last reference value, refreshing upon failure of factor $1+\delta_p$ comparison. Then $$(1+\delta_p)^{-2}X_v\le H_v\le(1+\delta_p)^2X_v.$$ Put <a id="eq:held-beta-upper"></a>$$\label{eq:held-beta-upper}
 X_v^{\mathrm{low}}=\max\{c_*,H_v/(1+\delta_p)^2\},\qquad
 \beta^+=3+\frac{c_\beta}{\ell}\log\frac{k}{X_v^{\mathrm{low}}}.$$ Here $X_v^{\mathrm{low}}\le X_v\le k$, and $X_v^{\mathrm{low}}\ge X_v/(1+\delta_p)^4$. Thus, for any prescribed small absolute $\epsilon>0$, choosing $\delta_p$ sufficiently small gives $$\label{eq:beta-upper-error}
       \beta_v\le\beta^+\le\beta_v+\epsilon/\ell.$$ The parameter stays in the allowed interval and changes only when $H_v$ is refreshed.

An *epoch* starts with a *wholesale change*, performed initially and whenever $\beta^+$ changes or the cumulative $\ell_1$ variation of the held size vector since the epoch started exceeds $A/100$. Here $A=\sum_i a_i$ at the start is held for that epoch. Empty to nonempty or nonempty to empty changes always start a new epoch. At a wholesale change, choose a dominant index $o$ if some $a_o\ge0.85A$ and $A>0$. It is unique. Keep that mark until the next wholesale change. All other active indices, or all active indices when there is no mark, are called regular. With a dominant index the regular indices are also called sides. No elementary size or rank tracker is restarted at an epoch boundary.

During a marked epoch, let $T_{\mathrm{side}}=\sum_{i\ne o}a_i$. Set $U=T_{\mathrm{side}}$ at its start. Subsequently hold $U$ until factor $1+\delta_s$ comparison with $T_{\mathrm{side}}$ fails, then refresh it to that side total. This includes transitions to or from zero. When sides exist set $u=U/A$; otherwise set $u=0$. For active children define <a id="eq:gap-weights"></a>$$\label{eq:gap-weights}
 h_i=1+\max\{0,\log(A/a_i)\}\quad(i\text{ regular}),\qquad
 h_o=u\quad(o\text{ dominant}).$$ The empty active domain needs none of these parameters.

<a id="eq:dominant-scale-definition"></a>
Finally prepare a coarse scale for a marked child. Independently of the fine estimates used below, track every child rank value $l^3(p_{ia})$ with tolerance $\delta_s$, and write its estimate as $\widehat l^{\mathrm{coarse}}_{ia}$. With a dominant index set $$\label{eq:dominant-scale-definition}
             D=\sum_{a\text{ noncore at }o}
                       \widehat l^{\mathrm{coarse}}_{oa};$$ set $D=0$ when no dominant index exists. All these term trackers run on every child and every rank even when their estimates are not currently used in $D$.

Here is a precise inventory of the coarse charges. Write $a_i^-,a_i^+$ for the old and new held sizes on a step, and similarly for other held quantities; these superscripts in this inventory refer to step-end states, not to the filtered hidden configuration. Let $\mathcal W_v$ be the wholesale steps after initialization, $\mathcal H_v$ the steps at which $H_v$ is refreshed, and $\mathcal U_v$ the non-wholesale steps at which $U$ is refreshed. Define the nonnegative charge <a id="eq:coarse-charge-definition"></a>$$\label{eq:coarse-charge-definition}
\begin{aligned}
 C_{v,t}={}&K_t+
    \sum_{i:a_i^-\ne a_i^+}(a_i^-+a_i^+)
    +\mathbf 1_{\{t\in\mathcal H_v\}}(H_v^-+H_v^+)\\
 &+\mathbf 1_{\{t\in\mathcal W_v\}}\sum_i(a_i^-+a_i^+)
    +\mathbf 1_{\{t\in\mathcal U_v\}}
                    (T_{\mathrm{side}}^-+T_{\mathrm{side}}^+)
    +|D^+-D^-|.
\end{aligned}$$ The side totals in this expression use the unchanged dominant mark on a non-wholesale step. A wholesale change of $U$, including a change of mark, is covered by the wholesale total in the preceding term.

<a id="stmt-lem-parameter-gaps"></a>
<div id="lem:parameter-gaps" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:coarse-data-budget"></a>
<a id="eq:parameter-gap"></a>
**Lemma 9** (Coarse budgets and parameter gaps). *The data above satisfy, for an absolute constant $C$ and a finite $D_v^{\mathrm{data}}$ depending only on the fixed star and $k$, $$\label{eq:coarse-data-budget}
             \mathbb E\sum_t C_{v,t}\le C E_v+D_v^{\mathrm{data}}.$$ The constant is uniform in the law and horizon. Every active child obeys $$\label{eq:parameter-gap}
        0\le h_i\le C\ell,\qquad
        \beta_i-\beta_v\ge\frac{c_g h_i}{\ell}$$ for an absolute $c_g>0$. If a dominant index and sides are present, then $0<u<1/4$, and with $H=1+\log(1/u)$ every side satisfies $h_i\ge cH$ for an absolute $c>0$. In addition, $n_i+B_i+\sup_\beta f_i^\beta\le C a_i$ at active children, and all these quantities vanish at inactive children.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-parameter-gaps"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-parameter-gaps</summary>

*Proof.* We give the amortization details because paying a large size on every small refresh would not suffice. Lemma <a href="#lem:rank-tracking" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:rank-tracking">8</a>, summed over the fixed collection of size terms, bounds the expected total variation of their estimates by $CE_v$ plus a fixed initialization constant. It bounds $\mathbb E\sum_tK_t$ in the same way. Constants may depend on the fixed absolute tolerances but never on $k$ or the degree.

For a scalar estimate $x$ held at a reference $b>0$, a factor $1+\delta$ failure gives $$b+x\le(1+2/\delta)|x-b|.$$ The last difference is bounded by the variation of $x$ since its previous reference setting; these intervals are disjoint. This pays all active to active changes of $a_i$ and all changes of $H_v$. Turning a child on after an off period costs its new size, and is paid by the variation from a previous value at most $2c_*$ to a value at least $5c_*$. The first such event may instead use an initialization charge. For a turn-off, if the last held size exceeds $4c_*$, variation since that setting pays it because the new estimate is at most $2c_*$. Otherwise the charge is at most $4c_*$ and is paid by the traversal from at least $5c_*$ at the start of this on period to at most $2c_*$ at its end. Each on period and each off period is used only once in these latter arguments. They overlap the scalar refresh intervals only a bounded number of times. This proves the budget for all changed-$a_i$ charges. Clipping the parent sum below at $c_*$ does not increase its variation, so the same argument applies to $H_v$.

When $\beta^+$ changes, the old and new active totals are at most $C(X_v^-+X_v^+)\le C(H_v^-+H_v^+)$, and are therefore paid by that parent refresh. For a wholesale event from the vector-variation test, let $R$ be the cumulative variation since the previous epoch started and let $A$ be that epoch’s initial total. The old total is at most $1.01A$, the new total is at most $1.01A+R$, and $R>A/100$. Their sum is consequently at most $203R$. The intervals of variation assigned to successive epochs are disjoint. If $A=0$, the new total is at most $R$, and the same conclusion holds. Discarding unused variation credit when a parent refresh intervenes cannot increase its total use. This proves the wholesale budget.

On a fixed marked epoch, variation of $T_{\mathrm{side}}$ is at most $\sum_i|\Delta a_i|$. The scalar relative-refresh argument pays every $U$ refresh by variation since its previous setting. The actual old side total is within factor $1+\delta_s$ of that reference, so the old plus new side totals have the same bound. A zero crossing is included: the variation then pays the positive reference or positive new total. Setting $U$ at a wholesale change is paid by the wholesale total. Thus all its asserted charges are covered.

At a non-wholesale step the dominant index is unchanged. The variation of <a href="#eq:dominant-scale-definition" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:dominant-scale-definition">[eq:dominant-scale-definition]</a> is at most the summed variation of its coarse term estimates plus $CK_t$: a changed flag inserts or removes one term, each at most one. At a wholesale step, its old and new values are at most $C(a_o^-+a_o^+)$, interpreted as zero when that mark is absent; this follows from the relative estimates and <a href="#eq:core-decomposition" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:core-decomposition">[eq:core-decomposition]</a>. The wholesale totals pay these changes. This proves <a href="#eq:coarse-data-budget" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:coarse-data-budget">[eq:coarse-data-budget]</a>.

<a id="eq:size-log-comparison"></a>
For the gaps, at every state of an epoch with $A>0$ the current active total is in $[0.99A,1.01A]$. In an unmarked epoch every child has $a_i\le0.86A$. In a marked epoch the marked size is at least $0.84A$, and the side total is at most $0.16A$; in particular the mark stays active. Therefore every regular child has $a_i\le0.86A$. By <a href="#eq:active-size-accuracy" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:active-size-accuracy">[eq:active-size-accuracy]</a> and superadditivity, $$\label{eq:size-log-comparison}
 \frac{M_v}{M_i}\ge
 1+\frac9{11}\frac{\sum_{j\ne i,\ a_j>0}a_j}{a_i}
 \ge1+\frac9{11}\left(\frac{0.99A}{a_i}-1\right).$$ For $x=A/a_i\ge1/0.86$, the logarithm of the last expression is at least $c(1+\log x)$ for an absolute $c>0$. To see the uniformity, the ratio to $1+\log x$ is continuous and positive on this interval and tends to one as $x\to\infty$; on a sufficiently large tail it is at least $1/2$, and on the remaining compact interval it has a positive minimum. This proves the regular comparison. For the dominant child, use the first inequality in <a href="#eq:size-log-comparison" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:size-log-comparison">[eq:size-log-comparison]</a>, the comparisons $T_{\mathrm{side}}\asymp U$, and $a_o\le1.01A$ to obtain $$\log(M_v/M_o)\ge cU/A=cu.$$ Here $u\le(1+\delta_s)0.16<1/4$, so $\log(1+cu)\ge c'u$. If no sides exist, $u=0$ and the claimed inequality is automatic.

At every active child $M_i\ge c_*$ and $M_v\ge M_i$, so the clipping in <a href="#eq:vertex-beta" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:vertex-beta">[eq:vertex-beta]</a> is inactive at both vertices. Hence $$\beta_i-\beta_v
                  =\frac{c_\beta}{\ell}\log(M_v/M_i),$$ which proves the gap in <a href="#eq:parameter-gap" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:parameter-gap">[eq:parameter-gap]</a>. Active held sizes have an absolute positive lower bound and $A\le1.1k$, proving $h_i\le C\ell$. On a side, $a_i\le T_{\mathrm{side}}\le(1+\delta_s)uA$, whence $$h_i\ge1+\log(1/u)-\log(1+\delta_s)\ge H/2.$$ The final bounds on core and flex data follow from <a href="#eq:core-decomposition" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:core-decomposition">[eq:core-decomposition]</a>, $B_i\le n_i$, and <a href="#eq:active-size-accuracy" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:active-size-accuracy">[eq:active-size-accuracy]</a>. Inactivity was addressed in <a href="#eq:inactive-rank-vanishing" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:inactive-rank-vanishing">[eq:inactive-rank-vanishing]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-20"></a>
#### Fine caps and their separate budget

The proportions constructed in the next section will use only the coarse data above and the raw core sums $B_i$. The following finer estimates enter only the final allocations. Keeping their variation out of the proportion potentials is necessary for the single factor of $\ell$ in Theorem <a href="#thm:allocation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:allocation">5</a>.

The parameter gap at a regular child leaves room to track its flex contribution between $f_i^{\beta_i}$ and $f_i^{\beta_v}$. A marked child can have an arbitrarily smaller gap as its side mass tends to zero. We therefore use a small upper approximation at that child; Section <a href="#sec:proportions" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:proportions">4</a> explains how this asymmetry changes the removal rule.

<a id="stmt-lem-caps"></a>
<div id="lem:caps" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:fine-cap-budget"></a>
**Lemma 10** (Caps). *Given a sufficiently small absolute $\epsilon_b>0$, the remaining absolute tolerances can be chosen so that there are caps $d_i=n_i+b_i$, zero on inactive children, with <a id="eq:cap-feasibility"></a>$$\label{eq:cap-feasibility}
 \begin{aligned}
 f_i^{\beta_i}\le b_i\le f_i^{\beta_v}
                                      &&(i\text{ regular}),\\
 T:=f_o^{\beta_v}\le b_o\le(1+\epsilon_b/\ell)T,
 \qquad c_DT\le D\le C_DT
                                      &&(o\text{ dominant}).
 \end{aligned}$$ Here $c_D,C_D>0$ are absolute, fixed independently of $\epsilon_b$ and the fine tolerances; in particular $D=0$ if and only if $T=0$. At every child $d_i\le C M_i\le C m_i$. Their variation away from wholesale changes satisfies $$\label{eq:fine-cap-budget}
 \mathbb E\sum_{t\notin\mathcal W_v}\sum_i|d_i(t)-d_i(t-1)|
          \le C\ell E_v+D_v^{\mathrm{cap}},$$ where $D_v^{\mathrm{cap}}$ is uniform in the law and horizon. At wholesale changes the old plus new caps are bounded by $C$ times the old plus new active totals.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-caps"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-caps</summary>

<a id="eq:flex-estimate-error"></a>
*Proof.* For every rank, at both endpoint parameters $3$ and $3+\epsilon_*$, run separate relative trackers with tolerance $\rho/\ell$, where $\rho>0$ is a small absolute constant to be selected. Interpolate their estimates by the convex coefficients expressing $\beta^+$ between these endpoints, and sum over the current noncore ranks at child $i$. Call the result $\widetilde f_i$. The affinity in $\beta$ and the relative endpoint estimates imply $$(1+\rho/\ell)^{-1} f_i^{\beta^+}
       \le\widetilde f_i\le(1+\rho/\ell)f_i^{\beta^+}.$$ Every noncore rank has $p>3p_*/8$, so <a href="#eq:rank-derivative" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:rank-derivative">[eq:rank-derivative]</a> applies to it. Together with <a href="#eq:beta-upper-error" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:beta-upper-error">[eq:beta-upper-error]</a>, this yields $$\label{eq:flex-estimate-error}
       |\widetilde f_i-f_i^{\beta_v}|
          \le\frac{e}{\ell}f_i^{\beta_v},\qquad
                      e\le C(\rho+\epsilon).$$ For example, $f_i^{\beta_v}-f_i^{\beta^+}
=(\beta^+-\beta_v)\sum_{\mathrm{noncore}}g(p_{ia})
\le(\epsilon/\ell)f_i^{\beta_v}$. If the true sum is zero then both endpoint estimates, and therefore the interpolated sum, are exactly zero, since all the functions have the same cutoff.

<a id="eq:regular-flex-slack"></a>
For a regular child, $h_i\ge1$ and the parameter gap gives $$\label{eq:regular-flex-slack}
 f_i^{\beta_i}
 =f_i^{\beta_v}-(\beta_i-\beta_v)
                  \sum_{\mathrm{noncore}}g(p_{ia})
 \le(1-\gamma/\ell)f_i^{\beta_v},
             \qquad \gamma=c_g/96>0.$$ Choose an absolute $0<\lambda_b\le\min\{\gamma/4,\epsilon_b/4,1/4\}$, and then choose $\rho,\epsilon$ so small that the $e$ in <a href="#eq:flex-estimate-error" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:flex-estimate-error">[eq:flex-estimate-error]</a> is at most $\lambda_b/4$. Define $$b_i=(1-\lambda_b/\ell)\widetilde f_i\quad(i\text{ regular}),
 \qquad
 b_o=(1+\lambda_b/\ell)\widetilde f_o\quad(o\text{ dominant}).$$ For a regular child the upper estimate is at most $f_i^{\beta_v}$, because $e\le\lambda_b$; the lower estimate is at least $(1-(\lambda_b+e)/\ell)f_i^{\beta_v}$, which dominates <a href="#eq:regular-flex-slack" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:regular-flex-slack">[eq:regular-flex-slack]</a>. For a dominant child, $(1+\lambda_b/\ell)(1-e/\ell)\ge1$, whereas $(1+\lambda_b/\ell)(1+e/\ell)\le1+\epsilon_b/\ell$. This proves the cap comparisons.

On the noncore range <a href="#eq:rank-derivative" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:rank-derivative">[eq:rank-derivative]</a> also gives $$l^{\beta_v}\le l^3
       =l^{\beta_v}+(\beta_v-3)g
       \le(1+\epsilon_*)l^{\beta_v}.$$ The coarse tracker defining $D$ therefore satisfies $$(1+\delta_s)^{-1}T\le D
       \le(1+\delta_s)(1+\epsilon_*)T.$$ These choices give the claimed $c_D,C_D$ and show that their values precede all fine choices. The bound $d_i\le C M_i$ follows from <a href="#eq:core-decomposition" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:core-decomposition">[eq:core-decomposition]</a> and the relative estimates.

It remains to account for variation. Lemma <a href="#lem:rank-tracking" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:rank-tracking">8</a> with tolerance $\rho/\ell$ pays the summed variation of all endpoint estimates by $C\ell E_v$ plus a fixed constant. On a non-wholesale step, $\beta^+$ and the dominant mark are unchanged, so their interpolation coefficients and the factors $1\pm\lambda_b/\ell$ are fixed. Changes of the noncore sets cost at most $CK_t$, as does the variation of $n_i$. An inactive child has zero true endpoint values and hence zero endpoint estimates; setting its cap to zero therefore adds no variation beyond the same term and flag changes. This proves <a href="#eq:fine-cap-budget" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:fine-cap-budget">[eq:fine-cap-budget]</a>. At a wholesale step, $d_i^-+d_i^+\le C(a_i^-+a_i^+)$, including zero inactive values, and summing proves the remaining statement. ◻

</details>

All the additive terms in this section arise from bounded potentials and bounded initialization charges for a fixed number of rank trackers. There are only $k$ ranks per vertex of the fixed finite tree. The term trackers are never reinitialized when an epoch changes or a tree label reappears. Thus their total additive allowance is uniform over every law and horizon. The coarse allowance <a href="#eq:coarse-data-budget" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:coarse-data-budget">[eq:coarse-data-budget]</a> and the fine allowance <a href="#eq:fine-cap-budget" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:fine-cap-budget">[eq:fine-cap-budget]</a> are separate: only the former will be multiplied by the $O(\ell)$ slopes of the next section’s potentials.

The remaining construction starts from the caps $d_i$ and distributes the required reduction $\bigl(\sum_i d_i-q_v\bigr)_+$ between children. The star’s parameters, flags, and proportion trackers can all be updated from its own rank inputs before $q_v$ is known. The actual allocations are then propagated from the root downwards.

<a id="sec:proportions"></a>
### Slow proportions with changing domains

We continue the construction at one fixed star $v$, using the parameters of Lemmas <a href="#lem:parameter-gaps" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:parameter-gaps">9</a> and <a href="#lem:caps" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:caps">10</a>. The caps $d_i$ will be reduced to fit the parent amount. This section constructs proportions for those reductions. The construction depends only on the star’s observed rank posteriors and held parameters; the parent amount $q_v$ is not an input.

<a id="eq:proportion-parameter-facts"></a>
Write $I$ for the active children, and extend all vectors by zero to the fixed set of children when comparing different times. Recall that $B_i$ is the sum of the current rank probabilities over the core flags, $S=\sum_i B_i$, and inactive children have no core flags. If a dominant child $o$ is marked, the other active children are called sides. For regular children and for a marked child with sides, respectively, the held parameters satisfy $$\label{eq:proportion-parameter-facts}
\begin{gathered}
  1\le h_i\le C\ell\quad(i\text{ regular}),\qquad
  h_o=u=U/A\in(0,1),\qquad H=1+\log(1/u),\\
  h_i\ge cH\quad(i\text{ a side}),\qquad
  \sum_{i\text{ regular}}e^{-h_i}\le
  \begin{cases} C,&\text{no dominant child},\\ Cu,&\text{a dominant child}.
  \end{cases}
\end{gathered}$$ The last assertion follows from $e^{-h_i}\le a_i/A$ and the comparison of $U$ with the side total. These facts hold throughout each epoch. When $I$ is empty there is no proportion to choose. When $I=\{o\}$, we use $\alpha_o=1$, $u=h_o=0$, and no side vector. Both potentials below are then defined to be zero. In particular, we never evaluate a denominator with $u=0$.

<a id="pm-h-22"></a>
#### Removal constraints and the simplex tracker

<a id="eq:alpha-feasibility"></a>
Fix a sufficiently small absolute $c_t>0$ and put $\eta_i=c_t h_i/\ell$. For a nonempty active domain we require $$\label{eq:alpha-feasibility}
  \alpha_i\ge0,\qquad \sum_{i\in I}\alpha_i=1,
  \qquad S\alpha_i\le(1+\eta_i)B_i\quad(i\in I).$$ This is automatic on a singleton domain.

To see the role of the tolerance $\eta_i$, temporarily fix any parent amount $q_v\ge L_v^{\beta_v}$. Removing the excess $R_0=(\sum_i d_i-q_v)_+$ in proportions $\alpha$ would give $q_i=d_i-R_0\alpha_i$. The cap bounds and superadditivity imply $$R_0\le\beta_v S+e_o,\qquad
 0\le e_o:=b_o-f_o^{\beta_v}\le\epsilon_b f_o^{\beta_v}/\ell,$$ where $e_o=0$ without a marked child. For $S>0$, a regular child’s removal is therefore at most $(\beta_v+e_o/S)(1+\eta_i)B_i$. The term $\beta_v B_i$ is already allowed by the core decomposition $L_i^{\beta_v}=n_i-\beta_v B_i+f_i^{\beta_v}$. The parameter gap $\beta_i-\beta_v$ pays the extra coefficient, provided $S$ is at least a fixed multiple of the dominant flex mass, which is comparable to $D$. At the marked child the error $e_o$ itself can be subtracted from its upper cap, since $\alpha_o\le1$.

When $S$ is small relative to $D$, the ratio $e_o/S$ need not be controlled. We then choose side removals $\omega_i$ first and give the marked child the remaining parent amount, capped by $d_o$. Superadditivity bounds its uncapped remaining amount below by $$L_o^{\beta_v}
                  +\sum_{i\ne o}(\omega_i-\beta_v B_i).$$ It therefore suffices to bound the total signed shortfall of the side removals below $\beta_v B_i$ by a small multiple of $uD/\ell$; the marked child’s flex slack pays this error. The removals must also stay below $\beta_iB_i$ to preserve the side lower bounds. The side tracker below constructs $w$ so that $\omega_i=Dw_i$ has both properties. Section <a href="#sec:allocation-finish" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:allocation-finish">5</a> verifies the two output rules, including their zero cases, and chooses between them by overlapping comparisons of $S$ and $D$.

Near dominance also changes the simplex tracker itself. The marked child’s parameter gap can be only of order $u/\ell$, forcing $\eta_o=c_tu/\ell$. Its potential must remain effective as $u\downarrow0$; the different denominator below ensures this. We will also normalize its primitive at $\alpha_o=1$, so changing $u$ costs in proportion to the side sizes rather than the full dominant size.

On every nonsingleton domain set, for $y\ge0$, <a id="eq:proportion-denominators"></a>$$\label{eq:proportion-denominators}
  F_i(y)=\frac{(h_i+y)^2}{h_i}\quad(i\text{ regular}),
  \qquad F_o(y)=u+Hy(1+y)\quad(o\text{ marked}).$$

<a id="stmt-lem-denominators"></a>
<div id="lem:denominators" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:denominator-estimates"></a>
**Lemma 11** (Uniform denominator estimates). *For every nonsingleton domain above, $$\label{eq:denominator-estimates}
  \int_0^\infty\frac{dy}{F_i(y)}\le C,
  \qquad
  \sum_{i\in I}\alpha_i F_i\bigl(\log(1/\alpha_i)\bigr)
       \le C\sum_{i\in I}\alpha_i h_i$$ for every $\alpha$ in its unit simplex. A summand at $\alpha_i=0$ means its continuous extension, which is zero. All constants are uniform as $u$ tends to zero.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-denominators"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-denominators</summary>

*Proof.* The regular integral equals $1$. For the marked child, split at $1$: $$\int_0^1\frac{dy}{u+Hy(1+y)}
   \le \frac{\log(1+H/u)}H,
 \qquad
 \int_1^\infty\frac{dy}{u+Hy(1+y)}\le\frac1H.$$ Since $\log(1/u)=H-1$ and $\log(1+H/u)\le H-1+\log(1+H)\le2H$, these bounds are absolute.

For the second assertion put $y_i=\log(1/\alpha_i)$ at positive coordinates. If $i$ is regular and $y_i\le4h_i$, its contribution is at most $25\alpha_i h_i$. If $y_i>4h_i$, then $h_i\ge1$ and $$e^{-y_i}\frac{(h_i+y_i)^2}{h_i}
       \le C e^{-y_i}y_i^2
       \le C e^{-y_i/2}\le C e^{-h_i}.$$ Thus the regular tail is at most $C$ without a marked child and at most $Cu$ with one. Without a marked child, $\sum_i\alpha_i h_i\ge1$, which pays this tail.

With a marked child use the elementary inequality $e^{-y}y(1+y)\le C(1-e^{-y})$, valid for all $y\ge0$. It follows, including the endpoint $\alpha_o=0$, that $$\alpha_o F_o\bigl(\log(1/\alpha_o)\bigr)
       \le C\{\alpha_o u+H(1-\alpha_o)\}.$$ By <a href="#eq:proportion-parameter-facts" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:proportion-parameter-facts">[eq:proportion-parameter-facts]</a>, $\sum_i\alpha_i h_i$ bounds this last expression up to an absolute factor. It also bounds $u$ up to an absolute factor: its dominant contribution is $\alpha_o u$, and its side contribution is at least $cH(1-\alpha_o)\ge cu(1-\alpha_o)$. This pays the regular tail and completes the proof. ◻

</details>

<a id="eq:alpha-primitive"></a>
For held parameters and a nonnegative vector $B$, with $S=\sum_i B_i$, define $$\label{eq:alpha-primitive}
\begin{aligned}
 g_i^\alpha(a;B)
   &=C_\alpha\ell\int_0^a
       \frac{S-(1+\eta_i)B_i/s}{F_i(\log(1/s))}\,ds
       &&(i\text{ regular}),\\
 g_o^\alpha(a;B)
   &=C_\alpha\ell\int_1^a
       \frac{S-(1+\eta_o)B_o/s}{F_o(\log(1/s))}\,ds,
       &&(o\text{ marked}),\\
 G^\alpha(\alpha;B)&=\sum_{i\in I}g_i^\alpha(\alpha_i;B).
\end{aligned}$$ The improper integrals at zero converge by $y=\log(1/s)$ and Lemma <a href="#lem:denominators" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:denominators">11</a>. Consequently these functions extend continuously to the whole simplex. No convexity in $\alpha$ is asserted or needed.

<a id="eq:alpha-input-slopes"></a>
We record the bounds needed when parameters change. Uniformly in $\alpha$, $$\label{eq:alpha-input-slopes}
 |G^\alpha(\alpha;B)-G^\alpha(\alpha;\widetilde B)|
       \le C\ell\|B-\widetilde B\|_1.$$ Here and below changing $B$ also changes $S$ to its sum. Indeed the coefficient of $B_i$ in its own $B_i/s$ integral is bounded by $C\ell\int_0^\infty dy/F_i(y)$. The sum of the coefficients of $S$ in the regular components has absolute value at most $C\ell\sum_i\alpha_i\le C\ell$, because $F_i\ge h_i\ge1$. The marked component’s $S$ coefficient is bounded by $C\ell\int_{\alpha_o}^1 ds/(sF_o(\log(1/s)))\le C\ell$. This proves <a href="#eq:alpha-input-slopes" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:alpha-input-slopes">[eq:alpha-input-slopes]</a> without a factor depending on the number of active children.

<a id="eq:alpha-component-bounds"></a>
The component bounds are $$\label{eq:alpha-component-bounds}
\begin{aligned}
 |g_i^\alpha(\alpha_i;B)|
   &\le C\ell(S\alpha_i+B_i) &&(i\text{ regular}),\\
 |g_o^\alpha(\alpha_o;B)|
   &\le C\ell\left(S(1-\alpha_o)+B_{\rm side}+uS\right),
   & B_{\rm side}&=\sum_{i\ne o}B_i.
\end{aligned}$$ For the second bound, let $L=\log(1/\alpha_o)$, allowing $L=\infty$. The change of variables in <a href="#eq:alpha-primitive" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:alpha-primitive">[eq:alpha-primitive]</a> gives $$g_o^\alpha(\alpha_o;B)
  =-C_\alpha\ell\int_0^L
       \frac{S(e^{-y}-1)+B_{\rm side}-\eta_o B_o}{F_o(y)}\,dy.$$ For $0\le y\le L$, the absolute numerator is at most $S(1-\alpha_o)+B_{\rm side}+\eta_o S$. Since $\eta_o=c_tu/\ell$, Lemma <a href="#lem:denominators" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:denominators">11</a> proves the bound. The base point $1$ is responsible for this cancellation; it permits parameter changes to be charged to side sizes even when the dominant size is much larger.

<a id="stmt-lem-alpha-tracker"></a>
<div id="lem:alpha-tracker" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:alpha-update-payment"></a>
**Lemma 12** (Simplex update). *Choose $C_\alpha$ sufficiently large after fixing $c_t$. For any old vector $\alpha^0$ in the same simplex, a global minimizer <a id="eq:alpha-minimization"></a>$$\label{eq:alpha-minimization}
 \alpha^1\in\mathop{\mathrm{arg\,min}}_\alpha
       \left\{G^\alpha(\alpha;B)+S\|\alpha-\alpha^0\|_1\right\}$$ satisfies <a href="#eq:alpha-feasibility" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:alpha-feasibility">[eq:alpha-feasibility]</a> and $$\label{eq:alpha-update-payment}
 S\|\alpha^1-\alpha^0\|_1
       \le G^\alpha(\alpha^0;B)-G^\alpha(\alpha^1;B).$$ The same assertions hold with the automatic singleton rule.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-alpha-tracker"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-alpha-tracker</summary>

*Proof.* Continuity and compactness give a global minimizer, and comparison with $\alpha^0$ gives <a href="#eq:alpha-update-payment" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:alpha-update-payment">[eq:alpha-update-payment]</a>. If $S=0$, then every $B_i=0$, the potential vanishes identically, and feasibility is automatic. We may retain $\alpha^0$. Suppose henceforth that $S>0$ and the domain is nonsingleton. For a positive coordinate write $$\gamma_i=\frac{C_\alpha\ell}{F_i(\log(1/\alpha_i))}
                  \left(S-\frac{(1+\eta_i)B_i}{\alpha_i}\right)$$ for the derivative of its primitive. If $\alpha_i=0$ and $B_i>0$, the inward derivative is $-\infty$: the negative term has size proportional to $1/(sF_i(\log(1/s)))$, which tends to infinity as $s\downarrow0$.

<a id="eq:alpha-derivative-contradiction"></a>
There must be a coordinate with derivative less than $-2S$, with this convention at zero. Otherwise all zero coordinates have $B_i=0$, and the inequalities $\gamma_i\ge-2S$ give $$\label{eq:alpha-derivative-contradiction}
 1\le\sum_{\alpha_i>0}\frac{\alpha_i}{1+\eta_i}
       \left(1+\frac{2F_i(\log(1/\alpha_i))}{C_\alpha\ell}\right).$$ There is a fixed bound $\eta_i\le\eta_{\max}$, so, with $T_\alpha=\sum_i\alpha_i h_i$, $$\sum_i\frac{\alpha_i}{1+\eta_i}
    \le1-\frac{c_t}{1+\eta_{\max}}\frac{T_\alpha}{\ell}.$$ The extra sum in <a href="#eq:alpha-derivative-contradiction" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:alpha-derivative-contradiction">[eq:alpha-derivative-contradiction]</a> is at most $CT_\alpha/(C_\alpha\ell)$ by <a href="#eq:denominator-estimates" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:denominator-estimates">[eq:denominator-estimates]</a>. Choose $C_\alpha$ so this is less than half the displayed deficit. Since $T_\alpha>0$ on every nonsingleton domain, this is a contradiction, however small $u$ and $T_\alpha$ may be.

If a coordinate violates <a href="#eq:alpha-feasibility" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:alpha-feasibility">[eq:alpha-feasibility]</a>, it is positive and has strictly positive primitive derivative. Transfer a small amount $\varepsilon$ from it to a coordinate whose derivative is below $-2S$. These coordinates are distinct and the transfer stays in the simplex. The primitive sum decreases at a rate greater than $2S$, whereas the movement penalty can increase by at most $2S\varepsilon$. At a zero receiving coordinate the one-sided derivative $-\infty$ gives the same strict improvement for sufficiently small $\varepsilon$. This contradicts global minimality and proves feasibility. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-23"></a>
#### The side tracker

<a id="eq:side-feasibility"></a>
<a id="eq:side-slack"></a>
When a dominant child has sides, choose a sufficiently small absolute $c_w>0$ and define $$\theta_i=\frac{c_w h_i}{\ell},\qquad
 z_i=\frac{a_i}{Ah_i}\quad(i\text{ a side}).$$ Both numbers are positive. Fix an absolute $C_w$ larger than $2\sup_i(\beta^++\theta_i)$, with an additional positive margin. The supremum is bounded absolutely by <a href="#eq:proportion-parameter-facts" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:proportion-parameter-facts">[eq:proportion-parameter-facts]</a> and the fixed parameter interval. The side domain is $w_i\ge0$, $\sum_iw_i\le C_w$. We require $$\label{eq:side-feasibility}
\begin{aligned}
 Dw_i&\le(\beta^++\theta_i)B_i&&\text{always},\\
 Dw_i&\ge\beta^+B_i-\theta_i z_iD&&\text{if }S\le2D.
\end{aligned}$$ The total additive slack has the useful bound $$\label{eq:side-slack}
 \sum_{i\text{ side}}\theta_i z_i
      =\frac{c_w}{\ell}\sum_{i\text{ side}}\frac{a_i}{A}
      \le \frac{Cc_wu}{\ell}.$$ With no sides, $w$ is the zero vector and all these statements use empty sums. When no dominant child is marked, set $D=0$ for the sole purpose of writing the side movement term.

<a id="eq:side-primitive"></a>
<a id="eq:side-potential-bounds"></a>
For fixed side parameters define $$\label{eq:side-primitive}
\begin{aligned}
 g_i^w(w_i;B_i,D)
  &=C'_w\int_0^{w_i}
       \frac{Ds-(\beta^++\theta_i/2)B_i+D\theta_i z_i/2}
            {\theta_i(s+z_i)}\,ds,\\
 G^w(w;B,D)&=\sum_{i\text{ side}}g_i^w(w_i;B_i,D).
\end{aligned}$$ These potentials are continuous and affine in $(B,D)$. For explicit bounds, set $L_i=\log(1+w_i/z_i)$. Integration gives <a id="eq:side-primitive-explicit"></a>$$\label{eq:side-primitive-explicit}
 g_i^w=C'_w\left\{
   \frac{D}{\theta_i}(w_i-z_iL_i)
   +\frac{Dz_iL_i}{2}
   -\frac{(\beta^++\theta_i/2)B_iL_i}{\theta_i}
                    \right\}.$$ Since $a_i/A<1$ for a side, $h_i=1+\log(A/a_i)$, and therefore $$\log(1+C_w/z_i)
   =\log\left(1+C_wh_i e^{h_i-1}\right)\le Ch_i.$$ Also $0\le z_iL_i\le w_i$ and $0\le w_i-z_iL_i\le w_i$. It follows that $$\label{eq:side-potential-bounds}
\begin{gathered}
 |g_i^w(w_i;B_i,D)|\le C\ell(Dw_i+B_i),\\
 |G^w(w;B,D)-G^w(w;\widetilde B,\widetilde D)|
   \le C\ell\left(\|B-\widetilde B\|_1+|D-\widetilde D|\right).
\end{gathered}$$ In the second line the coefficient of $D$ is bounded by $C\ell\sum_iw_i\le C\ell$, and the coefficient of each $B_i$ is bounded by $C\ell$. Thus no factor counts the sides.

<a id="stmt-lem-side-tracker"></a>
<div id="lem:side-tracker" class="lemma">

**Lemma 13** (Side update). *Choose the absolute multiplier $C'_w$ sufficiently large after fixing the small constants. For every old vector $w^0$ in the side domain, a global minimizer <a id="eq:side-minimization"></a>$$\label{eq:side-minimization}
 w^1\in\mathop{\mathrm{arg\,min}}_w\left\{G^w(w;B,D)+D\|w-w^0\|_1\right\}$$ satisfies <a href="#eq:side-feasibility" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:side-feasibility">[eq:side-feasibility]</a>, and <a id="eq:side-update-payment"></a>$$\label{eq:side-update-payment}
 D\|w^1-w^0\|_1\le G^w(w^0;B,D)-G^w(w^1;B,D).$$ This holds for arbitrary nonnegative $B,D$ with $S=\sum_iB_i$, without a requirement that they arise from ranks.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-side-tracker"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-side-tracker</summary>

*Proof.* Compactness gives existence and comparison with $w^0$ gives the payment. Suppose first that $D>0$, and abbreviate $b=\beta^+$, $\theta=\theta_i$, $z=z_i$, and $s=w_i$. At an upper violation, $B_i<Ds/(b+\theta)$. The numerator in <a href="#eq:side-primitive" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:side-primitive">[eq:side-primitive]</a> is consequently greater than $$\frac{D\theta}{2}\left(\frac{s}{b+\theta}+z\right).$$ Because $b+\theta$ has an absolute upper bound, a large $C'_w$ makes the primitive derivative greater than $2D$. The violating coordinate is positive and can be decreased while remaining in the side domain. The penalty changes by at most $D$ times this decrease, a contradiction to minimality.

Having proved upper feasibility, assume $S\le2D$. Then $$\sum_iw_i\le \max_i(b+\theta_i)\frac{\sum_{i\text{ side}}B_i}{D}
           \le2\max_i(b+\theta_i)<C_w.$$ Thus every coordinate has room for a small increase. At a lower violation, $B_i>D(s+\theta z)/b$. Substitution makes the same numerator strictly less than $$-\frac{D\theta}{2}\left(\frac{s}{b}+z\right);$$ The derivative is therefore less than $-2D$ when $C'_w$ is large. Increasing this coordinate strictly improves the objective, again a contradiction. This proves the lower inequality.

If $D=0$, the upper inequality is automatic. The lower inequality is required only when $S=0$, in which case every $B_i=0$ and it too is automatic. The potential need not vanish when $D=0<S$, but minimization and its payment remain valid. If both $D=S=0$, the potential vanishes, and retaining the old side vector is allowed. The empty side domain consists of its unique zero vector. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-24"></a>
#### Adaptive parameters and domains

The preceding updates will run at every step on which they are defined, regardless of which output rule is subsequently used. We now justify their total movement under all the changes from Section <a href="#sec:allocation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:allocation">3</a>.

We use explicitly the following coarse charges, whose expected total is at most $CE_v+D_v$ by <a href="#eq:coarse-data-budget" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:coarse-data-budget">[eq:coarse-data-budget]</a>: the number of changed core flags; the sum of old and new $a_i$ at changed size coordinates; the old and new total active sizes at wholesale refreshes; the old and new side totals at changes of $U$; and $|\Delta D|$ off wholesale steps. Here $D_v$ is finite and may depend on the fixed star and $k$, but is independent of the input law and horizon. Absolute constants may absorb the fixed coarse tolerances. The finely tracked caps $b_i$ are not arguments of either proportion potential.

<a id="stmt-lem-adaptive-proportions"></a>
<div id="lem:adaptive-proportions" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:proportion-movement"></a>
**Lemma 14** (Adaptive proportion movement). *There is a causal update schedule satisfying <a href="#eq:alpha-feasibility" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:alpha-feasibility">[eq:alpha-feasibility]</a> and <a href="#eq:side-feasibility" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:side-feasibility">[eq:side-feasibility]</a> after every step, such that $$\label{eq:proportion-movement}
 \mathbb E\sum_{t\text{ not wholesale}}
   \left(S_t\|\alpha_t-\alpha_{t-1}\|_1
        +D_t\|w_t-w_{t-1}\|_1\right)
       \le C\ell E_v+D'_v.$$ Vectors are extended by zero to the fixed child set. The side term is zero without a dominant child. The simplex term includes the automatic singleton state; an empty active domain contributes zero. The finite constant $D'_v$ is uniform in the law and horizon.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-adaptive-proportions"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-adaptive-proportions</summary>

*Proof.* We give the schedule and its entire accounting. All comparisons below are made on a finite set of child and rank indices.

<a id="pm-h-25"></a>
###### Filtering and drift precede every update decision.

At the start of step $t$, hold the old proportion states, old parameters, old $D$, and old core flags. First evaluate the potentials with the filtered old hidden counts, and then with the new hidden counts under the new conditioning, still using those old flags and parameters. With these quantities held, each potential is affine in the rank probabilities: for $G^w$, its term linear in the held $D$ is simply constant on these two legs. The signed expected filtering change is therefore zero. By <a href="#eq:alpha-input-slopes" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:alpha-input-slopes">[eq:alpha-input-slopes]</a> and <a href="#eq:side-potential-bounds" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:side-potential-bounds">[eq:side-potential-bounds]</a>, the absolute drift change is at most $C\ell$ times the sum of absolute child-rank drifts. Summed over all steps, its expectation is at most $C\ell E_v$. These statements concern all steps before selecting any refresh or domain-change events.

Denote the input at the end of this evaluation by $\overline B$: it uses the final rank probabilities and the old flags. Thus $\overline B_i\le n_i^{\rm old}$ even if filtering made the current ranks very different. We now compute the new flags, sizes, and all other held parameters by the rules already given.

<a id="pm-h-26"></a>
###### Wholesale steps.

At a wholesale step reinitialize the proportion domains, choose any vector in each new compact domain, and apply the appropriate minimizing updates; use the automatic rules for empty or singleton domains. The absolute old potential, evaluated at $\overline B$ and old $D$, is at most $C\ell$ times the old total active size. To see this for $G^\alpha$, sum <a href="#eq:alpha-component-bounds" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:alpha-component-bounds">[eq:alpha-component-bounds]</a> and use $\sum_i\overline B_i\le\sum_i n_i^{\rm old}\le
C\sum_i a_i^{\rm old}$. For $G^w$, use <a href="#eq:side-potential-bounds" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:side-potential-bounds">[eq:side-potential-bounds]</a>, $\|w\|_1\le C_w$, and $D^{\rm old}\le C\sum_i a_i^{\rm old}$. The new potential is bounded by the corresponding new total. Thus the entire potential jump at this step is bounded by its coarse wholesale charge times $C\ell$. No movement at this step is included in <a href="#eq:proportion-movement" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:proportion-movement">[eq:proportion-movement]</a>.

<a id="pm-h-27"></a>
###### Preparing a non-wholesale domain change.

<a id="eq:synthetic-core-errors"></a>
On a non-wholesale step $A$, $\beta^+$, and the presence and identity of the dominant mark are fixed. Empty/nonempty active transitions are wholesale, and a marked dominant coordinate cannot disappear within its epoch. Let $I^-$ and $I^+$ be the old and new active domains. Define the synthetic input on the fixed child set by <a id="eq:synthetic-core-input"></a>$$\label{eq:synthetic-core-input}
 B_i^*=\begin{cases}B_i^{\rm new},&i\in I^-\cap I^+,\\
                     0,&\text{otherwise},\end{cases}
 \qquad S^*=\sum_iB_i^*.$$ If $K_t$ is the number of changed core flags, then $$\label{eq:synthetic-core-errors}
 \|\overline B-B^*\|_1\le K_t,
 \qquad \|B^{\rm new}-B^*\|_1\le K_t,
 \qquad 0\le S^{\rm new}-S^*\le K_t.$$ For common indices, the first discrepancy is just the sum over changed flags, and each rank probability lies in $[0,1]$. For an index leaving the active domain, all its new flags are zero, so every old flag there is changed. For an entering index, all its old flags were zero, so every new flag there is changed. These observations prove all three bounds.

Replace $\overline B$ by $B^*$ and old $D$ by $D^{\rm new}$, holding the old domains and their other parameters. The input slope bounds charge this potential change to $C\ell(K_t+|D^{\rm new}-D^{\rm old}|)$. Run the minimizing updates on the old domains with inputs $(B^*,S^*,D^{\rm new})$. Denote the resulting vectors by $\alpha^1,w^1$. Lemmas <a href="#lem:alpha-tracker" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:alpha-tracker">12</a> and <a href="#lem:side-tracker" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:side-tracker">13</a> apply because their feasibility proofs use only nonnegativity of these inputs and the held parameter bounds. They pay <a id="eq:prepared-movement"></a>$$\label{eq:prepared-movement}
 S^*\|\alpha^1-\alpha^{\rm old}\|_1,
 \qquad D^{\rm new}\|w^1-w^{\rm old}\|_1$$ by decreases of the respective potentials.

Next delete departing coordinates and insert zero coordinates at entering indices. If $S^*>0$, a departing coordinate has $B_i^*=0$, so <a href="#eq:alpha-feasibility" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:alpha-feasibility">[eq:alpha-feasibility]</a> forces $\alpha_i^1=0$. Deletion and zero insertion then preserve the simplex and cause no movement. If $S^*=0$, every $B_i^*=0$, the alpha potential is zero, and we may redistribute the simplex mass to any vector in the new nonempty domain at zero weighted cost. If $D^{\rm new}>0$, the upper inequality in <a href="#eq:side-feasibility" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:side-feasibility">[eq:side-feasibility]</a> forces $w_i^1=0$ at each departing side. If $D^{\rm new}=0$, deletion of side coordinates has zero weighted cost, and their primitive components vanish because $B_i^*=D^{\rm new}=0$ there. Inserting a coordinate at zero always gives zero component potential. Write $\alpha^2,w^2$ for the vectors on the new domains after this operation.

<a id="pm-h-28"></a>
###### Changing the held parameters.

<a id="eq:prepared-coordinate-bounds"></a>
Continue to use $B^*$ and $D^{\rm new}$. At common regular coordinates the prepared state satisfies $$\label{eq:prepared-coordinate-bounds}
 S^*\alpha_i^2\le CB_i^*,\qquad
 D^{\rm new}w_i^2\le CB_i^*\quad(i\text{ a side}).$$ The assertions also hold at inserted zero coordinates, and at $S^*=0$ or $D^{\rm new}=0$ with the evident zero left side. The regular parameters $h_i,\eta_i,F_i$ and, on sides, $\theta_i,z_i$ change only if $a_i$ changes. Bound the absolute old and new component values using <a href="#eq:alpha-component-bounds" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:alpha-component-bounds">[eq:alpha-component-bounds]</a>, <a href="#eq:side-potential-bounds" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:side-potential-bounds">[eq:side-potential-bounds]</a>, and <a href="#eq:prepared-coordinate-bounds" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:prepared-coordinate-bounds">[eq:prepared-coordinate-bounds]</a>. Their jump is at most $C\ell B_i^*\le C\ell n_i^{\rm new}\le C\ell a_i^{\rm new}$, which is paid by the old-plus-new size charge at that coordinate. Departing and inserted components have already been seen to give zero extra jump.

For a change in $u$, the prepared alpha vector satisfies $$S^*(1-\alpha_o^2)\le C B_{\rm side}^*,\qquad
 B_{\rm side}^*\le C\sum_{i\text{ new side}}a_i^{\rm new},
 \qquad S^*\le C A.$$ The first inequality follows by summing prepared side feasibility when $S^*>0$ and is automatic when $S^*=0$; the last uses the new core-size bound and the epoch comparison of total size with $A$. Applying the dominant component bound on both sides of the parameter change shows that its jump is at most $$C\ell\left(B_{\rm side}^*+
                  (u^{\rm old}+u^{\rm new})S^*\right)
 \le C\ell\left(U^{\rm old}+U^{\rm new}\right).$$ This is a side-total charge, not a dominant-size charge. It includes the appearance of the first side or disappearance of the last side. To spell out these endpoints, on a singleton domain the potential is zero. If $S^*>0$, preparation forces all mass onto $o$ when the common side input is zero, and the dominant primitive at $\alpha_o=1$ is zero. If $S^*=0$, the whole alpha potential is zero. Thus assigning the singleton potential to be zero is compatible with the estimates on both sides of either transition. The side vector on an empty side domain is its unique zero vector.

<a id="pm-h-29"></a>
###### The final update and its movement weight.

With the new domains and parameters now installed, replace $B^*$ by $B^{\rm new}$, paying at most $C\ell K_t$ by <a href="#eq:synthetic-core-errors" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:synthetic-core-errors">[eq:synthetic-core-errors]</a> and the input slope bounds. Run the two minimizing updates once more, now starting at $\alpha^2,w^2$ and using the final inputs. This pays $S^{\rm new}\|\alpha^{\rm new}-\alpha^2\|_1$ and $D^{\rm new}\|w^{\rm new}-w^2\|_1$.

<a id="eq:adaptive-weight-comparison"></a>
The triangle inequality converts these payments to the differences between the final and old states. The alpha vectors at all these substeps have norm one, hence any two are at distance at most two. The domain-transition movement has weight zero at $S^*$. Writing $\delta=S^{\rm new}-S^*\ge0$, we obtain $$\label{eq:adaptive-weight-comparison}
\begin{aligned}
 S^{\rm new}\|\alpha^{\rm new}-\alpha^{\rm old}\|_1
 &\le S^*\|\alpha^1-\alpha^{\rm old}\|_1
      +S^{\rm new}\|\alpha^{\rm new}-\alpha^2\|_1+4\delta,\\
 D^{\rm new}\|w^{\rm new}-w^{\rm old}\|_1
 &\le D^{\rm new}\|w^1-w^{\rm old}\|_1
      +D^{\rm new}\|w^{\rm new}-w^2\|_1.
\end{aligned}$$ The first error is at most $4K_t$. The second inequality needs no change of weight, since both side updates used $D^{\rm new}$. These statements also cover zero weights and singleton states.

<a id="pm-h-30"></a>
###### Telescoping and uniformity.

Let $\mathcal G_t=G_t^\alpha+G_t^w$ after the final update. On non-wholesale steps the sum of the paid minimizing movements is at most $\mathcal G_{t-1}-\mathcal G_t$, plus the signed old-parameter filtering/drift change and the parameter/input charges just listed. On wholesale steps the same inequality holds with no movement term and with the wholesale charge. Summing over *all* steps retains the zero expected filtering sum; it does not condition that assertion on an adaptively selected subset of steps. Expected drift costs at most $C\ell E_v$, and all remaining changes cost at most $C\ell$ times the coarse charges. Equation <a href="#eq:adaptive-weight-comparison" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:adaptive-weight-comparison">[eq:adaptive-weight-comparison]</a> therefore gives <a href="#eq:proportion-movement" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:proportion-movement">[eq:proportion-movement]</a>, apart from the initial and terminal potential magnitudes.

Those magnitudes have a uniform finite bound. At a valid state, $\sum_iB_i\le\sum_i n_i\le Ck$, $D\le Ck$, $\|\alpha\|_1\le1$, and $\|w\|_1\le C_w$. The component bounds give $|\mathcal G_t|\le C\ell k$, including arbitrarily small positive $u$. Only the fixed collection of rank and parameter trackers contributes the other finite initialization allowances. Their states persist through all epochs and inactive intervals; no new allowance is assigned when a label or coordinate returns. Thus $D'_v$ is independent of the number of steps, histories, refreshes, and domain changes. The schedule uses only current observed posteriors and prior states. Global minimizers can be selected by fixed tie-breaking on the compact finite-dimensional domains, so it is causal. ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:allocation-finish"></a>
### Feasible outputs and completion of the allocation bound

The proportion vectors now satisfy the constraints needed for the two removal rules described in Section <a href="#sec:proportions" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:proportions">4</a>. We define the outputs, choose the absolute constants that preserve every child’s lower bound, and prove their movement estimate. A hysteresis rule will keep changes between the two formulas within the same movement budget.

All notation in this section is local to a star with parent $v$, unless a sum over stars is displayed. In particular, $S=\sum_i B_i$, $d_i=n_i+b_i$, and $T=f_o^{\beta_v}$ when a dominant child $o$ exists. Inactive coordinates, including their caps and outputs, are zero. We extend proportion vectors by zero to the fixed child set when comparing two times. A marked child persists throughout its epoch; changes of its identity, and transitions between empty and nonempty active sets, occur only at wholesale steps.

<a id="pm-h-32"></a>
#### The output rules and the choice of constants

<a id="eq:required-holes"></a>
<a id="eq:regime-ranges"></a>
The local data and proportion updates of Sections <a href="#sec:allocation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:allocation">3</a> and <a href="#sec:proportions" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:proportions">4</a> do not use the current parent allocation. Perform all of those updates first. Once $q_v$ has been obtained in the top-down traversal, put $$R_0=\left(\sum_i d_i-q_v\right)_+.
 \label{eq:required-holes}$$ If there is no active child, put every child output equal to zero. Otherwise there are two possible rules: <a id="eq:output-rule-one"></a><a id="eq:output-rule-two"></a>$$\begin{aligned}
 \text{I:}\qquad &q_i=d_i-R_0\alpha_i\quad\text{for every active }i;
 \label{eq:output-rule-one}\\
 \text{II:}\qquad &q_i=d_i-Dw_i\quad(i\ne o\text{ active}),
 &q_o=\min\left\{d_o,q_v-\sum_{i\ne o}q_i\right\}.
 \label{eq:output-rule-two}
\end{aligned}$$ Rule II is defined only when a dominant child exists. With no dominant child we always use Rule I. With a dominant child, the allowed ranges are $$\text{I requires }S\ge D/2,
 \qquad
 \text{II requires }S\le 2D.
 \label{eq:regime-ranges}$$ At initialization and after a wholesale refresh, choose either rule in its allowed range, with a fixed tie rule. On other steps retain the old rule unless it leaves its allowed range. Thus an I-to-II switch occurs only at $S<D/2$, and an II-to-I switch only at $S>2D$. The ranges cover all $S,D\ge0$, including $S=D=0$.

<a id="eq:dominant-output-slack"></a>
We specify the dependencies among the absolute constants before verifying these formulas. The rank functions, coarse size tolerances, and parameter gaps give fixed constants $$3\le\beta_v\le\beta_{\max},\qquad
 0\le h_i/\ell\le H_0,\qquad
 c_DT\le D\le C_DT,$$ where the last comparison includes the assertion that $D$ and $T$ vanish together.
