Write $c_g$ for the constant in <a href="#eq:parameter-gap" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:parameter-gap">[eq:parameter-gap]</a>. At noncore ranks the parameter derivative is bounded below by a fixed positive multiple of the rank function. Consequently there is an absolute $c_s>0$ such that, for a dominant child, $$L_o^{\beta_v}-L_o^{\beta_o}
   \ge c_s\frac{u}{\ell}T.
 \label{eq:dominant-output-slack}$$ Indeed the core contribution to this difference is $(\beta_o-\beta_v)B_o\ge0$. Affinity in the parameter and the derivative comparison give $f_o^{\beta_v}-f_o^{\beta_o}
\ge c_f(\beta_o-\beta_v)f_o^{\beta_v}$, and the gap is at least $c_g u/\ell$. This proof also covers $T=0$ and $u=0$.

<a id="eq:output-constant-smallness"></a>
<a id="eq:side-parameter-room"></a>
Let $C_z$ be an absolute constant for which <a href="#eq:side-slack" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:side-slack">[eq:side-slack]</a> reads $\sum_{i\ne o}\theta_i z_i\le C_z c_wu/\ell$. Choose $c_t,c_w,\epsilon_b>0$ so small that $$\begin{gathered}
 c_tH_0\le1,\qquad \beta_{\max}c_t\le c_g/4,
 \qquad c_wH_0\le1,\qquad c_w\le c_g/4,
 \label{eq:output-constant-smallness}\\
 C_zc_wC_D\le c_s/2,
 \qquad 4\epsilon_b/c_D\le c_g/4.
 \nonumber
\end{gathered}$$ These choices involve only previously fixed absolute constants. Next choose the cap margin $\lambda_b$, the parameter error $\epsilon$, and the fine tracking tolerance $\rho$ as in Lemma <a href="#lem:caps" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:caps">10</a>, decreasing $\epsilon$ further so that $\epsilon\le c_g/4$. It follows that every side satisfies $$\beta^++\theta_i
 \le\beta_v+\frac{\epsilon+c_wh_i}{\ell}
 \le\beta_v+\frac{c_gh_i}{2\ell}
 \le\beta_i.
 \label{eq:side-parameter-room}$$ Here $h_i\ge1$ on regular coordinates. The cap proof permits arbitrarily small final choices of $\epsilon,\rho$ after $\lambda_b$ is fixed. The bounded-domain constant $C_w$ and the potential multipliers in Section <a href="#sec:proportions" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:proportions">4</a> may now be taken sufficiently large. For instance the required bound on $C_w$ uses only $\beta^++\theta_i\le\beta_{\max}+1$. This order fixes every local constant independently of $k$, the degree, the depth, and the separation ratio $\tau$. We choose $\tau$ only after proving the local movement estimate.

<a id="stmt-lem-output-feasibility"></a>
<div id="lem:output-feasibility" class="lemma">

**Lemma 15** (Feasibility of the outputs). *Suppose $q_v\ge L_v^{\beta_v}$. The selected output rule satisfies $$L_i^{\beta_i}\le q_i\le d_i\le C M_i\le C m_i,
 \qquad \sum_iq_i\le q_v,$$ with an absolute constant $C$. In particular every child output is nonnegative. The conclusions include empty active sets, empty side sets, and vanishing values of $S,D,T$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-output-feasibility"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-output-feasibility</summary>

<a id="eq:parent-lower-for-output"></a>
<a id="eq:required-hole-bound"></a>
*Proof.* Superadditivity at the single parameter $\beta_v$ gives $$q_v\ge L_v^{\beta_v}\ge\sum_iL_i^{\beta_v}.
 \label{eq:parent-lower-for-output}$$ Inactive children have no core ranks and no positive lower-bound terms, so their required lower bounds and their outputs are both zero. For active children, expand $L_i^{\beta_v}=n_i-\beta_vB_i+f_i^{\beta_v}$. Set $e_o=b_o-T$ when a dominant child exists, and $e_o=0$ otherwise. Lemma <a href="#lem:caps" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:caps">10</a> implies $$0\le e_o\le\epsilon_bT/\ell,
 \qquad R_0\le\beta_vS+e_o.
 \label{eq:required-hole-bound}$$ To obtain the second inequality, subtract <a href="#eq:parent-lower-for-output" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:parent-lower-for-output">[eq:parent-lower-for-output]</a> from $\sum_i d_i$: regular flex caps contribute nonpositive errors, and only the dominant cap can contribute the error $e_o$.

<a id="eq:rule-one-flex-comparison"></a>
First consider Rule I with $S>0$. If there is a dominant child, <a href="#eq:regime-ranges" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:regime-ranges">[eq:regime-ranges]</a> and $D\ge c_DT$ give $$T\le 2S/c_D,
 \qquad e_o/S\le 2\epsilon_b/(c_D\ell).
 \label{eq:rule-one-flex-comparison}$$ The same bound on the error holds with error zero when there is no dominant child. At a regular child, <a href="#eq:alpha-feasibility" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:alpha-feasibility">[eq:alpha-feasibility]</a> and <a href="#eq:required-hole-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:required-hole-bound">[eq:required-hole-bound]</a> yield $$R_0\alpha_i
 \le(\beta_v+e_o/S)(1+\eta_i)B_i.$$ By $\eta_i=c_th_i/\ell$, $\eta_i\le1$, and <a href="#eq:output-constant-smallness" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:output-constant-smallness">[eq:output-constant-smallness]</a>, $$\begin{aligned}
 (\beta_v+e_o/S)(1+\eta_i)-\beta_v
 &\le \beta_{\max}c_t h_i/\ell
       +4\epsilon_b/(c_D\ell)\\
 &\le c_gh_i/(2\ell)
 \le\beta_i-\beta_v.
\end{aligned}$$ Thus $R_0\alpha_i\le\beta_iB_i$, and the lower cap bound gives $q_i\ge n_i-\beta_iB_i+f_i^{\beta_i}=L_i^{\beta_i}$. For the dominant coordinate use $\alpha_o\le1$ on the error term: $$R_0\alpha_o
 \le\beta_v(1+\eta_o)B_o+e_o
 \le\beta_oB_o+e_o.$$ Here $h_o=u$, so the same small choice of $c_t$ works even for arbitrarily small positive $u$, and gives equality in the relevant error when $u=0$. Since $b_o-e_o=T\ge f_o^{\beta_o}$, this proves the dominant lower bound as well.

If $S=0$ in Rule I, the presence of a dominant child forces $D=0$ by its allowed range, and therefore $T=e_o=0$. Without a dominant child the error is zero by definition. In either case <a href="#eq:required-hole-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:required-hole-bound">[eq:required-hole-bound]</a> gives $R_0=0$, so no division by $S$ is used: the outputs are their caps. At regular children the cap lower bound suffices, and at the dominant child $B_o=T=0$ gives the claim. For any nonempty active set, the simplex identity gives $$\sum_iq_i=\sum_i d_i-R_0
          =\min\left\{\sum_i d_i,q_v\right\}\le q_v.$$ Every hole is nonnegative, so $q_i\le d_i$; the proved lower bound ensures nonnegativity.

Now consider Rule II. Its side holes satisfy, by <a href="#eq:side-feasibility" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:side-feasibility">[eq:side-feasibility]</a> and <a href="#eq:side-parameter-room" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:side-parameter-room">[eq:side-parameter-room]</a>, $$0\le Dw_i\le(\beta^++\theta_i)B_i\le\beta_iB_i.$$ The side lower bounds follow from $b_i\ge f_i^{\beta_i}$, exactly by subtracting these holes from $n_i+b_i$. Write $q_o^{\mathrm{rem}}=q_v-\sum_{i\ne o}(d_i-Dw_i)$ for the uncapped remaining amount. The upper flex caps on the sides and <a href="#eq:parent-lower-for-output" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:parent-lower-for-output">[eq:parent-lower-for-output]</a> imply $$\begin{aligned}
 q_o^{\mathrm{rem}}
 &\ge L_o^{\beta_v}
        +\sum_{i\ne o}(-\beta_vB_i+Dw_i)\\
 &\ge L_o^{\beta_v}-D\sum_{i\ne o}\theta_i z_i\\
 &\ge L_o^{\beta_v}-C_zc_wC_D\frac{uT}{\ell}
 \ge L_o^{\beta_o}.
\end{aligned}$$ The second line uses the lower inequality of <a href="#eq:side-feasibility" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:side-feasibility">[eq:side-feasibility]</a>, which applies because Rule II has $S\le2D$, together with $\beta^+\ge\beta_v$. The last line uses <a href="#eq:dominant-output-slack" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:dominant-output-slack">[eq:dominant-output-slack]</a> and <a href="#eq:output-constant-smallness" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:output-constant-smallness">[eq:output-constant-smallness]</a>. Independently, $d_o=n_o+b_o\ge n_o+f_o^{\beta_o}\ge L_o^{\beta_o}$. Taking the smaller of these two admissible dominant amounts preserves its lower bound. It also gives the parent budget and the upper cap.

For completeness, when $D=0$ in Rule II its range forces $S=0$, and the comparison with $T$ forces $T=0$. All side holes vanish and <a href="#eq:required-hole-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:required-hole-bound">[eq:required-hole-bound]</a> gives $R_0=0$; thus the dominant cap also fits within the remaining budget. When there are no sides, $u=0$, the side sums are empty, and both rules simply give $q_o=\min\{d_o,q_v\}$. No side denominator or proportion is needed in this case. An empty active set was already assigned zero outputs. Finally the cap estimate $d_i\le CM_i$, together with $M_i\le m_i$, proves the claimed domination in every case. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-33"></a>
#### Paying for changes of rule

<a id="eq:switch-budget"></a>
We next bound the cost of the hysteresis rule without assuming that the absolute variation of the posterior sum $S$ is small. Fix a finite horizon and let $\mathcal W_v$ be the wholesale steps for this star. The coarse charge $C_{v,t}$ in <a href="#eq:coarse-data-budget" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:coarse-data-budget">[eq:coarse-data-budget]</a> pays core-flag changes, the variation of $D$, and, on wholesale steps, the old plus new active size totals. All of its coefficients are absolute. Define the switch charge <a id="eq:switch-charge"></a>$$J_{v,t}=
 \begin{cases}
 D_t,&t\notin\mathcal W_v\text{ is an I-to-II switch},\\
 S_t,&t\notin\mathcal W_v\text{ is an II-to-I switch},\\
 0,&\text{otherwise}.
 \end{cases}
 \label{eq:switch-charge}$$ There is a fixed finite constant $D_v^{\mathrm{sw}}$, independent of the hidden-process law and horizon, for which $$\mathbb E\sum_tJ_{v,t}\le C E_v+D_v^{\mathrm{sw}}.
 \label{eq:switch-budget}$$

Here is the full potential accounting for this claim. At the end of a step use <a id="eq:switch-potential"></a>$$\Psi_v=
 \begin{cases}
 D,&\text{a dominant child exists and the rule is I},\\
 S,&\text{a dominant child exists and the rule is II},\\
 0,&\text{no dominant child exists}.
 \end{cases}
 \label{eq:switch-potential}$$ Start every step by holding the old rule, mark, flags, and $D$ fixed. On this frozen state, the filtering change of $\Psi_v$ is either zero or the filtering change of the sum of the old flagged rank posteriors. Write $F_{v,t}$ for this signed change. The tower property gives <a id="eq:switch-filtering-zero"></a>$$\mathbb E[F_{v,t}\mid\mathcal F_{t-1}]=0.
 \label{eq:switch-filtering-zero}$$ On the subsequent drift leg, still with those old data fixed, the increase is at most the sum of the absolute child-rank drifts at that step. Denote this nonnegative sum by $e_{v,t}$; it has $\mathbb E\sum_t e_{v,t}\le E_v$.

On a non-wholesale step, changing the core flags changes the old-rule potential by at most the number of changed flags, since each current rank posterior lies in $[0,1]$. Updating $D$ changes it by at most $|\Delta D|$. The other local updates leave this potential unchanged. With the new inputs now in place, an I-to-II switch changes the potential from $D_t$ to $S_t<D_t/2$, a drop of at least $D_t/2$. An II-to-I switch changes it from $S_t$ to $D_t<S_t/2$, a drop of at least $S_t/2$.

On a wholesale step we do not claim switch credit. The old frozen value of $S$, even after filtering and drift, is at most the old number of core flags, and that number is bounded by a constant times the old active size total. The old held $D$ has the same size bound. The newly chosen potential is likewise bounded by a constant times the new active size total. Thus the entire reset of this potential costs at most $C C_{v,t}$. We obtain on every step the single inequality <a id="eq:switch-one-step"></a>$$\Psi_v(t)-\Psi_v(t-1)
 \le F_{v,t}+e_{v,t}+C C_{v,t}-\tfrac12 J_{v,t}.
 \label{eq:switch-one-step}$$ Importantly, $F_{v,t}$ is included on *every* step before the wholesale and switch tests; it is never multiplied by the indicator of an event chosen from the new posteriors. Taking expectations, summing, and using $\Psi_v(t)\ge0$ gives <a href="#eq:switch-budget" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:switch-budget">[eq:switch-budget]</a> from <a href="#eq:coarse-data-budget" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:coarse-data-budget">[eq:coarse-data-budget]</a>. The initial value is at most $Ck$, uniformly over valid states. This proves the asserted independence of law and horizon for this additional constant.

<a id="pm-h-34"></a>
#### The local movement estimate

<a id="stmt-lem-star-movement"></a>
<div id="lem:star-movement" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:star-output-movement"></a>
**Lemma 16** (Movement on a star). *For the outputs above and a fixed star $v$, there is a finite $D_v^{\mathrm{out}}$, depending only on its fixed finite structure and $k$, such that $$\mathbb E\sum_t\sum_{i\text{ child of }v}|q_i(t)-q_i(t-1)|
 \le C_{\mathrm{par}}\mathbb E\sum_t|q_v(t)-q_v(t-1)|
       +C\ell E_v+D_v^{\mathrm{out}}.
 \label{eq:star-output-movement}$$ Here $C_{\mathrm{par}}$ and $C$ are absolute, independent of $k$, degree, depth, and $\tau$. In fact the output estimates below allow $C_{\mathrm{par}}=1$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-star-movement"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-star-movement</summary>

<a id="eq:rule-two-hole-identity"></a>
*Proof.* Compare the vectors of holes $\zeta_i=d_i-q_i$, adding cap variation at the end. By Lemma <a href="#lem:output-feasibility" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:output-feasibility">15</a> these vectors are nonnegative. In Rule I one has <a id="eq:rule-one-hole-identity"></a>$$\zeta=R_0\alpha,
 \qquad \|\zeta\|_1=R_0.
 \label{eq:rule-one-hole-identity}$$ In Rule II let $\omega_i=Dw_i$ on the sides and $W_{\mathrm{side}}=\sum_{i\ne o}\omega_i$. The definition of the dominant output gives the exact identities $$\begin{aligned}
 \zeta_i&=\omega_i\quad(i\ne o),&
 \zeta_o&=(R_0-W_{\mathrm{side}})_+,&
 \|\zeta\|_1&=\max\{R_0,W_{\mathrm{side}}\}.
 \label{eq:rule-two-hole-identity}
\end{aligned}$$ Indeed before replacing the signed excess by its positive part the dominant hole equals $(\sum_i d_i-q_v-W_{\mathrm{side}})_+$, and $W_{\mathrm{side}}\ge0$ allows that replacement. These identities remain valid when $D$, $S$, or the side set vanishes.

Suppress the time index and use superscripts $-,+$ for old and new step-end values. On a non-wholesale step the dominant mark, if any, is the same at both times. First suppose both times use Rule I and the active sets are nonempty. Adding and subtracting $R_0^+\alpha^-$, and using $\|\alpha^-\|_1=1$, gives <a id="eq:hole-movement-one"></a>$$\|\Delta\zeta\|_1
 \le |\Delta R_0|+R_0^+\|\Delta\alpha\|_1
 \le |\Delta R_0|+C S^+\|\Delta\alpha\|_1.
 \label{eq:hole-movement-one}$$ The last bound follows from <a href="#eq:required-hole-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:required-hole-bound">[eq:required-hole-bound]</a> and <a href="#eq:rule-one-flex-comparison" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:rule-one-flex-comparison">[eq:rule-one-flex-comparison]</a>, with the zero case proved in Lemma <a href="#lem:output-feasibility" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:output-feasibility">15</a>. If the active sets are empty at both times the hole change is zero. A change between empty and nonempty is wholesale and is treated below.

If both times use Rule II, the positive-part map is 1-Lipschitz, so <a href="#eq:rule-two-hole-identity" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:rule-two-hole-identity">[eq:rule-two-hole-identity]</a> gives <a id="eq:hole-movement-two"></a>$$\begin{aligned}
 \|\Delta\zeta\|_1
 &\le |\Delta R_0|+2\|\Delta\omega\|_1\\
 &\le |\Delta R_0|+2D^+\|\Delta w\|_1
                      +2C_w|\Delta D|.
 \label{eq:hole-movement-two}
\end{aligned}$$ For the first line, the change in the side-hole total is at most $\|\Delta\omega\|_1$; the other copy is the change of the side vector itself. For the second, use $\Delta(Dw)=D^+\Delta w+(\Delta D)w^-$ and $\|w^-\|_1\le C_w$. Inserting or deleting a side causes no extra term because these vectors have been extended by zero.

At an I-to-II switch, $S^+<D^+/2$, so <a href="#eq:required-hole-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:required-hole-bound">[eq:required-hole-bound]</a> and $T^+\le D^+/c_D$ show $R_0^+\le C D^+$. The new hole total is at most $\max\{R_0^+,C_wD^+\}\le C D^+$. The old total is $R_0^-$, which is at most $R_0^++|\Delta R_0|$. Nonnegativity of the two hole vectors therefore yields <a id="eq:hole-downcross"></a>$$\|\Delta\zeta\|_1\le C D^++|\Delta R_0|.
 \label{eq:hole-downcross}$$ At an II-to-I switch, $S^+>2D^+$, whence $D^+\le S^+/2$ and $R_0^+\le C S^+$. The old total is at most $R_0^-+C_wD^-$, while the new total is $R_0^+$. Using $R_0^-\le R_0^++|\Delta R_0|$ and $D^-\le D^++|\Delta D|$ gives <a id="eq:hole-upcross"></a>$$\|\Delta\zeta\|_1
 \le C S^++C_w|\Delta D|+|\Delta R_0|.
 \label{eq:hole-upcross}$$ These two arguments bound the full change across a switch without requiring the unused rule to have held at the preceding inputs.

<a id="eq:output-pointwise-movement"></a>
Finally, positive part is 1-Lipschitz in <a href="#eq:required-holes" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:required-holes">[eq:required-holes]</a>, and therefore <a id="eq:cap-parent-variation"></a>$$|\Delta R_0|\le\sum_i|\Delta d_i|+|\Delta q_v|,
 \qquad
 \sum_i|\Delta q_i|\le\sum_i|\Delta d_i|+\|\Delta\zeta\|_1.
 \label{eq:cap-parent-variation}$$ Combining all the non-wholesale cases gives one useful pointwise bound, with a single copy of the parent movement: $$\begin{aligned}
 \sum_i|\Delta q_i|
 \le{}& |\Delta q_v|+2\sum_i|\Delta d_i|\nonumber\\
 &+C\left(S^+\|\Delta\alpha\|_1
        +D^+\|\Delta w\|_1+|\Delta D|+J_{v,t}\right).
 \label{eq:output-pointwise-movement}
\end{aligned}$$ Only defined tracker terms are included. In particular they are zero for an empty active set, and the side term is zero without sides or a dominant child.

<a id="eq:wholesale-output-movement"></a>
At a wholesale step use the actual output domination directly: $$\sum_i|\Delta q_i|
 \le\sum_i(q_i^-+q_i^+)
 \le C\left(\sum_i a_i^-+\sum_i a_i^+\right)
 \le C C_{v,t}.
 \label{eq:wholesale-output-movement}$$ There is no per-child or per-epoch initialization charge here; the sum of sizes is exactly the wholesale quantity already paid. Take expectations and sum <a href="#eq:output-pointwise-movement" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:output-pointwise-movement">[eq:output-pointwise-movement]</a> over non-wholesale steps and <a href="#eq:wholesale-output-movement" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:wholesale-output-movement">[eq:wholesale-output-movement]</a> over wholesale steps. The fine cap budget <a href="#eq:fine-cap-budget" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:fine-cap-budget">[eq:fine-cap-budget]</a>, the proportion budget <a href="#eq:proportion-movement" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:proportion-movement">[eq:proportion-movement]</a>, the coarse budget <a href="#eq:coarse-data-budget" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:coarse-data-budget">[eq:coarse-data-budget]</a>, and <a href="#eq:switch-budget" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:switch-budget">[eq:switch-budget]</a> give <a href="#eq:star-output-movement" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:star-output-movement">[eq:star-output-movement]</a>. Cap movement is added once; it is never used as an input to a potential having an $O(\ell)$ input slope. The coefficient of the parent variation in <a href="#eq:output-pointwise-movement" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:output-pointwise-movement">[eq:output-pointwise-movement]</a> is one, which proves the claimed absolute bound on $C_{\mathrm{par}}$. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-35"></a>
#### Uniform initialization and the tree sum

We record why the finite constants just used are uniform over laws and horizons. For a fixed star there are finitely many rank trackers, namely $k$ ranks per child and the ranks used at the parent. Each elementary reference potential is $p(1-p)+(p-p^\circ)^2$ with $p,p^\circ\in[0,1]$, and hence has a fixed bounded range. Its tracking tolerance is fixed once $k$ is fixed. This bounds all initial and terminal reference allowances uniformly over the posteriors. Superadditivity gives $\sum_i M_i\le k$; active size totals and core counts are at most $Ck$, and $D\le Ck$. The coarse refresh accounting thus has a uniform finite initialization allowance as well.

For the proportion potentials, $\|\alpha\|_1=1$ and $\|w\|_1\le C_w$. The regular component estimates in Section <a href="#sec:proportions" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:proportions">4</a> sum to $C\ell k$, and the dominant component estimate has that same bound uniformly as $u$ tends to zero. The side-potential components sum to $C\ell(D\|w\|_1+S)\le C\ell k$. Their signed terminal values therefore have a uniform finite lower bound, as required for their telescopes. The switch potential is nonnegative and bounded by $Ck$. These bounds use finitely many fixed tracker coordinates and one pair of endpoints for each telescope, never the number of histories, requests, or epochs. Wholesale reinitializations are paid jumps within those same telescopes. Trackers remain attached to the fixed vertices even while their coordinates are inactive; reusing a vertex label never creates a new unpaid endpoint allowance.

Initialize the star data at time zero with the stated tracking rules, run the proportion minimizations, and choose a valid regime. Set $q_{\rm root}=k$ and propagate the outputs top down. The root has $L_{\rm root}^{\beta_{\rm root}}=k$, since its hidden count is always $k$. Lemma <a href="#lem:output-feasibility" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:output-feasibility">15</a> therefore proves by induction, at initialization and after each step, $$q_v\ge L_v^{\beta_v},\qquad
 0\le q_v\le C_0m_v,\qquad
 \sum_{i\text{ child of }v}q_i\le q_v$$ for one absolute $C_0$, enlarging it to cover the root. At the required leaf $x$, its hidden count is at least one almost surely under the current posterior. Hence $p_{x1}=0$, $l^{\beta_x}(0)=1$, and $q_x\ge1$. All choices have used only the observed history and its conditional laws.

It remains to sum the movement estimate. Multiply <a href="#eq:star-output-movement" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:star-output-movement">[eq:star-output-movement]</a> for an internal vertex $v$ by $r_{|v|}$, and sum over the fixed finite tree. Every nonroot vertex appears exactly once as a child, with weight $r_{|v|-1}$, so the left-hand side is the quantity $V$ in Theorem <a href="#thm:allocation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:allocation">5</a>. The root has no movement. At every other internal vertex the parent term has weight $r_{|v|}=r_{|v|-1}/\tau$. Thus <a id="eq:allocation-absorption"></a>$$V\le \frac{C_{\mathrm{par}}}{\tau}V
        +C\ell\sum_{v\text{ internal}}r_{|v|}E_v
        +D_{\mathrm{tree}},
 \qquad
 D_{\mathrm{tree}}=
    \sum_{v\text{ internal}}r_{|v|}D_v^{\mathrm{out}}<\infty.
 \label{eq:allocation-absorption}$$ The rank-drift coupling <a href="#eq:rank-drift-tree" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:rank-drift-tree">[eq:rank-drift-tree]</a> bounds the middle sum by $6P$, uniformly for $\tau\ge2$. Choose any absolute $\tau\ge\max\{2,2C_{\mathrm{par}}\}$; it may be enlarged later to meet the independent rounding requirement. Absorbing the first term yields $$V\le 12C\ell P+2D_{\mathrm{tree}}.$$ The constants multiplying movement have no dependence on the depth, degree, number of leaves, or $k$. The finite additive constant may depend on the fixed tree, its radii, and $k$, but the uniform endpoint bounds above show that it is independent of the law and horizon. This proves Theorem <a href="#thm:allocation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:allocation">5</a>.

<a id="sec:pilot"></a>
### A compact potential for partition edits

The partitions change with the posterior counting measure. We will pay their edits by decreases of a potential, so we must first bound its increases when a comparator member moves. A Lipschitz estimate at each scale would charge the same movement once per level. The improvement comes from local concentration: when almost all nearby mass lies close to the request, a minimizing integrand has a much smaller positive slope there. The errors in this slope estimate sum to one logarithm through ball-mass ratios.

The potential is a minimum of linear functions of the measure. This makes filtering inexpensive by concavity. To control physical movement, we analyze the minimizing functions themselves: concentration forces an auxiliary site of nearly unit weight near the request, and relocating that site bounds its displacement. These two properties give the required slope estimate.

<a id="eq:pilot-mover"></a>
We use the true-metric measures from Section <a href="#sec:setup" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:setup">2</a>. Thus, after the new request $x$ has been revealed, $$\mu^+=\mu^- -\nu+\delta_x,\qquad
 \nu\le\mu^-,\qquad \nu(Y)=1,\qquad
 e_x=\int_Y d(x,y)\,d\nu(y),
 \label{eq:pilot-mover}$$ and both counting measures have total mass $k$. The radii are $r_j=r_0\tau^{-j}$, $1\le j\le J$, with $\tau\ge2$. The expectation of the sum of the quantities $e_x$ is $Q$.

<a id="pm-h-37"></a>
#### The finite-dimensional minimization

A *template* consists of fixed numbers $0<\sigma\le1$, $R\ge256$, and $L\ge0$. Put $$D_*=10R,\qquad D_1=10D_*=100R.$$ All distances in the definitions of the following scalar functions are dimensionless: <a id="eq:pilot-bumps"></a>$$\begin{aligned}
 b(v)&=\max\{0,\min\{1,2-v/\sigma\}\},\nonumber\\
 a(v)&=\begin{cases}
       \min\{v^2/\sigma^2,1\},&0\le v\le R,\\
       2-v/R,&R\le v\le2R,\\
       0,&v\ge2R,
       \end{cases}\nonumber\\
 G(v)&=\max\{0,\min\{1,v/D_*-1\}\}.
 \label{eq:pilot-bumps}
\end{aligned}$$ In particular, $b$ is one on $[0,\sigma]$ and zero on $[2\sigma,\infty)$; $a$ is one on $[\sigma,R]$; and $G$ is zero on $[0,D_*]$ and one on $[2D_*,\infty)$. Their Lipschitz constants are, respectively, $1/\sigma$, at most $2/\sigma$, and $1/D_*$. At radius $r$ abbreviate $a_s(p)=a(d(s,p)/r)$, and use the same convention for $b_s,G_s$.

<a id="eq:pilot-feasible"></a>
<a id="eq:pilot-integrand"></a>
<a id="eq:pilot-potential"></a>
Let $\mathcal Z_r$ be the set of vectors $z=(z_s)_{s\in Y}$ satisfying $$\begin{split}
 &z_s\ge0,\qquad
   \sum_{s\in Y}(z_s-G_s(p))_+\le1\quad(p\in Y),\\
 &d(s,s')\ge5\sigma r
       \quad\text{whenever }s\ne s'\text{ and }z_sz_{s'}>0.
 \end{split}
 \label{eq:pilot-feasible}$$ Here $(u)_+=\max\{u,0\}$. For $g:Y\to[0,1]$ with $r\mathop{\mathrm{Lip}}(g)\le L$, define $$\begin{aligned}
 F_z^g(p)
   &=(1+g(p))\left(1-\sum_s z_sb_s(p)\right)
       +256\sum_s z_s(1+g(s))a_s(p),
       \label{eq:pilot-integrand}\\
 \mathcal L_r(\mu,g)
   &=r\min_{z\in\mathcal Z_r}\int_Y F_z^g(p)\,d\mu(p).
       \label{eq:pilot-potential}
\end{aligned}$$

The two terms in $F_z^g$ favor a site near concentrated mass. A site at $s$ cancels part of the residual cost inside $B(s,\sigma r)$, while its surcharge grows quadratically with distance from $s$ until that distance reaches $\sigma r$. The large surcharge makes a site at intermediate distance from a concentration expensive. The factors involving $g$ allow a partition edit to lower the contribution of a site near the request. We will measure that decrease using the old minimizing site vector, with $g$ chosen from the partition structures.

The site vector is an auxiliary object, with no prescribed total weight. Its capacity constraints bound the total weight of nearby sites by one. The gradual cutoff $G$ will let us move a site and repair those constraints by reducing neighboring weights. The separation condition has a different role: it makes the positive supports of the residual bumps $b_s$ disjoint, so that this repair has a cost independent of the number of sites. The template superscript on $\mathcal L_r$ is suppressed when it is fixed.

<a id="stmt-lem-pilot-compact"></a>
<div id="lem:pilot-compact" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:pilot-size"></a>
<a id="eq:pilot-global-bounds"></a>
**Lemma 17** (Compactness, concavity, and uniform bounds). *For every nonnegative measure $\mu$ on the finite space $Y$, the minimum in <a href="#eq:pilot-potential" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:pilot-potential">[eq:pilot-potential]</a> exists. For fixed $g$, the function $\mu\mapsto\mathcal L_r(\mu,g)$ is concave, and $$0\le\mathcal L_r(\mu,g)\le2r\mu(Y).
 \label{eq:pilot-size}$$ Every feasible integrand satisfies $$0\le F_z^g\le514,\qquad
 r\mathop{\mathrm{Lip}}(F_z^g)\le\Lambda:=L+1026/\sigma.
 \label{eq:pilot-global-bounds}$$ Moreover, the positive supports of the functions $b_s$ with $z_s>0$ are pairwise disjoint.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-pilot-compact"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-pilot-compact</summary>

*Proof.* Testing capacity at $p=s$ gives $0\le z_s\le1$. For each pair with $d(s,s')<5\sigma r$, the separation condition is exactly the closed equation $z_sz_{s'}=0$. Thus $\mathcal Z_r$ is a closed subset of the finite cube $[0,1]^Y$, containing zero. It is compact, and the objective is continuous. Although this set need not be convex, a minimum therefore exists.

At every point $p$, the weights of all centers in $B(p,D_*r)$ sum to at most one, because $G_s(p)=0$ there. Every center contributing to $a_s(p)$ or $b_s(p)$ lies in that ball. It follows that $$0\le1-\sum_s z_sb_s(p)\le1,
 \qquad \sum_s z_sa_s(p)\le1.$$ These inequalities give the bound $514=2+512$ for the integrand, its nonnegativity, and the lower bound in <a href="#eq:pilot-size" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:pilot-size">[eq:pilot-size]</a>. Taking $z=0$ gives the upper bound for the minimum.

If $d(p,q)\le r$, every center contributing at either endpoint belongs to $B(p,(2R+1)r)\subset B(p,D_*r)$, so all its weights together sum to at most one. The residual term in <a href="#eq:pilot-integrand" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:pilot-integrand">[eq:pilot-integrand]</a> consequently has Lipschitz bound $(L+2/\sigma)/r$, and the surcharge term has bound $1024/(\sigma r)$. If $d(p,q)>r$, the bound $514$ on the range already implies the claimed Lipschitz bound, since $\Lambda\ge1026$. Finally, if $b_s(p)$ and $b_{s'}(p)$ are both positive, then $d(s,s')<4\sigma r$, contrary to separation for two positive weights.

For nonnegative measures $\mu_0,\mu_1$ and $0\le\theta\le1$, every feasible $z$ satisfies $$r\int F_z^g\,d(\theta\mu_0+(1-\theta)\mu_1)
 \ge\theta\mathcal L_r(\mu_0,g)
       +(1-\theta)\mathcal L_r(\mu_1,g).$$ Taking the minimum proves concavity. Convexity of $\mathcal Z_r$ was not used. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-38"></a>
#### The three local optimality estimates

<a id="eq:pilot-concentration-constants"></a>
<a id="eq:pilot-shell-moment"></a>
Fix, for this template, constants satisfying $$0<\gamma\le\min\{\sigma/64,1/(1+L)\},
 \qquad 0<\delta\le1/4112.
 \label{eq:pilot-concentration-constants}$$ They may be decreased for the applications below. For a point $x$ and a measure $\mu$ with $M:=\mu(B(x,\gamma r))>0$, write $$h=\frac{\mu(B(x,D_1r)\setminus B(x,\gamma r))}{M},
 \qquad
 t=\frac1M\int_{B(x,\gamma r)}\frac{d(x,p)}r\,d\mu(p).
 \label{eq:pilot-shell-moment}$$ In particular $0\le t\le\gamma$. The shell ratio $h$ measures mass outside the inner ball, whereas $t$ measures how tightly the inner mass is centered at $x$. Both errors matter: even when $h=0$, the mass in the inner ball need not lie at $x$. In this subsection $h$ denotes only the shell ratio, not a partition tier.

<a id="stmt-lem-pilot-local"></a>
<div id="lem:pilot-local" class="lemma">

**Lemma 18** (Local structure and positive slope). *Assume $h\le\delta$, and let $z$ be any minimizer in <a href="#eq:pilot-potential" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:pilot-potential">[eq:pilot-potential]</a>. Then the following assertions hold.*

1.  *A center $s$ with $\sigma r/4\le d(s,x)\le Rr/2$ has $z_s=0$.*

<a id="eq:pilot-local-structure"></a>
2.  *There is a unique positive center $s$ with $d(s,x)<\sigma r/4$. Its weight $z_s$ and normalized displacement $w=d(s,x)/r$ satisfy $$z_s\ge\tfrac34,\qquad
           1-z_s\le\frac{512\gamma}{\sigma^2}t+514h,
           \qquad w\le(5+L)(h+t).
           \label{eq:pilot-local-structure}$$*

<a id="eq:pilot-local-slope"></a>
3.  *If $d(x,y)<\sigma r/8$, then $$_+
              \le C_{\mathrm{loc}}(h+t)\frac{d(x,y)}r,
           \label{eq:pilot-local-slope}$$ where one admissible constant is <a id="eq:pilot-local-constant"></a>$$C_{\mathrm{loc}}
           =(L+1024/\sigma)(514+8/\sigma)
               +\frac{1024(5+L)}{\sigma^2}.
           \label{eq:pilot-local-constant}$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-pilot-local"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-pilot-local</summary>

*Proof.* We perform the calculations in units of $r$; integrals of $F$ therefore omit the common factor $r$ in the potential.

*Deleting a middle-distance site.* For $\sigma/4\le d(s,x)/r\le R/2$ and $p\in B(x,\gamma r)$, the distance $d(s,p)/r$ is at least $\sigma/4-\gamma$ and less than $R$. Consequently $$a_s(p)\ge(1/4-\gamma/\sigma)^2\ge(15/64)^2.$$ The support of $b_s$ is inside $B(x,D_1r)$. The coefficient of $z_s$ in the integral objective is therefore at least $$256\int a_s\,d\mu-2\int b_s\,d\mu
 \ge\left[\frac{225}{16}-2(1+\delta)\right]M>0.$$ Decreasing a weight preserves both constraints in <a href="#eq:pilot-feasible" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:pilot-feasible">[eq:pilot-feasible]</a>. A positive such weight would contradict minimality, proving the first assertion.

*Inserting a unit site.* Clear all weights at centers in $B(x,5D_*r)$, and put weight one at $x$. Separation holds, since every retained center is farther than $5D_*r$. To check capacity, consider a test point $p$ where the new site’s contribution is positive. Then $d(p,x)<2D_*r$. Every old center contributing positively at that test satisfies $d(s',p)<2D_*r$, because $z_{s'}\le1$ and $G_{s'}(p)<z_{s'}$. Such a center lies in $B(x,4D_*r)$ and has been cleared. The single new contribution is at most one. At other tests the new site contributes zero and weights have only been removed. Thus the competitor is feasible.

There is at most one positive old site within $\sigma r/4$ of $x$ by separation; denote its weight by $Z$, taking $Z=0$ if it does not exist. On the entire inner ball its $b$ value is one. Every other positive site has distance greater than $Rr/2$ from $x$ by the first assertion, so its $b$ value is zero on the inner ball. The old residual is thus $1-Z$ there, and the old integrand is at least $1-Z$. The new integrand there is at most $512(d(x,p)/r)^2/\sigma^2$, whose integral is at most $512M\gamma t/\sigma^2$.

<a id="eq:pilot-deficit-proof"></a>
All changed bumps are supported in $B(x,(5D_*+2R)r)\subset B(x,D_1r)$. Outside the inner ball the increase is at most $514$ pointwise, by Lemma <a href="#lem:pilot-compact" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:pilot-compact">17</a>. Minimality of the old vector gives $$1-Z\le512\gamma t/\sigma^2+514h
       \le512\gamma^2/\sigma^2+514\delta\le\tfrac14.
 \label{eq:pilot-deficit-proof}$$ Here both last summands are at most $1/8$ by <a href="#eq:pilot-concentration-constants" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:pilot-concentration-constants">[eq:pilot-concentration-constants]</a>. This proves existence, uniqueness, the weight bound, and the deficit bound in <a href="#eq:pilot-local-structure" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:pilot-local-structure">[eq:pilot-local-structure]</a>.

*Relocating that site and repairing capacity.* Write $s$ for this near site and $z=z_s$. The case $w=0$ already has the desired displacement bound. For $w>0$, move its weight from $s$ to $x$. Simultaneously replace every other weight $z_i$ with center in $B(x,5D_*r)$ by $(z_i-q)_+$, where $$q=w/D_*.$$ If $s\ne x$, the old weight at $x$ is zero by the just-proved uniqueness. Every other positive center lies beyond $Rr/2$ from $x$, so the moved site preserves separation.

For a capacity test $p$, put $$A=(z-G_s(p))_+,\quad A'=(z-G_x(p))_+,
 \quad B=\sum_{i\ne s}(z_i-G_i(p))_+.$$ When $A'\le A$, capacity can only improve. Otherwise $A'>0$, and $A'-A\le q$ by the Lipschitz constant of $G$. The same distance calculation as above puts every center contributing to $B$ within $4D_*r$ of $x$, hence among the reduced centers. If $b_i=(z_i-G_i(p))_+$, its contribution decreases by exactly $\min\{q,b_i\}$. For any nonnegative finite family $(b_i)$, <a id="eq:pilot-capacity-repair"></a>$$\sum_i\min\{q,b_i\}\ge\min\{q,\sum_i b_i\}.
 \label{eq:pilot-capacity-repair}$$ Indeed, a summand with $b_i\ge q$ proves the inequality immediately; if none exists, the left side is $\sum_i b_i$. If $B\ge q$, the reductions pay the increase $A'-A$. If $B<q$, all the other contributions vanish and the remaining contribution satisfies $A'\le z\le1$. This verifies every capacity test, with no dependence on the number of sites.

<a id="eq:pilot-relocation-gain"></a>
For $p$ in the inner ball put $u=d(s,p)/r$ and $v=d(x,p)/r$. Both are less than $\sigma$, so the moved site’s old and new $a$ values are $u^2/\sigma^2$ and $v^2/\sigma^2$. The triangle inequality gives $u^2\ge w^2-2wv+v^2$: if $v\le w$, square $u\ge w-v$; if $v>w$, the right side $(w-v)^2$ follows from $u\ge v-w$. Consequently, using $L\gamma\le1$, $$\begin{aligned}
 (1+g(s))u^2-(1+g(x))v^2
 &\ge (1+g(s))(w^2-2wv)-Lwv^2\\
 &\ge w^2-(4+L\gamma)wv
 \ge w^2-5wv.
\end{aligned}$$ The near site’s $b$ value stays one there; all other $b$ values stay zero; and reducing other weights can only lower their surcharges. The integral gain on the inner ball is therefore at least $$\frac{256zM}{\sigma^2}(w^2-5wt).
 \label{eq:pilot-relocation-gain}$$

For completeness, the pointwise loss outside the inner ball has a bound proportional to $w$, rather than a constant per changed site. Moving the near site’s center changes its residual contribution by at most $2zw/\sigma$, and its surcharge by at most $256z(4/\sigma+L)w$. Thus its total increase is at most $zK_0w$, where $$K_0=1026/\sigma+256L.$$ The reduction of other weights can only increase the residual. Since their positive $b$ supports are disjoint, the residual increase is at most $2q$ at every point. All changes vanish outside $B(x,(5D_*+2R)r)$. The shell loss is therefore at most $Mh(zK_0+2/D_*)w$. Combining this with <a href="#eq:pilot-relocation-gain" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:pilot-relocation-gain">[eq:pilot-relocation-gain]</a> and minimality, and dividing by the positive quantity $256zMw/\sigma^2$, gives $$w\le5t+\frac{\sigma^2}{256}
                   \left(K_0+\frac{2}{zD_*}\right)h
   \le5t+(5+L)h\le(5+L)(h+t).$$ For the middle inequality, use $z\ge1/2$, $\sigma\le1$, $D_*\ge2560$, and $1026/256+4/(256D_*)<5$. This proves the last local structural estimate.

*Positive slope at the request.* Put $v=d(x,y)/r<\sigma/8$. The near site’s $b$ is one at both points, and all other positive sites have $b=0$ at both points. The residual part of $F_z^g(x)-F_z^g(y)$ is consequently at most $L(1-z)v$. Writing $u=d(s,y)/r$, the near-site surcharge difference has positive part at most $$\frac{512z}{\sigma^2}(w^2-u^2)_+
 \le\frac{1024w}{\sigma^2}v,$$ because $(w^2-u^2)_+\le2w|w-u|$. Every other center contributing an $a$ value at either endpoint is within $(2R+\sigma/8)r<D_*r$ of $x$. Capacity at $x$, including the near site, bounds the total of these other weights by $1-z$. Their surcharge difference has absolute value at most $1024(1-z)v/\sigma$. Altogether, $$[F_z^g(x)-F_z^g(y)]_+
 \le\left[(L+1024/\sigma)(1-z)
              +1024w/\sigma^2\right]v.$$ Finally <a href="#eq:pilot-deficit-proof" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:pilot-deficit-proof">[eq:pilot-deficit-proof]</a> and $\gamma\le\sigma/64$ give $1-z\le(514+8/\sigma)(h+t)$, and the displacement bound proves <a href="#eq:pilot-local-slope" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:pilot-local-slope">[eq:pilot-local-slope]</a> with the stated constant. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-39"></a>
#### Summing the drift over scales

<a id="stmt-lem-pilot-drift"></a>
<div id="lem:pilot-drift" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:pilot-drift"></a>
<a id="eq:pilot-drift-constant"></a>
**Lemma 19** (Pilot drift bound). *Fix a template and constants as in <a href="#eq:pilot-concentration-constants" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:pilot-concentration-constants">[eq:pilot-concentration-constants]</a>. At each radius let $g_j$ be any function with $0\le g_j\le1$ and $r_j\mathop{\mathrm{Lip}}(g_j)\le L$. For measures satisfying <a href="#eq:pilot-mover" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:pilot-mover">[eq:pilot-mover]</a>, $$\sum_{j=1}^J
 [\mathcal L_{r_j}(\mu^+,g_j)
        -\mathcal L_{r_j}(\mu^-,g_j)]_+
 \le C_{\mathrm{dr}}\ell e_x.
 \label{eq:pilot-drift}$$ The constant is independent of $Y$, $k$, $J$, and $\tau\ge2$. For example, set $$\begin{aligned}
 p_0&=\left\lceil\log_2(D_1/\gamma)\right\rceil,\nonumber\\
 C_{\mathrm{dr}}
   &=\frac{2056}{\gamma}+\frac{8224}{\sigma}
       +\frac{2\Lambda p_0}{\delta\log2}
       +2C_{\mathrm{loc}}
                   \left(\frac{p_0}{\log2}+2\gamma\right).
 \label{eq:pilot-drift-constant}
\end{aligned}$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-pilot-drift"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-pilot-drift</summary>

<a id="eq:pilot-candidate-drift"></a>
*Proof.* Let $z^{(j)}$ minimize the old objective at radius $r_j$ and write $F_j=F_{z^{(j)}}^{g_j}$. Evaluating the new minimum at this vector yields $$\begin{split}
 &\mathcal L_{r_j}(\mu^+,g_j)-\mathcal L_{r_j}(\mu^-,g_j)\\
 &\hspace{15mm}\le r_j\int_Y(F_j(x)-F_j(y))\,d\nu(y)
 \le r_j\int_Y[F_j(x)-F_j(y)]_+\,d\nu(y).
 \end{split}
 \label{eq:pilot-candidate-drift}$$ If $e_x=0$, the probability measure $\nu$ is supported at $x$ and the two counting measures agree. Assume henceforth $e_x>0$.

The radii smaller than $2e_x/\gamma$ contribute at most <a id="eq:pilot-small-scales"></a>$$514\sum_{r_j<2e_x/\gamma}r_j
 \le\frac{2056}{\gamma}e_x,
 \label{eq:pilot-small-scales}$$ by $\tau\ge2$ and <a href="#eq:pilot-global-bounds" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:pilot-global-bounds">[eq:pilot-global-bounds]</a>. At every remaining radius, domination by $\mu^-$ and the definition of $e_x$ give <a id="eq:pilot-inner-mass"></a>$$M_j:=\mu^-(B(x,\gamma r_j))
 \ge\nu(B(x,\gamma r_j))
 \ge1-\frac{e_x}{\gamma r_j}\ge\tfrac12.
 \label{eq:pilot-inner-mass}$$ This is a consecutive range of coarse radii. Define $h_j,t_j$ there as in <a href="#eq:pilot-shell-moment" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:pilot-shell-moment">[eq:pilot-shell-moment]</a> with $\mu=\mu^-$.

<a id="eq:pilot-shell-sum"></a>
We record both scale sums explicitly. Set $A_j=\mu^-(B(x,D_1r_j))=(1+h_j)M_j$. For $u\ge0$, <a id="eq:pilot-capped-log"></a>$$\min\{1,u\}\le\frac{\log(1+u)}{\log2}.
 \label{eq:pilot-capped-log}$$ For $0\le u\le1$ this follows from the chord between the endpoint values of the concave function $\log(1+u)$, and for $u\ge1$ it follows from monotonicity. Since $\tau^{p_0}\gamma\ge D_1$, the mass $A_j$ is at most $M_{j-p_0}$ whenever that coarser index is in the range. Assigning the value $k$ to missing coarser masses gives $$\sum_j\log(A_j/M_j)
 \le\sum_j(\log M_{j-p_0}-\log M_j)
 \le p_0\log(2k).$$ All interior logarithms cancel; at most $p_0$ boundary differences remain, each between masses in $[1/2,k]$. This is also the instance of Lemma <a href="#lem:scale-logarithms" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:scale-logarithms">4</a> needed here. Thus $$\sum_j\min\{1,h_j\}\le\frac{p_0}{\log2}\log(2k).
 \label{eq:pilot-shell-sum}$$

For the moment sum only, order the same radii increasingly as $\rho_1<\cdots<\rho_n$, with $\rho_{i+1}=\tau\rho_i$, and set $m_i=\mu^-(B(x,\gamma\rho_i))$, $m_0=0$, and $\Delta_i=m_i-m_{i-1}$. Partitioning the $j$th inner ball into the successive differences of these balls gives $$t_j\le\gamma\sum_{i\le j}
                 \tau^{i-j}\frac{\Delta_i}{m_j}.$$ The first difference includes the entire first ball; hence this bound also accounts for any atom already inside that ball. Since $m_j\ge m_i$ for $j\ge i$, interchanging two finite sums gives <a id="eq:pilot-moment-sum"></a>$$\begin{aligned}
 \sum_{j=1}^n t_j
 &\le\gamma\sum_{i=1}^n\frac{\Delta_i}{m_i}
                  \sum_{j=i}^n\tau^{i-j}
 \le2\gamma\sum_{i=1}^n\frac{\Delta_i}{m_i}\nonumber\\
 &\le2\gamma\left(1+\log\frac{m_n}{m_1}\right)
 \le2\gamma(1+\log(2k)).
 \label{eq:pilot-moment-sum}
\end{aligned}$$ Indeed the first ratio $\Delta_1/m_1$ is one, and, for $i\ge2$, $\Delta_i/m_i\le\int_{m_{i-1}}^{m_i}du/u$. Empty scale ranges require no estimate.

Call a remaining radius bad if $h_j>\delta$. Because $\delta<1$, <a href="#eq:pilot-shell-sum" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:pilot-shell-sum">[eq:pilot-shell-sum]</a> bounds the number of bad radii by $p_0\log(2k)/(\delta\log2)$. The global Lipschitz bound gives at most $\Lambda e_x$ in <a href="#eq:pilot-candidate-drift" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:pilot-candidate-drift">[eq:pilot-candidate-drift]</a> at each one. At a good radius apply Lemma <a href="#lem:pilot-local" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:pilot-local">18</a> to points with $d(x,y)<\sigma r_j/8$. Their total contribution, summed over good radii and integrated against $\nu$, is at most $$C_{\mathrm{loc}}e_x
 \left[\frac{p_0}{\log2}\log(2k)
                      +2\gamma(1+\log(2k))\right].$$ Here $h_j=\min\{1,h_j\}$ on good radii. For each fixed exceptional point $y$, with $d(x,y)\ge\sigma r_j/8$, use boundedness instead: <a id="eq:pilot-far-scales"></a>$$\sum_{r_j\le8d(x,y)/\sigma}514r_j
       \le\frac{8224}{\sigma}d(x,y).
 \label{eq:pilot-far-scales}$$ Integrating proves that all exceptional terms together cost at most $8224e_x/\sigma$. This bound has no factor counting scales. Combining the estimates, using $1+\log(2k)\le2\ell$ and $\ell\ge1$, gives precisely <a href="#eq:pilot-drift" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:pilot-drift">[eq:pilot-drift]</a> with <a href="#eq:pilot-drift-constant" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:pilot-drift-constant">[eq:pilot-drift-constant]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-40"></a>
#### Filtering before selecting edits

The following formulation will allow both heavy-center edits and the summably weighted tier edits to use the same drift budget. It also fixes the order of conditioning: the filtering estimate is applied at every step with the old coefficients, before selecting any edits.

<a id="stmt-cor-pilot-edits"></a>
<div id="cor:pilot-edits" class="corollary">

<a id="eq:pilot-edit-budget"></a>
**Corollary 20** (Budget for summably weighted templates). *Let templates be indexed by a finite or countable set $I$, with fixed weights $\omega_\alpha\ge0$. Each template has fixed admissible parameters and the drift constant $C_{\mathrm{dr},\alpha}$ of Lemma <a href="#lem:pilot-drift" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:pilot-drift">19</a>. Assume $$A:=\sum_{\alpha\in I}\omega_\alpha<\infty,
 \qquad
 B:=\sum_{\alpha\in I}\omega_\alpha C_{\mathrm{dr},\alpha}<\infty.$$ At each step, filter and drift the counting measure while keeping the previous functions $g_{\alpha,j}$ fixed. Then edit these functions using the new history, retaining their template bounds. Suppose the weighted total potential drop in the edit stage is at least a nonnegative charge $c_t$, taking $c_t=0$ on steps without edits. Then $$\mathbb E\sum_t c_t
 \le B\ell Q+2kA\sum_{j=1}^Jr_j
 \le B\ell Q+\frac{2kr_0A}{\tau-1}.
 \label{eq:pilot-edit-budget}$$ In particular, if $A,B$ are absolute constants, the multiplicative budget is $O(\ell Q)$ and the additive bound is independent of the input law and horizon.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-cor-pilot-edits"><summary>Proof of stmt-cor-pilot-edits</summary>

<a id="eq:pilot-filtering"></a>
*Proof.* Let $\mu_{t-1}$ be the previous post-step posterior counting measure. The posterior identity is $$\mathbb E[\mu_t^-\mid\mathcal F_{t-1}]=\mu_{t-1}.$$ For every old, $\mathcal F_{t-1}$-measurable function $g=g_{t-1,\alpha,j}$, concavity gives $$\mathbb E[\mathcal L_{r_j}(\mu_t^-,g)\mid\mathcal F_{t-1}]
       \le\mathcal L_{r_j}(\mu_{t-1},g).
 \label{eq:pilot-filtering}$$ One can see this directly without any assumption on convexity of site vectors: choose a minimizing vector at $\mu_{t-1}$, use it as a competitor for each refined posterior, and take its conditional expectation. Its objective is linear in the measure and therefore returns exactly the old objective.

Write $\Phi_{t-1}$ for the weighted total potential after the preceding step, $\Phi_t^-$ for its value after filtering at old $g$’s, and $\widehat\Phi_t$ for its value after drift, still at old $g$’s. Write $\Phi_t$ for its value after the edits. The three established bounds are $$\mathbb E\Phi_t^-\le\mathbb E\Phi_{t-1},\qquad
 \widehat\Phi_t-\Phi_t^-\le B\ell e_x,
 \qquad \Phi_t\le\widehat\Phi_t-c_t.$$ All these potentials are nonnegative and at most $2kA\sum_jr_j$. The same statements hold for a countable family: finite partial sums give the filtering inequality by convergence of nonnegative bounded sums, and the absolute bound by $2kr_j\omega_\alpha$ for each potential makes the signed differences summable. The drift bound sums because its weighted constants sum to $B$.

Taking expectations, summing in $t$, and using $\mathbb E\sum_t e_x=Q$ proves $\mathbb E\sum_t c_t\le B\ell Q+\mathbb E\Phi_0-\mathbb E\Phi_T$. Dropping the nonnegative terminal value and applying <a href="#eq:pilot-size" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:pilot-size">[eq:pilot-size]</a> yields <a href="#eq:pilot-edit-budget" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:pilot-edit-budget">[eq:pilot-edit-budget]</a>. In particular, <a href="#eq:pilot-filtering" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:pilot-filtering">[eq:pilot-filtering]</a> was summed over *all* steps. It was never conditioned on the event that a later edit was selected. ◻

</details>

For use in verifying the edit hypothesis, if $z$ is an old minimizer at the already updated measure $\mu^+$, then <a id="eq:pilot-edit-candidate"></a>$$\mathcal L_r(\mu^+,g^{\rm old})
      -\mathcal L_r(\mu^+,g^{\rm new})
 \ge r\int_Y(F_z^{g^{\rm old}}-F_z^{g^{\rm new}})\,d\mu^+.
 \label{eq:pilot-edit-candidate}$$ Thus the next section only needs a lower bound on the gain of the old candidate. In the tier application the template is fixed across tiers and $\omega_h=e^{-h}$ for $h=1,2,4,\ldots$. Since these tiers form a subset of the positive integers, $\sum_h e^{-h}\le\sum_{n\ge1}e^{-n}=1/(e-1)$, so both constants in Corollary <a href="#cor:pilot-edits" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cor:pilot-edits">20</a> are bounded independently of the number of tiers.

<a id="sec:partitions"></a>
### Online partitions and the embedded comparator

Probabilistic tree embeddings use scale partitions to represent a metric (Bartal 1996, 1998; Fakcharoenphol, Rao, and Talwar 2004) <a id="pm-cite-26"></a>[[Bartal96](#ref-Bartal96), [Bartal98](#ref-Bartal98), [FRT04](#ref-FRT04)]. Local ball-mass ratios and their logarithmic scale sums are familiar from measured descent (Krauthgamer et al. 2005, Lemma 1.7 and Section 1.3) <a id="pm-cite-27"></a>[[KLMN05](#ref-KLMN05)]. Protection by heavy balls and maintenance of separated nets also appear in Lee’s evolving fusible-HST construction (Lee 2018, secs. 3.1.2–3.2) <a id="pm-cite-28"></a>[[Lee18](#ref-Lee18)]; its withdrawn competitive-ratio claim is discussed in Section <a href="#sec:introduction" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:introduction">1</a>. Here the changing measure is the posterior of the hidden comparator. The pilot potentials will pay the edits induced by maintaining these partitions.

The partitions at distinct scales need not refine one another. A point’s entire string of scale labels will specify its leaf in a fixed tree. We protect requests where the comparator measure is locally concentrated by large disjoint balls. At the other scales, chronological rosters of smaller balls provide a separation estimate whose coefficient depends on the local mass ratio. The pilot potentials pay changes to both structures.

We use the finite metric, selected labeled comparator, scales, and filtration of Section <a href="#sec:setup" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:setup">2</a>. In particular, $$r_j=r_0\tau^{-j},\qquad r_0=\mathop{\mathrm{diam}}Y,\qquad
 40r_J<\min_{u\ne v}d(u,v),\qquad 1\le j\le J.$$ All the numerical constants chosen below are absolute. The constant $\tau$ also satisfies the absorption requirements of the allocation and rounding arguments. At a request $x$, the true-metric posterior measures obey <a id="eq:partition-mover"></a>$$\label{eq:partition-mover}
 \mu^+=\mu^--\nu+\delta_x,\qquad
 \nu(Y)=1,\qquad \nu\le\mu^-,\qquad
 e_x=\int d(x,y)\,d\nu(y).$$ The selected mover is specified even on a zero move. In particular every ball centered at $x$ has $\mu^+$-mass at least one, and $\mathbb E\sum_t e_{x_t}=Q$.

<a id="stmt-prop-embedded-cost"></a>
<div id="prop:embedded-cost" class="proposition">

<a id="eq:embedded-cost"></a>
**Proposition 21** (Embedded comparator cost). *There are finite alphabets $\mathcal A_1,\ldots,\mathcal A_J$, depending only on $Y,k$ and the fixed constants, and online random maps $$\xi_t:Y\longrightarrow\mathcal A_1\times\cdots\times\mathcal A_J$$ with the following properties. Each coordinate cell at level $j$ has diameter at most $40r_j$ and an anchor within $20r_j$ of all its points. Thus $\xi_t$ is injective. Give the full alphabet tree the leaf distance $r_{j-1}$ when the first differing coordinate is $j$. For the selected hidden members $X_t^1,\ldots,X_t^k$, $$\label{eq:embedded-cost}
 P:=\mathbb E\sum_t\sum_{a=1}^k
 d_{\mathcal T}\bigl(\xi_{t-1}(X_{t-1}^a),\xi_t(X_t^a)\bigr)
 \le C\ell Q+D_{\rm part}.$$ Here $C$ is absolute and $D_{\rm part}<\infty$ is independent of the input law and its horizon. A coordinate label common to successive maps has a fixed anchor, except for the explicit heavy-ball reuse below, when its anchor moves by at most $40r_j$.*

</div>

The maps for a given finite input law may use its true-prefix posteriors. The alphabets and all initialization bounds will be fixed before that law or its horizon is chosen. Two forms of conditioning will be kept separate: pilot potentials use filtering in $\mathcal F_t$, whereas ball and retirement probabilities average over the independent partition tape after fixing the whole true input.

<a id="pm-h-42"></a>
#### Heavy balls and their edit cost

<a id="eq:heavy-condition"></a>
Use the pilot template of Section <a href="#sec:pilot" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:pilot">6</a> with $\sigma=1$, $R=512$, and Lipschitz bound one for $g$ in scale units. Let $D_H=D_1=100R$. Choose $\gamma_H,\delta_H>0$ small enough for Lemma <a href="#lem:pilot-local" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:pilot-local">18</a>, and also with $\gamma_H<1/100$, $\delta_H<1/100$. They may be decreased further to meet the absolute requirement in Section <a href="#sec:travel" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:travel">8</a>. At scale $r$, call the current request *heavy* when $$\label{eq:heavy-condition}
 \mu^+(B(x,D_Hr))
 \le(1+\delta_H)\mu^+(B(x,\gamma_Hr)).$$ Maintain an initially empty center set $\mathcal H$. If the request is heavy and $d(x,\mathcal H)>8r$, remove all centers within $100r$ of $x$, and insert $x$. Otherwise leave $\mathcal H$ unchanged. Distance to the empty set is infinite. Every inserted center receives a fresh independent uniform radius in $[16r,20r]$, held until its removal. These radii affect the balls and their labels, but never the center schedule.

Centers in $\mathcal H$ have pairwise distance greater than $100r$, by induction on insertions. Their balls are therefore disjoint. If a new ball intersects an old ball as subsets of $Y$, reuse that old ball’s label. Otherwise use a fresh label. There is at most one intersected old ball: two such old centers would be at distance at most $80r$. Moreover, an intersected center is within $40r$ of $x$, so was removed in this update. The reused label’s anchor consequently moves by at most $40r$. Intersection is meant in the finite metric itself; no geodesic or ambient ball intersection is needed. Heavy balls have precedence over every other structure at the level. In particular their final key regions are their entire balls.

Write $$E_x(r)=\{p\in Y:3r<d(p,x)\le120r\}.$$ First we pay the mass in this annulus at heavy updates. Let $\rho_H$ be zero on $[0,3]$, affine from zero to one on $[3,4]$, and one thereafter, and set $$g_{\mathcal H}(p)=\rho_H\bigl(d(p,\mathcal H)/r\bigr).$$ At a heavy update, apply Lemma <a href="#lem:pilot-local" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:pilot-local">18</a> to $\mu^+$ and the old $g$. The inner mass is at least one and the shell ratio is at most $\delta_H$. Every minimizing pilot configuration has a site $s$ with $d(s,x)<r/4$ and $z_s\ge1/2$, and no positive sites at distances in $[r/4,256r]$ from $x$. The near site’s old $g(s)$ is one, because every old center is more than $8r$ from $x$, and its new value is zero. For every $p\in E_x(r)$, $$2.75<d(s,p)/r\le120.25,$$ so its surcharge bump $a_s(p)$ equals one. The decrease of this surcharge is at least $128r\mu^+(E_x(r))$.

<a id="eq:heavy-annulus-budget"></a>
An increase of $g(p)$ requires removal of a center within $4r$ of $p$, and cannot occur inside $B(x,3r)$, where the new $g$ is zero. Thus every increase is supported at distances in $(3r,104r]$ from $x$. All possible increasing-coefficient sites have zero weight by the forbidden-site conclusion above. Only the residual term can increase, by at most $r\mu^+(E_x(r))$, since its coefficient lies in $[0,1]$. Using the old minimizer as a feasible candidate after the edit proves $$\mathcal L_r(\mu^+,g_{\rm old})
 -\mathcal L_r(\mu^+,g_{\rm new})
 \ge127r\mu^+(E_x(r)).$$ The all-step filtering and drift telescope of Corollary <a href="#cor:pilot-edits" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cor:pilot-edits">20</a> now gives $$\label{eq:heavy-annulus-budget}
 \mathbb E\sum_{t,j:\,\mathcal H_j\text{ updates at }t}
 r_j\mu_t^+(E_{x_t}(r_j))
 \le C\ell Q+D_H'.$$ Here and below the constants denoted by $D$ are finite initialization bounds depending only on the fixed finite data. Filtering and drift are summed at the old parameters on every step, before testing whether an update occurs; there is no conditioning of the filtering inequality on the event of an update.

<a id="eq:partition-log-sums"></a>
We will use two elementary scale sums repeatedly. Set $$L_j(x)=\log\frac{\mu^+(B(x,D_Lr_j))}
                         {\mu^+(B(x,\gamma_Lr_j))},$$ where the absolute constants $D_L>\gamma_L>0$ will be fixed below. Lemma <a href="#lem:scale-logarithms" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:scale-logarithms">4</a>, with inner masses at least one and outer masses at most $k$, gives $$\label{eq:partition-log-sums}
 \sum_{j=1}^J L_j(x)\le p_L\log k,
 \qquad
 \#\{j:\text{the request is nonheavy at }j\}
 \le\frac{p_H\log k}{\log(1+\delta_H)}\le C\ell,$$ where $p_L,p_H$ are fixed positive integers satisfying $\tau^{p_L}\gamma_L\ge D_L$ and $\tau^{p_H}\gamma_H\ge D_H$. For the second bound every nonheavy level contributes more than $\log(1+\delta_H)$ to its heavy-template logarithmic ratio. These sums involve one common measure $\mu^+$ on the step, so the scale telescope applies.

<a id="stmt-lem-heavy-edits"></a>
<div id="lem:heavy-edits" class="lemma">

**Lemma 22** (Heavy edits). *The expected sum of $r_j$ times overall level-key changes caused by heavy edits, evaluated at the hidden members’ old physical positions with the base structures held fixed, is at most $C\ell Q+D_H''$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-heavy-edits"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-heavy-edits</summary>

*Proof.* Let $\psi$ be the clipped increasing ramp on $[9,10]$. For each member at $p$, add the auxiliary potential $r\psi(d(p,\mathcal H)/r)$. Sum it over members and levels, and telescope along actual hidden trajectories from one step end to the next. Its initial value is at most $k\sum_jr_j$ and it is nonnegative. We bound an edit charge plus the full-step change of this potential.

Consider first a stationary member $p$ at an update. If $d(p,x)\le3r$ and an old center lies within $10r$ of $p$, both the old and new heavy balls contain $p$. Their intersection forces reuse, so its key does not change and its potential does not increase. If no such old center exists, its old potential is $r$ and its new potential is zero, paying a possible charge of $r$. Outside this inner ball, all affected old or new balls and all changing ramps lie in $B(x,120r)$: a removed center is within $100r$, its ball has radius at most $20r$, and its ramp has range at most $10r$. The charge plus potential increase is at most $2r$, supported in $E_x(r)$.

For the designated mover $y\to x$, its final potential at an update is zero. The same argument pays its charge when $d(y,x)\le3r$. Otherwise the unpaid amount is at most $r$, and $\sum_{r_j<d(x,y)/3}r_j\le C d(x,y)$. At a step without an update there is no edit charge. On a heavy level a final center is within $8r$ of $x$, so the final potential is again zero. Only nonheavy levels can have a positive physical-motion change; at each it is at most $d(x,y)$, by the ramp’s Lipschitz bound. Their number is bounded in <a href="#eq:partition-log-sums" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:partition-log-sums">[eq:partition-log-sums]</a>.

The conditional expected stationary counting measure equals $\mu^+-\delta_x$, and in particular is bounded by $\mu^+$. Consequently <a href="#eq:heavy-annulus-budget" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:heavy-annulus-budget">[eq:heavy-annulus-budget]</a> pays the stationary errors, and the other terms sum to $C\ell Q$. Nonnegativity and the stated initial bound complete the telescope. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-43"></a>
#### Chronological rosters and a fixed alphabet

For the second pilot template choose $\sigma>0$ so small that $3\sigma<1/8$ and $10\sigma<\gamma_H$, and put $R=4D_H$ and $D_L=100R$. Choose $0<\gamma_L<\sigma$ sufficiently small for this template in Lemma <a href="#lem:pilot-local" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:pilot-local">18</a>. The Lipschitz bound used for its $g$’s will be eight in scale units. In particular $\gamma_L<\gamma_H$ and $D_L>D_H$.

Let $$\mathcal H_k=\{1,2,4,\ldots,2^b\},\qquad
 b=\min\{n\in\mathbb N\cup\{0\}:2^n\ge\log k\}.$$ The symbol $\mathcal H_k$ denotes the set of tier parameters and is distinct from the center set $\mathcal H$. Smaller tiers have precedence. A request qualifies in tier $h\in\mathcal H_k$ at scale $r$ if <a id="eq:tier-qualification"></a>$$\label{eq:tier-qualification}
 \mu^+(B(x,D_Lr))\le e^h\mu^+(B(x,\gamma_Lr)).$$ The last tier always qualifies. Fix an absolute $C_K$, to be chosen large below, and set $K=K_h=\lceil e^{C_Kh}\rceil$.

Each tier keeps chronological insertion records and a random subroster of balls used by the partition. The insertion test below uses only qualification and chronological ages. Every entry young enough to affect that test is deterministically present; later retirement changes the partition but does not feed back into the insertion schedule. This lets us estimate separation and retirement probabilities with that schedule fixed. The age cutoffs also bound the records relevant to the test and partition.

Each pair $(j,h)$ has an initially empty chronological roster of insertions. The age of an entry centered at $c$ is the number of subsequent insertions in that same roster within distance $20r$ of $c$. Insert $x$ if and only if it qualifies and there is no chronological entry of current age less than $K$ within $r/2$ of $x$. A noninsertion changes no ages. These tests inspect the chronological records, not their realized survival. Records of age at least $K^2$ can be discarded forever: they cannot affect an insertion test or any future presence.

<a id="eq:tier-radius-density"></a>
The *actual* roster used in the partition has the following lifetimes. An entry is present deterministically at ages $0,\ldots,K-1$. At each increment to an age in $\{K,\ldots,K^2-1\}$, a still present entry retires with fresh probability $1/K$, independently. It retires compulsorily on reaching age $K^2$. At insertion its persistent radius $V\in[r,2r]$ has density $$\label{eq:tier-radius-density}
 f(v)=\frac{\lambda}{r}
       \frac{e^{-\lambda(v/r-1)}}{1-e^{-\lambda}}
       \mathbf 1_{[r,2r]}(v),
 \qquad \lambda=\log(1+K^2).$$ All entry radii and all lifetime trials are mutually independent. Fresh tape blocks are indexed by level, structure, and insertion ordinal, not by the recyclable label slots. The tier key at a point is the label of its earliest present covering entry, if any. The overall level key is the heavy key if present, otherwise the first applicable tier key, otherwise a fixed singleton key for that point. On a step, do the heavy edit first, followed by the roster edits in increasing tier order; a roster’s retirements and insertion form one edit. Each edit is evaluated at the old physical member positions.

Fixing the entire true input makes all comparator posteriors, heavy-center schedules, roster insertion schedules, and chronological ages deterministic. The schedules use neither radii nor lifetimes, nor allocation or rounding states. In particular conditioning on actual presences still leaves the entry radii independent with law <a href="#eq:tier-radius-density" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:tier-radius-density">[eq:tier-radius-density]</a>.

<a id="stmt-lem-roster-count"></a>
<div id="lem:roster-count" class="lemma">

**Lemma 23** (Local age count). *In a subset of $Y$ of diameter at most $20r$, there are at most $A$ chronological entries of age less than the positive integer $A$. In particular the bounds are $K$ and $K^2$ at the two age cutoffs, regardless of how many entries have already retired.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-roster-count"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-roster-count</summary>

*Proof.* If there were $n>A$ such entries, the earliest of them would have at least $n-1\ge A$ later insertions within $20r$ of its center, contradicting its age. Repeated centers cause no exception. ◻

</details>

When a request qualifies, its final tier covers $x$ deterministically. Indeed an insertion supplies an age-zero center at $x$; if there is no insertion, the test supplies an entry of age less than $K$ within $r/2$, and no ages change. That entry is present and its radius is at least $r$. We shall refer to this as the tier’s guaranteed coverage.

<a id="stmt-lem-fixed-alphabet"></a>
<div id="lem:fixed-alphabet" class="lemma">

**Lemma 24** (Fixed labels and anchors). *The construction can use fixed finite label pools at each level, independent of the input law and horizon. Labels can be assigned so that distinct current records have distinct labels, including shadowed records, and a freshly chosen label was absent throughout the preceding step and all earlier intermediate states of the current step. The sole exception is the prescribed heavy-ball reuse. Every point with a given current label is within $20r$ of its anchor; its cell has diameter at most $40r$. A label common to consecutive maps has the same anchor or is a reused heavy label with anchor displacement at most $40r$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-fixed-alphabet"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-fixed-alphabet</summary>

*Proof.* Write $n=|Y|$. Applying Lemma <a href="#lem:roster-count" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:roster-count">23</a> to each singleton shows that a tier has at most $M=nK^2$ chronological records of age less than $K^2$. Reserve a record’s label until that age cutoff, even if its actual lifetime has ended or its ball is completely shadowed by earlier structures. This convention bounds all reserved labels by $M$; it does not alter the partition. During a step at most one new record is inserted. The records present at any intermediate time during two consecutive steps belong to their two starting record sets and at most two new records, so their total number is at most $2M+2$. A pool of $4M+4$ slots suffices to choose a fresh slot outside this entire set whenever needed. A slot released on the previous step is still excluded on the current step. This is a finite implementation of the stated cooldown rule, not a growing list of all historical labels.

The heavy structure has at most $n$ centers and one insertion per step, so a separate pool of $4n+4$ slots works by the same rule, with reuse allowed precisely for the unique intersected ball. Use disjoint pools for different tiers and the heavy structure, and $n$ additional fixed singleton labels. Pool sizes are chosen for every level in advance and do not depend on the realized center schedule. A chronological record that persists keeps its label and center even while shadowed. Thus any common slot can only change its anchor by the explicit heavy reuse.

Heavy and tier keys are anchored at their centers, and singleton keys at their points. Every key cell is a subset of its defining ball, whose radius is at most $20r$; restricting by precedence only shrinks it. The diameter and anchor bounds follow. Heavy precedence further implies that a heavy key’s region is its entire ball. Empty key regions cause no difficulty; their reserved anchors and slots are still defined. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-44"></a>
#### Separation in the final map

<a id="stmt-lem-first-hit"></a>
<div id="lem:first-hit" class="lemma">

**Lemma 25** (First hit). *Fix a true input, a roster at a fixed step, and points $x,y$ with $d=d(x,y)<r/4$. The probability that the earliest present ball covering either point covers exactly one is at most $Chd/r$. The expectation is over the partition tape.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-first-hit"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-first-hit</summary>

<a id="eq:radius-separation-tail"></a>
*Proof.* Condition on all presences, leaving the radii independent. For a center $c$, put $a=\min\{d(c,x),d(c,y)\}$ and $p_a=\Pr(V\ge a)$. The two distances differ by at most $d$, so separation requires $V\in[a,a+d]$, up to endpoints of probability zero. If $r\le a\le2r$, direct integration of <a href="#eq:tier-radius-density" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:tier-radius-density">[eq:tier-radius-density]</a> gives $$f(a)=\frac{\lambda}{r}
       \left(p_a+\frac1{e^\lambda-1}\right).$$ The density is decreasing, hence $$\label{eq:radius-separation-tail}
 \Pr\bigl(V\in[a,a+d]\bigr)
 \le\frac{\lambda d}{r}
       \left(p_a+\frac1{e^\lambda-1}\right).$$ For $a<r$, use the maximum density at $r$ and $p_a=1$; for $a>2r$, the left side is zero. Thus the same bound holds when an interval crosses a support endpoint.

List the present balls in chronological order and let $q_i$ be the probability all balls preceding ball $i$ miss both points. The event that ball $i$ hits at least one has probability $p_{a_i}$, so independence gives $$\sum_i q_i p_{a_i}
 =\Pr(\text{some present ball hits }x\text{ or }y)\le1.$$ Every potentially hitting center lies within $2r$ of one of the points. Those centers have diameter at most $4r+d<5r$, and all present entries have age less than $K^2$. There are therefore at most $K^2$ candidates by Lemma <a href="#lem:roster-count" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:roster-count">23</a>. Multiplying <a href="#eq:radius-separation-tail" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:radius-separation-tail">[eq:radius-separation-tail]</a> by $q_i$ and summing gives $$\Pr(\text{separating first hit})
 \le\frac{\lambda d}{r}
       \left(1+\frac{K^2}{e^\lambda-1}\right)
 =\frac{2\lambda d}{r}.$$ Finally $\lambda=\log(1+K^2)\le Ch$, since $C_K$ is an absolute constant and $h\ge1$. The bound is uniform in the conditioned presences, so it also holds without that conditioning. ◻

</details>

<a id="eq:final-map-separation"></a>
Let $\xi_{t,j}$ denote the final overall key at level $j$. For every fixed input and every $y\in Y$, $$\label{eq:final-map-separation}
 \mathbb E_{\rm tape}\sum_{j=1}^Jr_j
 \mathbf 1_{\{\xi_{t,j}(x)\ne\xi_{t,j}(y)\}}
 \le C\ell d(x,y).$$ To prove this, put $d=d(x,y)$; the case $d=0$ is immediate. The radii $r_j\le4d$ contribute at most $C d$ by geometric summation. At a larger heavy level a final center lies within $8r$ of $x$, so its ball of radius at least $16r$ contains both points. There is agreement regardless of its sampled radius.

At a larger nonheavy level, the centers of heavy balls that could contain one of the two points lie within $20r$ of their union, a set of diameter at most $40r+d<41r$. There is at most one such center. Its uniform radius separates the two points with probability at most $d/(4r)$. Next let $h_*(j)$ be the first qualifying tier at this level. It supplies guaranteed coverage of $x$. If no heavy ball separates the points and no tier through $h_*(j)$ has a separating first hit, their overall keys agree: the first structure hitting either hits both, and such a structure exists by guaranteed coverage. Lemma <a href="#lem:first-hit" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:first-hit">25</a> and a union bound therefore give probability at most $$\frac{Cd}{r}\left(1+\sum_{h\le h_*(j)}h\right)
 \le\frac{Cd}{r}(1+L_j(x)).$$ Indeed the dyadic sum is less than $2h_*(j)$, and the first dyadic value at least $L_j(x)$ is at most $2\max\{1,L_j(x)\}$. After multiplying by $r$, sum the additive ones only over nonheavy levels and the logarithms by <a href="#eq:partition-log-sums" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:partition-log-sums">[eq:partition-log-sums]</a>. This proves <a href="#eq:final-map-separation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:final-map-separation">[eq:final-map-separation]</a>. In particular the argument does not multiply the number of nonheavy levels by a worst-case tier coefficient; the individual coefficients telescope through the local mass ratios.

<a id="pm-h-45"></a>
#### Noncoverage and the cost of roster edits

<a id="stmt-lem-tier-edits"></a>
<div id="lem:tier-edits" class="lemma">

**Lemma 26** (Tier edits). *The expected sum of $r_j$ times overall level-key changes due to roster edits, evaluated at the old physical positions in the stated edit order, is at most $C\ell Q+D_L'$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-tier-edits"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-tier-edits</summary>

*Proof.* For each level and tier use the nonnegative potential $$\Phi_{j,h}=r_j\sum_{a=1}^k
 \mathbf 1_{\{X^a\text{ is not covered by that tier}\}},$$ ignoring all other structures in the definition of coverage. Telescope from step end to step end, so the potential includes physical movement. The sum of initial values is at most $k|\mathcal H_k|\sum_jr_j$, an allowed fixed initialization bound.

<a id="eq:unqualified-tier-sum"></a>
If there is no insertion, the roster has no edit. Stationary members give zero potential change, and only the mover $y\to x$ can increase the potential. This requires $y$ covered and $x$ uncovered. It is impossible in a qualified tier, by guaranteed coverage. In an unqualified tier, Lemma <a href="#lem:first-hit" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:first-hit">25</a> gives expected positive increase at most $Chd(x,y)$ when $r>4d(x,y)$. For $r\le4d(x,y)$, the bound follows from the trivial increase at most $r\le4h d(x,y)$. Unqualification says $h<L_j(x)$, and $$\label{eq:unqualified-tier-sum}
 \sum_{j=1}^J\sum_{h\in\mathcal H_k:\,h<L_j(x)}h
 \le2\sum_{j=1}^J L_j(x)\le C\ell.$$ Thus all noninsertion increases cost at most $C\ell Q$.

At an insertion, let $C_a\in\{0,r\}$ be a member’s overall key charge caused by this tier edit at its old position. Let $U_-$ be old tier noncoverage at that position and $U_+$ final tier noncoverage at its final physical position. In particular $U_+=0$ for the mover, because the newly inserted ball covers $x$. The following table accounts for the entire quantity $C_a+r(U_+-U_-)$.

<div class="center">

| Old tier status               | Stationary member                                  | Designated mover                                 |
|:------------------------------|:---------------------------------------------------|:-------------------------------------------------|
| First covering entry persists | $C_a=0$, $U_-=U_+=0$                               | $C_a=0$, $U_-=U_+=0$                             |
| No covering entry             | $C_a\le r(1-U_+)$, $U_-=1$; total at most zero     | $C_a\le r$, $U_-=1$, $U_+=0$; total at most zero |
| First covering entry retires  | $C_a\le r$, $U_-=0$, $U_+\le1$; total at most $2r$ | $C_a\le r$, $U_-=U_+=0$; total at most $r$       |

</div>

For the first row, new entries are appended after all existing ones, so a surviving first coverer keeps the tier key unchanged. Other structures are held fixed during this edit. For the second row, an overall key can change only if this previously uncovered point becomes covered by the tier. These facts also hold when an earlier structure shadows the tier, in which case its charge may simply be zero.

There is a further zero-error case at a heavy level. If the old position lies in $B(x,\gamma_Hr)$, the final heavy ball already covers it during all tier edits. Hence $C_a=0$. The new tier ball covers that position if it is stationary, and covers the final point $x$ if it is the mover. Thus $U_+=0$ and no retirement error is needed in this inner ball.

<a id="eq:actual-retirement-bound"></a>
We next bound the probability of the exceptional retirement row. Fix the full true input and a point $p$. Conditional on the old realized presences and radii, a pre-edit first coverer at a noncompulsory age can be removed on this step only by its fresh independent trial, of probability at most $1/K$. For compulsory removal use an unconditional bound over the tape. At most $K^2$ chronological candidates of age less than $K^2$ have centers within $2r$ of $p$. A candidate reaching age $K^2$ must previously have survived the $K^2-K$ trials at ages $K,\ldots,K^2-1$. Consequently $$\label{eq:actual-retirement-bound}
 \Pr_{\rm tape}(\text{the old first coverer of }p\text{ retires})
 \le\frac1K+K^2(1-1/K)^{K^2-K}\le\frac{C}{K}.$$ The last inequality follows, for example, from $K^2(1-1/K)^{K^2-K}\le K^2e^{-(K-1)}\le C/K$. If $d(p,x)>22r$, no covering entry can have its age incremented: its center is within $2r$ of $p$ but would have to be within $20r$ of $x$. The retirement probability is then zero. The compulsory part of <a href="#eq:actual-retirement-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:actual-retirement-bound">[eq:actual-retirement-bound]</a> is not asserted conditional on an observed surviving old roster; it averages over the independent tape with the chronological schedule fixed.

<a id="eq:tier-insertion-mass"></a>
For an insertion at time $t$, level $j$, and tier $h$, write $$\label{eq:tier-insertion-mass}
 W_{t,j,h}=
 \begin{cases}
 \mu^+\{p:\gamma_Hr<d(p,x)\le D_Hr\},&\text{if heavy},\\
 \mu^+(B(x,D_Lr)),&\text{if nonheavy}.
 \end{cases}$$ Although the value at a fixed step and level does not depend on $h$, the indices specify the insertion being charged. The stationary errors are confined to this region, since $D_H>22$, and the heavy inner ball has zero error.
