Average <a href="#eq:actual-retirement-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:actual-retirement-bound">[eq:actual-retirement-bound]</a> first over the tape with the full input fixed, and then over input continuations given the true prefix. The insertion event and $W_{t,j,h}$ are fixed by that prefix. The expected stationary counting measure is $\mu^+-\delta_x\le\mu^+$. Thus the expected stationary error is at most $CrW_{t,j,h}/K$. This averaging statement is conditional only on the true prefix, not on the complete partition tape in $\mathcal F_t$.

<a id="eq:insertion-charge-budget"></a>
At a nonheavy insertion, the mover’s error is also covered by $CrW_{t,j,h}/K$, since $W_{t,j,h}\ge\mu^+(\{x\})\ge1$. At a heavy insertion, its remaining error is at most $Cr/K$ and only occurs if $d(y,x)>\gamma_Hr$. For each tier, $$\sum_{r_j<d(x,y)/\gamma_H}\frac{Cr_j}{K_h}
 \le\frac{C d(x,y)}{K_h},\qquad
 \sum_{h\in\mathcal H_k}\frac1{K_h}\le C.$$ Here the fixed factor $1/\gamma_H$ is absorbed into $C$. These mover errors therefore cost at most $CQ$. It remains to prove $$\label{eq:insertion-charge-budget}
 \mathbb E\sum_{t,j,h:\,\text{insertion}}\frac{r_jW_{t,j,h}}{K_h}
 \le C\ell Q+D_{\mathrm{ins}}.$$

<a id="pm-h-46"></a>
###### An auxiliary short roster.

We need a potential drop at an insertion to pay the remaining insertion charge. The insertion test excludes young records near $x$, but an older surviving ball may still cover $x$. A potential built from the actual surviving centers would therefore have no guaranteed change there. Instead we use an auxiliary roster containing only entries of age less than $K$. The insertion test then forces a vacant neighborhood of $x$ before the new entry arrives. Random retirement before the age cutoff keeps the expected increases elsewhere small.

<a id="eq:short-roster-g"></a>
This auxiliary roster uses the same deterministic insertion records and ages. An entry starts present at age zero, has independent retirement probability $1/\sqrt K$ at each subsequent age increment, and retires compulsorily at age $K$. Its sole output is the deterministic expected function below; it does not enter the partition. Let $\rho_L$ be the clipped increasing ramp on $[1/8,1/4]$, taking value one at infinity, and set $$\label{eq:short-roster-g}
 g(p)=\mathbb E_{\rm short}
       \rho_L\bigl(d(p,\mathcal S)/r\bigr),$$ where $\mathcal S$ is the set of surviving hypothetical centers. The expectation is over these auxiliary lifetimes, given the chronological schedule. The function $g$ lies in $[0,1]$ and has $r\mathop{\mathrm{Lip}}(g)\le8$; it is a deterministic function of the true prefix, and is unchanged at noninsertions.

<a id="eq:short-retirement-bound"></a>
<a id="eq:short-positive-support"></a>
At an insertion the test excludes all old centers of age less than $K$ within $r/2$ of $x$. Every hypothetical survivor has age less than $K$. Hence before insertion $g=1$ throughout $B(x,r/8)$, while the new age-zero entry gives $g=0$ there afterward. Elsewhere couple old and new hypothetical lifetimes by continuing the old trials. If the old ramp at $p$ can increase, a nearest surviving center within $r/4$ of $p$, selected by a fixed tie rule, must retire. Its noncompulsory fresh trial has probability $1/\sqrt K$. There are at most $K$ possible compulsory candidates within $r/4$ of $p$ by Lemma <a href="#lem:roster-count" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:roster-count">23</a>, and a candidate reaching age $K$ must have survived $K-1$ previous trials. Thus $$\label{eq:short-retirement-bound}
 (g_{\mathrm{new}}(p)-g_{\mathrm{old}}(p))_+
 \le\varepsilon_K:=K^{-1/2}+K(1-K^{-1/2})^{K-1}
 \le C K^{-1/2}.$$ The last bound follows by comparing the second term with $K\exp(-(K-1)/\sqrt K)$; its ratio to $K^{-1/2}$ is bounded over integers $K\ge2$. Only centers within $20r$ of $x$ change age. Therefore positive changes of $g$ are supported in $$\label{eq:short-positive-support}
 B(x,21r)\setminus B(x,r/8).$$ The auxiliary estimate is $O(K^{-1/2})$, whereas the bound used for actual retirements is $O(K^{-1})$. The exponentially large choice of $K_h$ will make the auxiliary error small enough compared with the insertion gain.

<a id="pm-h-47"></a>
###### The insertion gain in the pilot.

<a id="eq:tier-candidate-change"></a>
Fix an insertion, abbreviate $W=W_{t,j,h}$, and let $z$ be an old minimizer of the second-template pilot at $\mu^+$ and $g_{\mathrm{old}}$. Its residual $R_z(p)=1-\sum_s z_s b_s(p)$ lies in $[0,1]$. The change of its objective when $g$ is edited is exactly $$\label{eq:tier-candidate-change}
 r\int\left[
   \Delta g(p)R_z(p)+256\sum_s z_s\Delta g(s)a_s(p)
 \right]d\mu^+(p).$$ At a nonheavy level, its positive part is at most $CrW/\sqrt K$. Indeed the residual increase is supported in $B(x,21r)$. Any increasing-coefficient site lies in that same ball, and its surcharge support lies in $B(x,(21+2R)r)\subset B(x,D_Lr)$. At any test point the weights of all sites with nonzero $a_s$ sum to at most one, by the pilot capacity constraint and $2R<D_*$. Now apply <a href="#eq:short-retirement-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:short-retirement-bound">[eq:short-retirement-bound]</a> to both terms of <a href="#eq:tier-candidate-change" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:tier-candidate-change">[eq:tier-candidate-change]</a>.

At a heavy level the same bound holds with the annular definition of $W$, rather than the possibly much larger inner mass. To see this, every possible increasing-coefficient site satisfies $r/8<d(s,x)\le21r$. Since $1/8-\gamma_H>\sigma$ and $21+\gamma_H<R$, its bump $a_s$ equals one throughout $B(x,\gamma_Hr)$. Its $b_s$-support lies inside $B(x,D_Hr)$. Writing $M_H=\mu^+(B(x,\gamma_Hr))$, the coefficient of $z_s$ in the old objective, divided by $r$, is at least $$256M_H-2\mu^+(B(x,D_Hr))
 \ge[256-2(1+\delta_H)]M_H>0.$$ Deleting that weight preserves both capacity and separation constraints, so optimality forces $z_s=0$. Hence no surcharge increases in <a href="#eq:tier-candidate-change" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:tier-candidate-change">[eq:tier-candidate-change]</a>; the residual increases only in <a href="#eq:short-positive-support" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:short-positive-support">[eq:short-positive-support]</a>, a subset of the heavy annulus in <a href="#eq:tier-insertion-mass" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:tier-insertion-mass">[eq:tier-insertion-mass]</a>. Its positive contribution is again at most $CrW/\sqrt K$.

It remains to exhibit the negative contribution. Put $M_L=\mu^+(B(x,\gamma_Lr))$. Qualification and the definition of $W$ give, in both the heavy and nonheavy cases, <a id="eq:insertion-inner-mass"></a>$$\label{eq:insertion-inner-mass}
 M_L\ge e^{-h}\mu^+(B(x,D_Lr))\ge e^{-h}W.$$ There are two exhaustive cases.

If some site $s$ within $3\sigma r$ of $x$ has $z_s\ge1/2$, then $g(s)$ drops from one to zero because $3\sigma<1/8$. On the entire heavy annulus $\gamma_Hr<d(p,x)\le D_Hr$, the bump $a_s(p)$ equals one: the lower distance is greater than $\gamma_H-3\sigma>\sigma$ in scale units, and the upper distance is less than $D_H+3\sigma<R$. Its surcharge drop is at least $128rW$ when the request is heavy. When it is nonheavy, failure of <a href="#eq:heavy-condition" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:heavy-condition">[eq:heavy-condition]</a> says that this annulus has mass greater than $\delta_H M_H\ge\delta_H M_L$. The drop is then at least $128\delta_HrM_L\ge128\delta_Hre^{-h}W$.

If no such site exists, then for every $p\in B(x,\gamma_Lr)$, any contributing $b_s(p)$ has its center within $(2\sigma+\gamma_L)r<3\sigma r$ of $x$, and has weight less than $1/2$. At most one positive site contributes there, since two centers within $2\sigma r$ of the same point would violate their $5\sigma r$ separation. It follows that $R_z(p)\ge1/2$ on this inner ball. The unit drop of $g(p)$ there gives a residual decrease at least $rM_L/2\ge re^{-h}W/2$.

<a id="eq:tier-pilot-drop"></a>
<a id="eq:weighted-insertion-drop"></a>
Combining either negative contribution with the established positive error, and using the old optimum as a candidate for the new minimum, gives absolute $c,C>0$ such that $$\label{eq:tier-pilot-drop}
 \mathcal L_r(\mu^+,g_{\mathrm{old}})
 -\mathcal L_r(\mu^+,g_{\mathrm{new}})
 \ge rW\left(ce^{-h}-\frac C{\sqrt K}\right).$$ Choose $C_K$ sufficiently large, once and for all, that for every $h\ge1$, $$C e^{-C_Kh/2}\le(c/2)e^{-h},\qquad
 e^{-C_Kh}\le(c/2)e^{-2h}.$$ Since $K\ge e^{C_Kh}$, multiplying <a href="#eq:tier-pilot-drop" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:tier-pilot-drop">[eq:tier-pilot-drop]</a> by $e^{-h}$ yields $$\label{eq:weighted-insertion-drop}
 e^{-h}\bigl[
 \mathcal L_r(\mu^+,g_{\mathrm{old}})
 -\mathcal L_r(\mu^+,g_{\mathrm{new}})\bigr]
 \ge(c/2)e^{-2h}rW\ge\frac{rW}{K}.$$

For each tier apply Corollary <a href="#cor:pilot-edits" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cor:pilot-edits">20</a> with its held function <a href="#eq:short-roster-g" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:short-roster-g">[eq:short-roster-g]</a> and fixed weight $e^{-h}$. The template and Lipschitz constants are the same for all tiers, and $\sum_{h\in\mathcal H_k}e^{-h}\le\sum_{n\ge1}e^{-n}<\infty$. Thus the filtering and summed drift budgets remain $C\ell Q$, with no factor equal to the number of tiers. At noninsertions there is no parameter edit. At insertions <a href="#eq:weighted-insertion-drop" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:weighted-insertion-drop">[eq:weighted-insertion-drop]</a> pays exactly the desired charges. The potentials are nonnegative and their total initial value is at most $2k\sum_jr_j\sum_he^{-h}$. This proves <a href="#eq:insertion-charge-budget" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:insertion-charge-budget">[eq:insertion-charge-budget]</a>.

Finally sum the per-member case table over all insertions, add the noninsertion estimate <a href="#eq:unqualified-tier-sum" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:unqualified-tier-sum">[eq:unqualified-tier-sum]</a>, and telescope the noncoverage potentials. The expected retirement errors are covered by <a href="#eq:insertion-charge-budget" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:insertion-charge-budget">[eq:insertion-charge-budget]</a> and the heavy-mover geometric sum. Their terminal nonnegative values can be discarded. This proves the lemma with an initialization bound independent of the law and horizon. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-48"></a>
#### The fixed tree and its posterior interface

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-tier-edits-2"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-tier-edits</summary>

*Proof of Proposition <a href="#prop:embedded-cost" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:embedded-cost">21</a>.* Let $\mathcal A_j$ be the fixed level alphabet in Lemma <a href="#lem:fixed-alphabet" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:fixed-alphabet">24</a>, and take the tree of all prefixes of words in $\mathcal A_1\times\cdots\times\mathcal A_J$, including unused words. Initially the center structures are empty and the map uses singleton labels. At every step map a point to its complete string of final keys. The last-level cell diameter is at most $40r_J$, less than the minimum nonzero distance of $Y$; hence last-level cells are singletons, giving injectivity and a requested leaf with anchor exactly equal to the actual request.

<a id="eq:key-to-tree-charge"></a>
For any two words $u,v$, $$\label{eq:key-to-tree-charge}
 d_{\mathcal T}(u,v)
 \le\tau\sum_{j=1}^Jr_j\mathbf 1_{\{u_j\ne v_j\}}.$$ Indeed, if their first disagreement is $j$, the left side is $r_{j-1}=\tau r_j$. For each member on each step, first edit all level keys at its old physical position, and then move it to its new position under the final map. The triangle inequality for key disagreements bounds the right side of <a href="#eq:key-to-tree-charge" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:key-to-tree-charge">[eq:key-to-tree-charge]</a> by the heavy edit charges, all tier edit charges, and the final-map physical disagreements. The first two have budgets $C\ell Q+D$ by Lemmas <a href="#lem:heavy-edits" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:heavy-edits">22</a> and <a href="#lem:tier-edits" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:tier-edits">26</a>. Only the designated mover has a nonzero final physical disagreement; <a href="#eq:final-map-separation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:final-map-separation">[eq:final-map-separation]</a>, applied after fixing the true input and hence that member’s old position, bounds its expected charge by $C\ell d(x,y)$. Averaging over inputs and summing proves <a href="#eq:embedded-cost" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:embedded-cost">[eq:embedded-cost]</a>; $\tau$ is absolute.

All initialized potentials above are bounded by fixed multiples of $k(1+|\mathcal H_k|)\sum_jr_j$. Their number of time updates never enters the bound. The finite pools and the full prefix tree likewise depend only on $Y,k$ and the chosen absolute constants. Consequently the additive constant is uniform over input laws and horizons. ◻

</details>

For completeness, the coupling used by the allocation theorem is now fully specified. Put $$N_v(t)=\#\{a:\xi_t(X_t^a)\text{ has prefix }v\},\qquad
 m_v(t)=\mathbb E[N_v(t)\mid\mathcal F_t].$$ The final map and the requested leaf are available from the observed true prefix and independent partition tape before virtual service. The requested leaf contains the designated comparator member after drift, with conditional probability one. The filtering substep at time $t$ uses $N_v(t-1)$ with the *old* map $\xi_{t-1}$, comparing its laws given $\mathcal F_{t-1}$ and $\mathcal F_t$. The drift substep compares that same old-map variable with $N_v(t)$, both conditioned on $\mathcal F_t$, along the hidden labeled coupling whose expected cost is <a href="#eq:embedded-cost" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:embedded-cost">[eq:embedded-cost]</a>. In particular the allocation drift budgets are paid by this $P$. No ball-separation or survival probability is asserted after conditioning on the complete tape; those estimates have already been averaged to establish $P$.

For a finite horizon the partition outcomes can, if desired, be encoded on a finite probability space: on finite $Y$, a radius is used only through its ball subset, so its continuous law has finitely many relevant outcomes, and only finitely many insertions and lifetime trials occur. The expected short-roster functions require no additional random observations. The construction therefore meets the finite-data requirements of Theorem <a href="#thm:allocation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:allocation">5</a>.

Apply that theorem once to the fixed full tree. Its trackers continue at every vertex when a label becomes unused and later reappears; they are not restarted when a slot is recycled. The resulting bound is $V\le C\ell P+D_1$ with a single fixed-tree initialization bound. Partition schedules use only the true-prefix posteriors and chronological ages, so neither these allocations nor the independent rounding randomness feed back into the partition construction.

<a id="sec:travel"></a>
### Travel of parked mass

The allocation theorem controls movement between tree vertices. A second cost arises when a vertex retains its key but that key acquires a new anchor in the metric. We pay this cost using the comparator mass in the key, normalized by a slowly updated estimate of its size. The estimate is essential: keeping its coefficient fixed during filtering allows us to use conditional expectation without paying the absolute variation of a posterior.

Throughout this section the tree, label pools, and maps are those of Section <a href="#sec:partitions" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:partitions">7</a>, and the allocations are supplied by Theorem <a href="#thm:allocation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:allocation">5</a>. A state at time $t$ means the state after all updates for that request. Write $\mu_t$ for the true-metric posterior counting measure at this state. All multiplicative constants below are absolute. Additive constants may depend on the fixed finite metric, $k$, the scales, and the fixed label pools, but not on the input law or its horizon.

<a id="pm-h-50"></a>
#### Parking and valid representatives

<a id="eq:park-definition"></a>
<a id="eq:park-subtree-total"></a>
For every tree vertex, including the root and leaves, define its parked mass by $$\label{eq:park-definition}
 p_{t,v}^{\circ}=q_{t,v}-\sum_{i\text{ child of }v}q_{t,i}.$$ The empty sum at a leaf is zero. Allocation feasibility makes these numbers nonnegative. Telescoping within a subtree gives $$\label{eq:park-subtree-total}
 \sum_{w\text{ descendant of }v}p_{t,w}^{\circ}=q_{t,v},
 \qquad \sum_v p_{t,v}^{\circ}=k,$$ where a vertex counts as its own descendant.

<a id="stmt-lem-park-variation"></a>
<div id="lem:park-variation" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:park-variation"></a>
**Lemma 27** (Parked variation and witnesses). *At every step, $$\label{eq:park-variation}
 \sum_v r_{|v|}\lvert\Delta p_v^{\circ}\rvert
 \le \left(1+\frac1\tau\right)
       \sum_{v\ne\mathrm{root}}r_{|v|-1}\lvert\Delta q_v\rvert.$$ If $q_{t,v}>0$, there is a point of $Y$ whose current key string has prefix $v$. Such a point is called a validity witness for $v$. If $|v|=j\ge1$, every validity witness is within $20r_j$ of the anchor of the last key of $v$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-park-variation"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-park-variation</summary>

*Proof.* Taking differences in <a href="#eq:park-definition" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:park-definition">[eq:park-definition]</a> and using the triangle inequality gives $$\begin{aligned}
 \sum_v r_{|v|}\lvert\Delta p_v^{\circ}\rvert
 &\le \sum_v r_{|v|}\lvert\Delta q_v\rvert
     +\sum_v r_{|v|}\sum_{i\text{ child of }v}
                                  \lvert\Delta q_i\rvert\\
 &=\frac1\tau\sum_{v\ne\mathrm{root}}
                 r_{|v|-1}\lvert\Delta q_v\rvert
   +\sum_{i\ne\mathrm{root}}r_{|i|-1}\lvert\Delta q_i\rvert.
\end{aligned}$$ The root term in the first sum vanishes because its allocation is always $k$. This proves <a href="#eq:park-variation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:park-variation">[eq:park-variation]</a>, including the root’s parked mass. The bound $q_{t,v}\le C_0m_{t,v}$ implies $m_{t,v}>0$ whenever $q_{t,v}>0$. Since $Y$ is finite, some point mapped below $v$ then has positive posterior counting mass. It is a witness. The anchor-distance assertion is part of Lemma <a href="#lem:fixed-alphabet" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:fixed-alphabet">24</a>: a level key is a heavy ball of radius at most $20r_j$, a smaller roster ball, or a singleton. ◻

</details>

Represent a park at a nonroot vertex by its last key’s anchor. Represent the root by one fixed point of $Y$. A balanced integer rounding of $p_{t,v}^{\circ}$ can be positive only if $p_{t,v}^{\circ}>0$, since the floor and ceiling of zero are both zero. Thus the same witness statement will apply to occupied integer parks in Section <a href="#sec:rounding" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:rounding">9</a>.

<a id="eq:label-park-domination"></a>
Let $\Lambda_j^H$ be the fixed pool of heavy-label slots at level $j$. For $\lambda\in\Lambda_j^H$, let $C_{t,j,\lambda}\subseteq Y$ be the points currently assigned that level key, and let $a_{t,j,\lambda}$ be its anchor. An absent label has empty region and an arbitrary fixed placeholder anchor. Heavy precedence and disjointness mean that, when present, $C_{t,j,\lambda}$ is the entire heavy ball carrying the label. Set <a id="eq:label-park-size"></a>$$\label{eq:label-park-size}
 \begin{aligned}
 b_{t,j,\lambda}
   &=\sum_{\substack{|v|=j\\\text{last key of }v=\lambda}}
                                              p_{t,v}^{\circ},\\
 M_{t,j,\lambda}
   &=\sum_{\substack{|v|=j\\\text{last key of }v=\lambda}}m_{t,v}
     =\mu_t(C_{t,j,\lambda}).
 \end{aligned}$$ These definitions use the fixed slots even when they are absent, in which case both quantities are zero. In particular, $$\label{eq:label-park-domination}
 0\le b_{t,j,\lambda}\le C_0 M_{t,j,\lambda},\qquad
 \sum_{j,\lambda\in\Lambda_j^H}r_j\lvert\Delta b_{j,\lambda}\rvert
 \le\sum_v r_{|v|}\lvert\Delta p_v^{\circ}\rvert.$$ The second inequality follows by grouping fixed vertex coordinates, then using the triangle inequality. It also holds across label disappearance and reuse.

<a id="eq:retained-anchor-distance"></a>
Let $E_{t,j,\lambda}$ be the event that the label is present at both successive states and its anchor changes. By Lemma <a href="#lem:fixed-alphabet" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:fixed-alphabet">24</a>, this is an explicit reuse of a heavy label at an intersecting new heavy ball. In particular, $$\label{eq:retained-anchor-distance}
 \mathbf 1_{E_{t,j,\lambda}}
 d(a_{t-1,j,\lambda},a_{t,j,\lambda})
 \le 40r_j\mathbf 1_{E_{t,j,\lambda}}.$$ The quantity to be bounded is <a id="eq:anchor-travel-definition"></a>$$\label{eq:anchor-travel-definition}
 T_{\rm anchor}
   =\mathbb E\sum_{t,j,\lambda\in\Lambda_j^H}
                  r_j b_{t,j,\lambda}\mathbf 1_{E_{t,j,\lambda}}.$$

<a id="stmt-prop-anchor-travel"></a>
<div id="prop:anchor-travel" class="proposition">

<a id="eq:anchor-travel-bound"></a>
**Proposition 28** (Retained-anchor travel). *There is an absolute constant $C$ and a finite constant $D_{\rm anchor}$, independent of the input law and horizon, such that $$\label{eq:anchor-travel-bound}
 T_{\rm anchor}\le C\bigl(V+\ell P+\ell Q\bigr)+D_{\rm anchor}.$$ Consequently the expected cost of moving the new parked masses with their retained anchors is at most $40T_{\rm anchor}$. No multiplicative constant in this statement depends on the number of levels or on $k$.*

</div>

The proof uses a size tracker and a pointwise estimate for its old coefficients. We establish both before analyzing the travel potential.

<a id="pm-h-51"></a>
#### An affine size tracker

<a id="eq:key-size-comparison"></a>
Put $\rho=6/5$. For each fixed heavy-label slot maintain a reference $\widehat m_{t,j,\lambda}\in[1,k+1]$ for $s_{t,j,\lambda}=1+M_{t,j,\lambda}$. Initially set the reference equal to $s_{0,j,\lambda}$. At each subsequent step, after computing the new membership and posterior size, keep the old reference if $$\label{eq:key-size-comparison}
 \rho^{-1}\widehat m_{t-1,j,\lambda}
       \le s_{t,j,\lambda}
       \le\rho\widehat m_{t-1,j,\lambda};$$ otherwise refresh it to $s_{t,j,\lambda}$. Thus every completed state satisfies the same comparison with its current reference. The tracker continues to run while a slot is absent; recycling a slot does not restart it.

<a id="stmt-lem-key-size-refresh"></a>
<div id="lem:key-size-refresh" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:key-size-refresh-budget"></a>
**Lemma 29** (Size-refresh budget). *If $R_{t,j,\lambda}$ denotes a refresh, then $$\label{eq:key-size-refresh-budget}
 \mathbb E\sum_{t,j,\lambda\in\Lambda_j^H}
 r_j\bigl(\widehat m_{t-1,j,\lambda}
            +\widehat m_{t,j,\lambda}\bigr)\mathbf 1_{R_{t,j,\lambda}}
 \le C\ell P+D_{\rm size},$$ where $C$ is absolute and $D_{\rm size}$ depends only on the fixed metric, scales, $k$, and label pools.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-key-size-refresh"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-key-size-refresh</summary>

<a id="eq:entropy-relative-credit"></a>
*Proof.* The payment is a tangent gap, equivalently the Bregman divergence for the convex function $s\log s$ (Bregman 1967) <a id="pm-cite-29"></a>[[Bregman67](#ref-Bregman67)]. For $u,s\in[1,k+1]$, the tangent at $u$ to $f(s)=-s\log s$ is <a id="eq:entropy-tangent"></a>$$\label{eq:entropy-tangent}
 T_u(s)=f(u)+f'(u)(s-u)=u-(1+\log u)s.$$ It is affine in $s$, with slope of absolute value at most $\ell$. A refresh $u\to s$ releases <a id="eq:entropy-refresh-credit"></a>$$\label{eq:entropy-refresh-credit}
 T_u(s)-T_s(s)=s\log(s/u)-s+u
       \ge\frac{(s-u)^2}{2\max(s,u)}.$$ For completeness, if $s\ge u$ the left side equals $\int_u^s(s-z)z^{-1}\,dz$, and if $s\le u$ it equals $\int_s^u(z-s)z^{-1}\,dz$. Bounding the denominator by $\max(s,u)$ gives the inequality. At failure of <a href="#eq:key-size-comparison" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:key-size-comparison">[eq:key-size-comparison]</a>, $|s-u|\ge(1-\rho^{-1})\max(s,u)$, so $$\label{eq:entropy-relative-credit}
 T_u(s)-T_s(s)\ge\frac{(1-\rho^{-1})^2}{4}(s+u)
                         =\frac{s+u}{144}.$$

We check the input budget for the weighted sum of these tangents. Let $N_{t,j,\lambda}^{\rm key}$ be the actual number of hidden members whose level-$j$ key at time $t$ is $\lambda$. Filtering replaces $\mathbb E[N_{t-1,j,\lambda}^{\rm key}\mid\mathcal F_{t-1}]$ by $\mathbb E[N_{t-1,j,\lambda}^{\rm key}\mid\mathcal F_t]$, using the old map and old reference. Its signed contribution has conditional expectation zero. Drift then replaces this old hidden count by the new count, with both conditioned on $\mathcal F_t$.

<a id="eq:key-disagreement-sum"></a>
If a member’s two mapped leaves first differ at level $j_0$, the radius sum of its possible level-key disagreements is at most $$\label{eq:key-disagreement-sum}
 \sum_{j\ge j_0}r_j
       \le\frac{r_{j_0-1}}{\tau-1}
       =\frac{d_T(\text{old leaf},\text{new leaf})}{\tau-1}.$$ At one level a member changes at most two heavy-slot indicators, one for the departing key and one for the arriving key. Applying this pathwise to the coupled hidden trajectory and then taking conditional expectations gives <a id="eq:key-count-drift"></a>$$\label{eq:key-count-drift}
 \mathbb E\sum_{t,j,\lambda\in\Lambda_j^H}r_j
 \left|M_{t,j,\lambda}
       -\mathbb E[N_{t-1,j,\lambda}^{\rm key}\mid\mathcal F_t]\right|
 \le\frac{2}{\tau-1}P\le2P.$$ Thus the tangents’ expected total signed input increase is at most $2\ell P$.

The tangents need not be nonnegative. Their endpoint values nevertheless have a uniform bound: $$|T_u(s)|\le(k+1)(1+\ell)
       \qquad(1\le u,s\le k+1).$$ Write $L_H=\sum_j r_j|\Lambda_j^H|$, a finite quantity fixed before the input law or horizon is chosen. The absolute value of the summed tangent potential is at most $(k+1)(1+\ell)L_H$ at both endpoints. Telescoping its signed changes over every step, and using <a href="#eq:entropy-relative-credit" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:entropy-relative-credit">[eq:entropy-relative-credit]</a>, proves <a href="#eq:key-size-refresh-budget" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:key-size-refresh-budget">[eq:key-size-refresh-budget]</a>, for example with $C=288$ and $D_{\rm size}=288(k+1)(1+\ell)L_H$. Filtering is included on all steps before the decision to refresh; it is not restricted to the adaptively chosen refresh events. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-52"></a>
#### A pointwise bound on old parked coefficients

<a id="eq:individual-park-coefficient"></a>
At a completed state, <a href="#eq:label-park-domination" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:label-park-domination">[eq:label-park-domination]</a> and the size comparison give the individual bound $$\label{eq:individual-park-coefficient}
 \frac{b_{j,\lambda}}{\widehat m_{j,\lambda}}
 \le\rho C_0\frac{M_{j,\lambda}}{1+M_{j,\lambda}}
 \le\rho C_0.$$ Summing this bound over all levels would introduce the height of the tree. The useful additional constraint is that parks at different depths draw on the same subtree allocations. All finer parks near a point can therefore be charged to comparator mass in a slightly larger ball at a coarser scale. Meanwhile a heavy ball whose anchor is near that point contains a smaller ball and bounds the normalization from below. Comparing these two ball masses will give a logarithmic coefficient sum.

<a id="stmt-lem-park-coefficients"></a>
<div id="lem:park-coefficients" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:pointwise-park-coefficients"></a>
**Lemma 30** (Pointwise parked coefficients). *Fix any completed fractional state and any $y\in Y$. At each level whose heavy-center set has a center within $7r_j$ of $y$, let $\lambda_j$ be its label. Such a center is unique. Then $$\label{eq:pointwise-park-coefficients}
 \sum_{\substack{j:\ d(y,a_{j,\lambda_j})\le7r_j}}
                \frac{b_{j,\lambda_j}}{\widehat m_{j,\lambda_j}}
 \le C\ell,$$ with an absolute $C$, independent of the state and the number of levels.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-park-coefficients"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-park-coefficients</summary>

<a id="eq:park-denominator-lower"></a>
*Proof.* Call the levels in the sum selected, and abbreviate their numerator to $b_j$. Distinct heavy centers at one level are separated by more than $100r_j$, proving uniqueness. Their radii are at least $16r_j$; therefore the selected heavy ball contains $B(y,r_j)$. Heavy precedence and the size comparison imply $$\label{eq:park-denominator-lower}
 \widehat m_{j,\lambda_j}
       \ge\rho^{-1}\bigl(1+\mu(B(y,r_j))\bigr).$$ Here $\mu$ is the posterior counting measure of the fixed state.

<a id="eq:cumulative-park-mass"></a>
We also have, for every level $j$, the cumulative capacity estimate $$\label{eq:cumulative-park-mass}
 \sum_{\substack{i\ge j\\i\text{ selected}}}b_i
                      \le C_0\mu(B(y,67r_j)).$$ To prove it, consider each positive park counted on the left. At its depth $i$, a validity witness lies within $20r_i$ of its anchor, hence within $27r_i$ of $y$. Group all these parks by their depth-$j$ ancestor $v$. Their sum in each group is at most $q_v$ by <a href="#eq:park-subtree-total" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:park-subtree-total">[eq:park-subtree-total]</a>. The group has at least one witness $z$ within $27r_i\le27r_j$ of $y$. Every point with prefix $v$ shares its level-$j$ cell with $z$, and that cell has diameter at most $40r_j$. All such points therefore belong to $B(y,67r_j)$. Regions of distinct depth-$j$ prefixes are disjoint, so their posterior counts sum to at most $\mu(B(y,67r_j))$. Summing $q_v\le C_0m_v$ proves <a href="#eq:cumulative-park-mass" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:cumulative-park-mass">[eq:cumulative-park-mass]</a>.

For an explicit scale summation put, at every level, $$A_j=1+\mu(B(y,67r_j)),\qquad B_j=1+\mu(B(y,r_j)).$$ Both are between $1$ and $k+1$. Extend $B_j=k+1$ for $j\le0$. Since $\tau\ge2$, the fixed offset $p=7$ satisfies $\tau^p\ge67$, and hence $A_j\le B_{j-p}$. It follows that <a id="eq:park-log-telescope"></a>$$\label{eq:park-log-telescope}
 \sum_{j=1}^J\log(A_j/B_j)
 \le\sum_{j=1}^J\bigl(\log B_{j-p}-\log B_j\bigr)
 \le p\log(k+1).$$ The final inequality cancels the common middle terms; at most $p$ uncancelled positive terms remain, each at most $\log(k+1)$, and the remaining terms are nonpositive. This includes $J<p$.

There are at most $p\log(k+1)/\log2$ levels with $A_j>2B_j$. Their contribution is $O(\ell)$ by <a href="#eq:individual-park-coefficient" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:individual-park-coefficient">[eq:individual-park-coefficient]</a>. Partition the remaining selected levels into groups $$G_a=\{j:\ j\text{ selected},\ A_j\le2B_j,
                         \ 2^a\le A_j<2^{a+1}\},
 \qquad 0\le a\le\lfloor\log_2(k+1)\rfloor.$$ For a nonempty group let $j_a$ be its smallest, hence coarsest, index. By <a href="#eq:park-denominator-lower" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:park-denominator-lower">[eq:park-denominator-lower]</a>, every denominator in this group is at least $2^{a-1}/\rho$. By <a href="#eq:cumulative-park-mass" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:cumulative-park-mass">[eq:cumulative-park-mass]</a>, $$\sum_{j\in G_a}b_j
  \le C_0\mu(B(y,67r_{j_a}))
  =C_0(A_{j_a}-1)\le C_0 2^{a+1}.$$ Its total coefficient is consequently at most $4\rho C_0$. There are at most $1+\log_2(k+1)$ groups, proving <a href="#eq:pointwise-park-coefficients" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:pointwise-park-coefficients">[eq:pointwise-park-coefficients]</a>. All these statements hold for each individual state and point, with no averaging and no conditioning on a future request. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-53"></a>
#### Paying retained-anchor motion

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-park-coefficients-2"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-park-coefficients</summary>

<a id="eq:anchor-potential"></a>
*Proof of Proposition <a href="#prop:anchor-travel" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:anchor-travel">28</a>.* Use the clipped ramp $$\kappa(u)=
 \begin{cases}
  0,&u\le4,\\
  (u-4)/3,&4<u<7,\\
  1,&u\ge7.
 \end{cases}$$ For a completed state define $$\label{eq:anchor-potential}
 \Phi_t=\sum_{j,\lambda\in\Lambda_j^H}
    r_j\frac{b_{t,j,\lambda}}{\widehat m_{t,j,\lambda}}
    \int_{C_{t,j,\lambda}}
           \kappa\!\left(\frac{d(a_{t,j,\lambda},z)}{r_j}\right)
                                                   \,d\mu_t(z).$$ This is the posterior expectation of the sum of the same terms over the hidden members assigned to each key. The ramp measures mass away from the anchor. When a heavy label is reused at a new request, the concentrated mass is far from its old anchor and close to its new one, so the anchor change decreases the potential. Dividing by the held key size makes this decrease comparable to $r_jb_{t,j,\lambda}$, the travel charge, rather than to its product with comparator mass.

<a id="eq:anchor-potential-endpoints"></a>
The potential is nonnegative. Moreover, at a completed state the normalized integral is at most $\rho$, so $$\label{eq:anchor-potential-endpoints}
 0\le\Phi_t\le\rho\sum_{j,\lambda\in\Lambda_j^H}r_jb_{t,j,\lambda}
                         \le\rho k r_0.$$ The last inequality follows from <a href="#eq:park-subtree-total" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:park-subtree-total">[eq:park-subtree-total]</a>, since each park occurs at just one depth and $r_j\le r_0$.

We now decompose the change of <a href="#eq:anchor-potential" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:anchor-potential">[eq:anchor-potential]</a> in a specified order. This is an accounting order: the current partition and allocation are already available from the fractional history. Old parameters are held until their indicated substep.

<a id="pm-h-54"></a>
###### Filtering with old membership and all old parameters.

In the hidden-member expression for $\Phi_{t-1}$ retain the old locations, old key map, old anchors, old parks, and old references. Replace conditioning on $\mathcal F_{t-1}$ by conditioning on $\mathcal F_t$. The coefficients and the old map are $\mathcal F_{t-1}$-measurable. Thus the tower property makes this signed potential change have conditional expectation zero. We perform this comparison on every step, before any refresh or anchor event is selected.

<a id="pm-h-55"></a>
###### Drift to new membership and new locations.

Use the coupled hidden trajectory, now conditioned on $\mathcal F_t$, but continue to hold all anchors, parks, and size references at their old values. For a member whose level key changes, its total possible positive change among heavy slots is at most $C r_j$, by <a href="#eq:individual-park-coefficient" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:individual-park-coefficient">[eq:individual-park-coefficient]</a> and $0\le\kappa\le1$. Summing <a href="#eq:key-disagreement-sum" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:key-disagreement-sum">[eq:key-disagreement-sum]</a> over members and steps pays these terms by $CP$ in expectation. This includes changes to heavy membership caused by partition edits, regardless of physical movement.

<a id="eq:anchor-unchanged-key-drift"></a>
For unchanged membership in a heavy key, the same old anchor appears in both ramp terms. If the old location is $y$ and the new one is $z$, then $$\label{eq:anchor-unchanged-key-drift}
 r_j\frac{b^{\rm old}_{j,\lambda}}{\widehat m^{\rm old}_{j,\lambda}}
 \left[\kappa\!\left(\frac{d(a^{\rm old}_{j,\lambda},z)}{r_j}\right)
       -\kappa\!\left(\frac{d(a^{\rm old}_{j,\lambda},y)}{r_j}\right)
 \right]_+
 \le\frac{d(y,z)}3
       \frac{b^{\rm old}_{j,\lambda}}{\widehat m^{\rm old}_{j,\lambda}}
       \mathbf 1_{\{d(a^{\rm old}_{j,\lambda},y)<7r_j\}}.$$ Indeed $\kappa$ is $1/3$-Lipschitz and cannot increase from a point where it already equals one. Lemma <a href="#lem:park-coefficients" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:park-coefficients">30</a> bounds the sum of the coefficients on the right by $C\ell$, for every old point $y$ and the old fractional state. This pointwise bound remains valid when that $y$ is sampled in the coupling conditional on the new history; we require no independence between the old mover and the new request. Only the designated comparator mover changes physical position on this step. Summing <a href="#eq:anchor-unchanged-key-drift" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:anchor-unchanged-key-drift">[eq:anchor-unchanged-key-drift]</a> therefore costs at most $C\ell e_x$ conditionally, and at most $C\ell Q$ in total expectation. The entire drift budget is $C(P+\ell Q)$.

<a id="pm-h-56"></a>
###### Refreshing the size references.

At this point each integral uses the new key membership and new posterior locations, but still the old anchor. For one label write this integral as $I$, its new size as $M$, and $s=1+M$. Always $0\le I\le M$. At a refresh from $u=\widehat m^{\rm old}$ to $s$ the change is $$r_j b^{\rm old}I\left(\frac1s-\frac1u\right).$$ If $s\ge u$ this is nonpositive. If $s<u$, its positive part is at most $r_jb^{\rm old}I/s\le r_jb^{\rm old}$. The old completed state gives $b^{\rm old}\le\rho C_0 u$ by <a href="#eq:individual-park-coefficient" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:individual-park-coefficient">[eq:individual-park-coefficient]</a>. Hence all positive refresh jumps are at most a constant times the charge in <a href="#eq:key-size-refresh-budget" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:key-size-refresh-budget">[eq:key-size-refresh-budget]</a>. This argument does not assume that the new integral is controlled by the old denominator.

<a id="pm-h-57"></a>
###### Updating the parked amounts.

<a id="eq:anchor-park-update-budget"></a>
The new size references now satisfy $\widehat m^{\rm new}\ge(1+M)/\rho$, whether refreshed or retained. Consequently $I/\widehat m^{\rm new}\le\rho$. Replacing $b^{\rm old}$ by $b^{\rm new}$ changes the potential by at most $\rho r_j|\Delta b|$ in absolute value. Equations <a href="#eq:label-park-domination" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:label-park-domination">[eq:label-park-domination]</a> and <a href="#eq:park-variation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:park-variation">[eq:park-variation]</a> give the expected total budget $$\label{eq:anchor-park-update-budget}
 \rho\mathbb E\sum_{t,j,\lambda\in\Lambda_j^H}r_j|\Delta b_{j,\lambda}|
                      \le\rho(1+\tau^{-1})V.$$

<a id="pm-h-58"></a>
###### Updating anchors and collecting the travel payment.

An absent final key has zero integral and zero new parked mass, so choosing its placeholder anchor costs nothing. A newly present key had $b^{\rm old}=0$: the fresh-label rule ensures that it was absent from the preceding map. Its anchor initialization can increase the potential by at most $$\rho r_j b^{\rm new}=\rho r_j|\Delta b|,$$ which is another copy of the variation budget <a href="#eq:anchor-park-update-budget" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:anchor-park-update-budget">[eq:anchor-park-update-budget]</a>. A retained key with unchanged anchor contributes zero.

<a id="eq:anchor-heavy-mass-comparison"></a>
It remains to consider a retained moving heavy label at an update for the request $x$, at radius $r=r_j$. Its new anchor is $x$, its old anchor $a$ satisfies $d(a,x)>8r$, and its new key region is the entire new heavy ball $C$. Put $$H=\mu_t(B(x,\gamma_H r)).$$ The served comparator contains $x$, so $H\ge1$. The new heavy ball contains this inner ball and is contained in $B(x,D_Hr)$. The heavy condition <a href="#eq:heavy-condition" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:heavy-condition">[eq:heavy-condition]</a> therefore gives $$\label{eq:anchor-heavy-mass-comparison}
 H\le M^{\rm new}_{j,\lambda}=\mu_t(C)\le(1+\delta_H)H,
 \qquad
 \widehat m^{\rm new}_{j,\lambda}
       \le\rho(1+M^{\rm new}_{j,\lambda})
       \le\rho(2+\delta_H)H.$$ The last inequality is exactly where the inner mass $H\ge1$ is needed; it accounts for the added one in the normalization.

On $B(x,\gamma_Hr)$ the old ramp is one because $d(a,z)>(8-\gamma_H)r>7r$, and the new ramp is zero because $d(x,z)\le\gamma_Hr<4r$. Outside this inner ball but inside $C$, the ramp can increase by at most one, and that region has mass at most $\delta_H H$. Hence changing $a$ to $x$ changes the unnormalized integral by at most $-(1-\delta_H)H$. With the new park and new reference already in place, <a href="#eq:anchor-heavy-mass-comparison" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:anchor-heavy-mass-comparison">[eq:anchor-heavy-mass-comparison]</a> shows that the potential decreases by at least <a id="eq:retained-anchor-drop"></a>$$\label{eq:retained-anchor-drop}
 \frac{1-\delta_H}{\rho(2+\delta_H)}\,r b^{\rm new}_{j,\lambda}
                         \ge\frac14 r b^{\rm new}_{j,\lambda}.$$ Here we used the absolute choices $\gamma_H<1/100$ and $\delta_H<1/100$. Thus this payment is uniform even when the retained park remains at exactly the same tree vertex.

Combining the five substeps, summing signed changes over all times, and taking expectations yields $$\frac14 T_{\rm anchor}
 \le \mathbb E\Phi_0-\mathbb E\Phi_T
       +C(P+\ell Q)+C\ell P+C D_{\rm size}+CV.$$ The filtering contributions have vanished by conditional expectation; the size-refresh estimate pays their later adaptively selected consequences. Use <a href="#eq:anchor-potential-endpoints" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:anchor-potential-endpoints">[eq:anchor-potential-endpoints]</a> and $\ell\ge1$ to obtain <a href="#eq:anchor-travel-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:anchor-travel-bound">[eq:anchor-travel-bound]</a>, with an additive constant bounded by $C(D_{\rm size}+kr_0)$. Finally, <a href="#eq:retained-anchor-distance" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:retained-anchor-distance">[eq:retained-anchor-distance]</a> proves the asserted bound for actual anchor displacement.

The only scale summations used were the geometric sum <a href="#eq:key-disagreement-sum" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:key-disagreement-sum">[eq:key-disagreement-sum]</a> and the fixed-offset and dyadic sums in Lemma <a href="#lem:park-coefficients" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:park-coefficients">30</a>. Their constants are independent of the height. The finite label pools enter only the endpoint constant $D_{\rm size}$, and their persistent tracker states ensure that no initialization allowance is charged again when a label is recycled. ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:rounding"></a>
### Balanced rounding and realization in the metric

Balanced subtree rounding with constant movement loss on separated trees was developed in (Bansal et al. 2015, sec. 5.2) <a id="pm-cite-30"></a>[[BBMN15](#ref-BBMN15)]. The formulation here also allows internal parks and a prescribed parent total correlated with the old child counts.

The allocation theorem allows mass to stop at internal vertices. We round both subtree amounts and parks, working from the root toward the leaves. At each vertex the already rounded parent fixes an integer total for its children and its park. That total may be correlated with their old counts. Our local kernel first meets the total in every realized state, then repairs the coordinate means by exchanges that preserve each state’s total.

The resulting integer parks can be matched along the tree. Parks matched at different vertices are paid by tree movement; parks matched at the same vertex require the separate anchor-travel estimate when their representative has moved.

<a id="eq:rounding-invariants"></a>
Throughout this section, the filtration $\mathcal F_t$ contains the request prefix and the independent partition tape, as in Section <a href="#sec:allocation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:allocation">3</a>. It excludes a further independent tape used only for rounding. All fractional states and anchors at time $t$ are $\mathcal F_t$-measurable. Write $$p_v^\circ(t)=q_v(t)-\sum_{i\text{ child of }v}q_i(t),$$ where the sum is empty at a leaf. We seek integer subtree counts $n_v(t)$ and integer parks $c_v(t)$ satisfying $$\label{eq:rounding-invariants}
 \begin{aligned}
 n_{\rm root}(t)&=k,&
 n_v(t)&=c_v(t)+\sum_{i\text{ child of }v}n_i(t),\\
 n_v(t)&\in\{\lfloor q_v(t)\rfloor,\lceil q_v(t)\rceil\},&
 \mathbb E[n_v(t)\mid\mathcal F_t]&=q_v(t),\\
 c_v(t)&\in\{\lfloor p_v^\circ(t)\rfloor,
                    \lceil p_v^\circ(t)\rceil\},&
 \mathbb E[c_v(t)\mid\mathcal F_t]&=p_v^\circ(t).
 \end{aligned}$$ These requirements concern conditional means given $\mathcal F_t$ alone. We do not prescribe the same means after additionally conditioning on the realized rounded ancestors; such requirements would generally be inconsistent with their prescribed integer totals.

<a id="pm-h-60"></a>
#### A finite rounding kernel for one star

<a id="stmt-lem-rounding-star"></a>
<div id="lem:rounding-star" class="lemma">

**Lemma 31** (Balanced rounding with a prescribed random total). *Let $a,b\in[0,\infty)^d$, and let $X\in\mathbb Z_{\ge0}^d$ be a random vector with $$X_i\in\{\lfloor a_i\rfloor,\lceil a_i\rceil\},
 \qquad \mathbb EX_i=a_i.$$ Put $M=\sum_iX_i$. Let $N$ be any random integer, arbitrarily correlated with $X$, such that $$N\in\left\{\left\lfloor\sum_i b_i\right\rfloor,
                 \left\lceil\sum_i b_i\right\rceil\right\},
 \qquad \mathbb EN=\sum_i b_i.$$ There is a randomized kernel from the finite set of states $(X,N)$ to vectors $X'$ such that, in every state and every outcome of the kernel, $$X'_i\in\{\lfloor b_i\rfloor,\lceil b_i\rceil\},
 \qquad \sum_iX'_i=N.$$ Its coordinate means, averaged over both the original state and the kernel, satisfy $$\mathbb EX'_i=b_i.$$ With $$T=\sum_i|a_i-b_i|,
 \qquad I=\mathbb E|N-M|,$$ the kernel can be chosen to satisfy <a id="eq:rounding-star-bound"></a>$$\label{eq:rounding-star-bound}
             \mathbb E\|X'-X\|_1\le32T+11I.$$ Moreover, for any balanced random total $N$ with the displayed mean, a balanced vector with total $N$ and coordinate means $b_i$ exists without specifying an old vector $X$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-rounding-star"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-rounding-star</summary>

<a id="eq:rounding-clip"></a>
*Proof.* All expectations in this proof are taken under the given joint distribution; no independence assumption is made. Set $l_i=\lfloor b_i\rfloor$ and $u_i=\lceil b_i\rceil$. Clip $X_i$ into $[l_i,u_i]$, obtaining $Y_i$. For each coordinate, $$\label{eq:rounding-clip}
                       \mathbb E|Y_i-X_i|\le |a_i-b_i|.$$ Indeed, if $a_i\le b_i$, upper clipping is impossible. Lower clipping either does nothing, or $l_i>\lfloor a_i\rfloor$, in which case every possible $X_i$ is at most $l_i$ and its expected cost is $l_i-a_i\le b_i-a_i$. If $a_i\ge b_i$, the symmetric argument applies to upper clipping. This includes integral targets and equality of endpoints. Summing <a href="#eq:rounding-clip" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:rounding-clip">[eq:rounding-clip]</a> gives $\mathbb E\|Y-X\|_1\le T$.

<a id="eq:rounding-preliminary"></a>
For each state, adjust $Y$ within the integer intervals $[l_i,u_i]$ until its sum equals the actual value of $N$, obtaining $Z$. The adjustment is always possible: the sums of allowed vectors comprise every integer between $\sum_i l_i$ and $\sum_i u_i$, and both endpoint integers of $\sum_i b_i$ lie in that interval. Increasing coordinates only, or decreasing coordinates only, as needed, costs exactly $|N-\sum_iY_i|$. A fixed coordinate order makes this choice deterministic. Thus, writing $L=\mathbb E\|Z-X\|_1$, $$\label{eq:rounding-preliminary}
 \begin{aligned}
 L&\le \mathbb E\|Y-X\|_1+\mathbb E\left|N-\sum_iY_i\right|\\
  &\le 2T+I,\\
 D:=\sum_i|\mathbb EZ_i-b_i|
  &\le \sum_i|\mathbb EZ_i-a_i|+T
   \le L+T\le3T+I.
 \end{aligned}$$ Only the means of $Z$ may now be wrong. Requiring $\mathbb E[X'_i\mid N]=b_i$ would force $N=\sum_i b_i$ on every positive-probability event. We instead repair the means under the given joint law while preserving each state’s actual total.

To construct this repair by finite optimization, let $$H=\{i:b_i\notin\mathbb Z\},\qquad f_i=b_i-l_i\quad(i\in H),
 \qquad s=\left\lfloor\sum_{i\in H}f_i\right\rfloor.$$ An allowed vector is specified by a subset $A\subseteq H$ of coordinates rounded upward. Its cardinality is $N-\sum_i l_i$, and hence is either $s$ or $s+1$. If $\sum_{i\in H}f_i$ is integral, only $s$ occurs. The preliminary vector $Z$ specifies such a subset $A_0$ in each original state.

<a id="eq:rounding-repair-objective"></a>
For every original state $\omega=(X,N)$ of positive probability, consider all probability distributions on subsets of the required cardinality for that state. Their product is a nonempty compact finite-dimensional set. A choice in this product is precisely a randomized kernel that keeps the observed total $N$ fixed. Minimize on it $$\label{eq:rounding-repair-objective}
 \mathbb E|A\mathbin{\triangle}A_0|
       +10\sum_{i\in H}|\Pr(i\in A)-f_i|.$$ Here $|A\mathbin{\triangle}A_0|$ is the number of coordinates changed; it equals the $\ell_1$ change of the associated integer vectors. A minimum exists by continuity. Let $p_i=\Pr(i\in A)$ at a minimizer. Since every outcome has the required total, $$\sum_{i\in H}p_i=\mathbb EN-\sum_i l_i
                                      =\sum_{i\in H}f_i.$$ Consequently, if a discrepancy remains, there are distinct indices $i,j$ with $p_i>f_i$ and $p_j<f_j$.

First suppose that a supported state-and-subset pair has $i\in A$, $j\notin A$. On a sufficiently small amount $\varepsilon>0$ of its probability mass replace $A$ by $A\setminus\{i\}\cup\{j\}$. This preserves that state’s total. Choose $\varepsilon$ no larger than the two discrepancies and the mass of this state-and-subset pair. The sum of marginal discrepancies falls by $2\varepsilon$, whereas the first term in <a href="#eq:rounding-repair-objective" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:rounding-repair-objective">[eq:rounding-repair-objective]</a> rises by at most $2\varepsilon$. This contradicts minimality.

In the remaining case, no supported subset contains $i$ without $j$. Because $p_i>f_i>0$, some supported subset $A$ contains both. Because $p_j<f_j<1$, some supported subset $B$ contains neither. Their cardinalities differ by at most one. There must therefore be an index $h\in B\setminus A$: otherwise $B\subseteq A\setminus\{i,j\}$ would give $|B|\le|A|-2$. On equal sufficiently small probability masses $\varepsilon$ of these two state-and-subset pairs, make the changes $$A\longmapsto A\setminus\{i\}\cup\{h\},
 \qquad
 B\longmapsto B\setminus\{h\}\cup\{j\}.$$ Each change occurs within its own original state and preserves that state’s total. The marginal of $h$ does not change, the marginal of $i$ decreases by $\varepsilon$, and that of $j$ increases by $\varepsilon$. The Hamming cost in <a href="#eq:rounding-repair-objective" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:rounding-repair-objective">[eq:rounding-repair-objective]</a> rises by at most $4\varepsilon$, by the triangle inequality for symmetric difference. The discrepancy penalty falls by $20\varepsilon$. Again this is a strict improvement. This argument allows the two subsets to belong to different original states and different values of the rounded parent; it does not alter their probabilities or totals.

Thus every minimizer has $p_i=f_i$ for all $i\in H$. The other coordinates are fixed integers, so the corresponding vector $X'$ has all required means. Comparison with the kernel that always keeps $A_0$ gives $$\mathbb E\|X'-Z\|_1\le10D.$$ Combining with <a href="#eq:rounding-preliminary" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:rounding-preliminary">[eq:rounding-preliminary]</a> proves $$\mathbb E\|X'-X\|_1\le L+10D
       \le(2T+I)+10(3T+I)=32T+11I.$$

For initialization, choose in each state $N$ any allowed subset of the required size, and minimize the same objective relative to that subset. The exchange argument still proves that the minimum has the desired means; it used only the correct mean and the two possible values of $N$. If $H$ is empty, $N=\sum_i b_i$ and the sole allowed vector is $b$ itself, so no repair is needed. This proves the initialization assertion as well. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-61"></a>
#### The joint rounding process

We apply Lemma <a href="#lem:rounding-star" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:rounding-star">31</a> in a fixed order from the root toward the leaves. At an internal vertex $v$, the coordinates of its star are its ordinary children together with one extra coordinate for its park. The target vector is $(q_i)_{i\text{ child of }v}$ together with $p_v^\circ$, whose sum is $q_v$. The prescribed integer total is the already generated $n_v$. At a leaf simply set $c_v=n_v$.

At time zero, put $n_{\rm root}=k$ and use the initialization assertion successively at every internal vertex. The resulting conditional means are those in <a href="#eq:rounding-invariants" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:rounding-invariants">[eq:rounding-invariants]</a>, by induction over the fixed order. Consistency holds in every outcome, so summing all parks gives exactly $k$. No movement is charged for this choice of the initial simulated configuration; its mismatch from the actual starting tuple is charged once below.

<a id="eq:old-rounding-means"></a>
Consider an update from $t-1$ to $t$. The old rounding variables are functions of $\mathcal F_{t-1}$ and the independent rounding tape. The fractional algorithm and the requests do not depend on that tape. Therefore revealing $\mathcal F_t$ preserves their conditional distribution from $\mathcal F_{t-1}$; in particular, $$\label{eq:old-rounding-means}
 \mathbb E[n_v(t-1)\mid\mathcal F_t]=q_v(t-1),
 \qquad
 \mathbb E[c_v(t-1)\mid\mathcal F_t]=p_v^\circ(t-1).$$ Formally, if $U$ denotes the independent rounding tape and $Z=g(\mathcal F_{t-1},U)$ is one of these variables, its conditional law given $\mathcal F_t$ is obtained by integrating $g$ over the unchanged law of $U$. Its dependence on the observed data is already fixed by $\mathcal F_{t-1}\subseteq\mathcal F_t$. This also shows why the additional rounding tape must not affect any of the fractional observations.

Condition now on a fractional history through time $t$. At a star $v$, use the joint law of its old child and parking counts and its newly generated parent count $n_v(t)$. This is a finite law determined by the known past kernels and the kernels already used above $v$ at this step. The parent count has conditional mean $q_v(t)$ by the top-down induction, while <a href="#eq:old-rounding-means" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:old-rounding-means">[eq:old-rounding-means]</a> supplies the old coordinate means. The lemma therefore applies even though this newly generated parent can be correlated with all the old local counts. Select a minimizing finite kernel and sample it at the actual state encountered. Subsequent stars do not change counts already assigned, so the induction gives all of <a href="#eq:rounding-invariants" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:rounding-invariants">[eq:rounding-invariants]</a> jointly.

This description defines causal kernels, rather than merely unrelated couplings at separate times. Each kernel uses the joint law conditional on the fractional history, then acts on the realized local state and parent total. It need not impose its marginal targets separately on the events specifying individual ancestor roundings. At a fixed finite horizon there are only finitely many relevant request and partition outcomes: on $Y$, a radius is needed only through the finite ball it defines, and only finitely many insertion and retirement draws are used. We can therefore choose one minimizing kernel at each relevant history without a measurable-selection issue. Choices at histories of probability zero can be arbitrary balanced continuations. The distributional policy is allowed to depend on the given law and horizon at this stage.

<a id="stmt-prop-rounding"></a>
<div id="prop:rounding" class="proposition">

<a id="eq:rounding-global"></a>
<a id="eq:rounding-realization"></a>
**Proposition 32** (Rounding and metric realization). *Assume the fractional allocations and anchors constructed in the preceding sections, with $\tau\ge22$. They admit a causal rounding satisfying <a href="#eq:rounding-invariants" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:rounding-invariants">[eq:rounding-invariants]</a> for which $$\label{eq:rounding-global}
 N_{\rm rnd}:=\mathbb E\sum_{t=1}^H\sum_{v\ne{\rm root}}
                 r_{|v|-1}|n_v(t)-n_v(t-1)|\le132V.$$ Placing the integer parks at their current anchors gives simulated configurations $S_t$ of exactly $k$ members containing each current request. There is a matching of consecutive configurations with expected total cost at most $$\label{eq:rounding-realization}
                         120N_{\rm rnd}+40T_{\rm anchor}.$$ Consequently their expected movement is at most $C(V+\ell P+\ell Q)+D$ for an absolute $C$ and a finite additive term $D$ depending only on the fixed finite structure.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-rounding"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-rounding</summary>

*Proof.* We first prove the movement estimate in the tree. For an internal vertex $v$ and time $t$, define $$T_{v,t}=\sum_{i\text{ child of }v}|\Delta q_i(t)|
                                      +|\Delta p_v^\circ(t)|,
 \qquad
 I_{v,t}=\mathbb E[|\Delta n_v(t)|\mid\mathcal F_t].$$ The local lemma, conditional on $\mathcal F_t$, gives $$\mathbb E\left[\sum_{i\text{ child of }v}|\Delta n_i(t)|
                          +|\Delta c_v(t)|\,\middle|\,\mathcal F_t\right]
                                  \le32T_{v,t}+11I_{v,t}.$$ Since $p_v^\circ=q_v-\sum_iq_i$, $$T_{v,t}\le2\sum_{i\text{ child of }v}|\Delta q_i(t)|
                                          +|\Delta q_v(t)|.$$ Multiply by $r_{|v|}$, sum over internal vertices and times, and take expectations. Every nonroot vertex occurs once as an ordinary child, and $\Delta q_{\rm root}=0$. Thus <a id="eq:rounding-target-sum"></a>$$\label{eq:rounding-target-sum}
 \mathbb E\sum_{t,v\text{ internal}}r_{|v|}T_{v,t}
                                            \le(2+\tau^{-1})V.$$ The same weighting of the parent terms gives <a id="eq:rounding-parent-sum"></a>$$\label{eq:rounding-parent-sum}
 \mathbb E\sum_{t,v\text{ internal}}r_{|v|}I_{v,t}
                                       \le\tau^{-1}N_{\rm rnd},$$ because the root count is fixed and the internal nonroot vertices are a subset of all nonroot vertices. Dropping the nonnegative parking movements from the local left-hand sides now yields the exact recurrence <a id="eq:rounding-absorption"></a>$$\label{eq:rounding-absorption}
 N_{\rm rnd}\le(64+32/\tau)V+(11/\tau)N_{\rm rnd}.$$ All sums are finite. For $\tau\ge22$ this gives $N_{\rm rnd}\le(128+64/\tau)V\le132V$, proving <a href="#eq:rounding-global" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:rounding-global">[eq:rounding-global]</a>. In particular, initialization of the rounding does not introduce a new additive variation term. The same absolute $\tau$ can satisfy this threshold and the allocation theorem’s absorption threshold.

<a id="eq:rounding-transport-cost"></a>
We next describe the matching in the tree. Give the edge entering a nonroot vertex $v$ length $r_{|v|-1}$. The consistency identities in <a href="#eq:rounding-invariants" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:rounding-invariants">[eq:rounding-invariants]</a> imply that $n_v(t)$ is the total number of integer parks in the subtree at $v$. First match $\min\{c_v(t-1),c_v(t)\}$ old and new copies at every identical vertex $v$. The unmatched copies define integer supplies and demands with equal total. Starting at the leaves, cancel supplies against demands whenever they meet, and carry the uncancelled copies to the parent. At the edge entering $v$, the number of copies carried is exactly $|\Delta n_v(t)|$: the signed imbalance in that subtree is $\Delta n_v(t)$, and after cancellation all remaining copies have the same sign. The root has zero final imbalance. Following each matched pair’s path therefore gives total tree length exactly $$\label{eq:rounding-transport-cost}
                   \sum_{v\ne{\rm root}}r_{|v|-1}|\Delta n_v(t)|.$$ This also proves the lower bound for any matching, since each subtree’s net imbalance must cross its entering edge. Fixing orders for cancellations makes the chosen matching a function of the current and previous rounded states.

Every occupied vertex has a current validity witness. Indeed $c_v(t)>0$ implies $p_v^\circ(t)>0$, since the only balanced rounding of zero is zero. Hence $q_v(t)>0$, and the allocation domination $q_v(t)\le C_0m_v(t)$ implies $m_v(t)>0$. Some point of $Y$ must then have the prefix defining $v$. This is also the witness assertion of Lemma <a href="#lem:park-variation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:park-variation">27</a>. At a nonroot vertex of depth $j$, its witness is within $20r_j$ of the vertex’s current anchor, by Lemma <a href="#lem:fixed-alphabet" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:fixed-alphabet">24</a>.

<a id="eq:rounding-distinct-anchors"></a>
Consider a matched old vertex $u$ and new vertex $v$ with $u\ne v$. Let their last common prefix be $w$ of depth $j$. If $j\ge1$, choose an old witness $y_-$ for $u$ and a new witness $y_+$ for $v$. Write $a_-(z)$ and $a_+(z)$ for an anchor before and after the step, respectively. Both witnesses use the same depth-$j$ key at $w$ in the two consecutive maps. By the fixed-alphabet construction, its two anchors are either equal or related by an explicit heavy-ball reuse, and in either case $$d(a_-(w),a_+(w))\le40r_j.$$ The witnesses are within $20r_j$ of the corresponding anchors at $w$. They are also within $20r_{|u|}$ and $20r_{|v|}$ of the anchors of their own endpoints. Since both endpoint radii are at most $r_j$, the triangle inequality gives $$\label{eq:rounding-distinct-anchors}
 \begin{aligned}
 d(a_-(u),a_+(v))
 &\le20r_{|u|}+20r_j+40r_j+20r_j+20r_{|v|}\\
 &\le120r_j.
 \end{aligned}$$ The tree path between distinct endpoints passes through an edge of length $r_j$: this remains true when $w=u$ or $w=v$, since then the path starts with the edge to the next depth. Thus <a href="#eq:rounding-distinct-anchors" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:rounding-distinct-anchors">[eq:rounding-distinct-anchors]</a> is at most $120$ times this path’s tree length. If $j=0$, both anchors belong to $Y$, so their distance is at most $\mathop{\mathrm{diam}}(Y)=r_0$. Their path crosses an edge of length $r_0$, also when one endpoint is the root. The same bound follows. Only occupied vertices were used in this reasoning; no assertion about distances associated with nonexistent prefixes is needed.

It remains to pay for the copies matched at one and the same vertex. The root anchor is fixed, so it contributes zero. For a nonroot vertex $v$ of depth $j$, put $h_v=\min\{c_v(t-1),c_v(t)\}$. If its anchor moves while both copies are occupied, its last label is retained across the step. Only heavy labels can move in this way, and the displacement is at most $40r_j$. Write $E_{t,j,\lambda}$ for the event that label $\lambda$ is retained and moves at this step. This event and both anchors are $\mathcal F_t$-measurable. Since $h_v\le c_v(t)$, regardless of any correlations created by the matching, $$\mathbb E[h_v\mathbf 1_{E_{t,j,\lambda}}\mid\mathcal F_t]
       \le p_v^\circ(t)\mathbf 1_{E_{t,j,\lambda}}.$$ Summing first over all depth-$j$ vertices with last label $\lambda$ turns the right-hand side into $b_{t,j,\lambda}\mathbf 1_{E_{t,j,\lambda}}$, in the notation of Section <a href="#sec:travel" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:travel">8</a>. Summing over labels, levels and times pays the expected cost of all these coincident-vertex matches by $40T_{\rm anchor}$. Together with <a href="#eq:rounding-transport-cost" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:rounding-transport-cost">[eq:rounding-transport-cost]</a> and the bound for distinct vertices, this proves <a href="#eq:rounding-realization" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:rounding-realization">[eq:rounding-realization]</a>.

Finally let $x$ be the current request. Its assigned leaf has $q_v\ge1$ by Theorem <a href="#thm:allocation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:allocation">5</a>, so balancedness gives $c_v=n_v\ge1$. At level $J$, a point assigned to a ball key is within $20r_J$ of that key’s center. Since $40r_J$ is smaller than the least positive distance in $Y$, this point equals the center. A fallback key is anchored at its point as well. The anchor of the request leaf is therefore exactly $x$, and the simulated configuration serves it. The claimed final estimate now follows from <a href="#eq:rounding-global" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:rounding-global">[eq:rounding-global]</a>, <a href="#eq:rounding-realization" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:rounding-realization">[eq:rounding-realization]</a>, and Proposition <a href="#prop:anchor-travel" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:anchor-travel">28</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-62"></a>
#### Uniform finite-law synthesis

We can now complete the distributional statement in the setup. We give the additive accounting explicitly because its independence of the horizon is essential for the transfer in the next section.

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-rounding-2"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-rounding</summary>

<a id="eq:rounding-budget-summary"></a>
*Proof of Proposition <a href="#prop:finite-law" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:finite-law">3</a>.* Fix the finite metric $Y$, the server number $k$ and the initial tuple $s$. Choose the absolute separation factor once, sufficiently large for Theorem <a href="#thm:allocation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:allocation">5</a> and with $\tau\ge22$. Fix the scales and the finite alphabets from Lemma <a href="#lem:fixed-alphabet" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:fixed-alphabet">24</a>. These choices depend only on $Y,k$ and the absolute construction parameters. For a given finite-support input law at a finite horizon, choose its hidden optimal trajectory and carry out the partition, allocation, anchor and rounding constructions. Their interfaces give absolute constants $C_P,C_A,C_T$ and finite remainders $D_P,D_A,D_T$ such that $$\label{eq:rounding-budget-summary}
 \begin{aligned}
 P&\le C_P\ell Q+D_P,
       &&\text{by Proposition~\ref{prop:embedded-cost}},\\
 V&\le C_A\ell P+D_A,
       &&\text{by Theorem~\ref{thm:allocation}},\\
 T_{\rm anchor}&\le C_T(V+\ell P+\ell Q)+D_T,
       &&\text{by Proposition~\ref{prop:anchor-travel}}.
 \end{aligned}$$ In particular the conditional laws used by the allocation are those of the hidden members under the final online map. Filtering keeps the old map with the old hidden variable, and drift uses the coupled old and new maps, exactly as required by the first two estimates. Rounding neither changes those observations nor supplies information to the partition rules.

Here is a census of the fixed objects and their possible endpoint charges in <a href="#eq:rounding-budget-summary" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:rounding-budget-summary">[eq:rounding-budget-summary]</a>. Let $\mathcal T$ be the full finite prefix tree, let $d_v$ be the number of children of an internal vertex, and let $L_j$ be the number of label slots at level $j$. These numbers can be large. They are fixed before the law and horizon are chosen.

1.  At each fixed vertex and rank, the elementary tracking potential is $p(1-p)+(p-p^\circ)^2\in[0,1]$. There are finitely many such rank, flag and endpoint-function trackers, indexed by the $k$ ranks of the fixed vertices, with the fixed finite number of versions used in the allocation construction. Their tolerances, including the tolerances of order $1/\ell$, have already been fixed. The resulting initialization and endpoint charges are finite functions of $k$ and the vertex index, rather than of a trajectory’s number of resets.

    At an internal vertex, the sizes, core sums and cap scales are bounded by fixed multiples of $k$. The proportion variables have the fixed simplex or bounded-$\ell_1$ domains used in their definitions. The primitive bounds in Section <a href="#sec:proportions" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:proportions">4</a> therefore bound the absolute values of both proportion potentials by a finite quantity, for example $C\ell k(d_v+1)$ after increasing an absolute constant. The switching potential is bounded by $Ck$. Initial size and epoch charges likewise involve only these bounded sizes. Multiplying these finitely many bounds by their fixed star weights $r_{|v|}$ and by the fixed coefficients in the local estimates gives a finite common choice of $D_A$.

2.  At scale $r_j$, each pilot potential lies in $[0,2r_jk]$. There is one heavy template and a finite number of tier templates, the latter with their fixed weights $e^{-h}$. The heavy distance-ramp and the tier noncoverage potentials lie in $[0,r_jk]$ for each template. Their initial and terminal contributions are bounded by the corresponding finite sums of $2r_jk$ and $r_jk$ over levels and tiers, with the fixed coefficients from Section <a href="#sec:partitions" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:partitions">7</a>. Hence $D_P$ can be chosen uniformly.

3.  Each held key size in Section <a href="#sec:travel" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:travel">8</a> is in $[1,k+1]$. The affine tangent potential to $-s\log s$, evaluated at an argument and reference in that interval, has absolute value at most $C(k+1)\ell$. The normalized travel potential for a level-$j$ label is nonnegative and at most $Cr_jk$, because its parked-mass coefficient $b/\widehat m$ is bounded by an absolute constant and its counting integral is at most $k$. The sums $$\sum_{j=1}^J L_j r_j(k+1)\ell
           \quad\hbox{and}\quad
     \sum_{j=1}^J L_j r_jk$$ therefore bound all relevant endpoint terms up to fixed coefficients. They give a finite common choice of $D_T$, including its size-tracker initialization charges.

4.  The rounding process has no additional variation remainder, by <a href="#eq:rounding-global" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:rounding-global">[eq:rounding-global]</a>. Its initial configuration $S_0$ is a multiset of $k$ points in $Y$. Consequently the initial minimum matching distance from $s$ to $S_0$ is at most $k\mathop{\mathrm{diam}}(Y)$, for every rounding outcome.

This list uses persistent state at every fixed slot. A rank or key tracker continues through absence and later reappearance of its label; no new initialization allowance is charged when a slot is reused. Epoch changes and normalization refreshes are paid transitions in the preceding estimates. The alphabets have bounded size because each tier and scale has at most $|Y|K^2$ relevant roster entries, with fixed additional slots for the label exclusion rule. Fresh random draws at successive insertions do not create fresh tree vertices or fresh potentials. Thus the finite endpoint bounds above hold uniformly over all possible histories, and are summed over slots and templates once. The number of histories, the number of label reuses and the horizon do not enter $D_P,D_A,D_T$.

<a id="eq:rounding-finite-additive"></a>
To make the final combination explicit, put $$C_1=120\cdot132+40C_T,
 \qquad C_2=40C_T,
 \qquad C_*=C_1C_A+C_2.$$ Proposition <a href="#prop:rounding" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:rounding">32</a> and <a href="#eq:rounding-budget-summary" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:rounding-budget-summary">[eq:rounding-budget-summary]</a> give $$\begin{aligned}
 \mathbb E[\text{simulated movement}]
 &\le C_1V+C_2\ell P+C_2\ell Q+40D_T\\
 &\le C_*\ell P+C_2\ell Q+C_1D_A+40D_T\\
 &\le (C_*C_P+C_2)\ell^2Q
                +C_*\ell D_P+C_1D_A+40D_T,
\end{aligned}$$ where $\ell\ge1$ was used in the last line. Apply Lemma <a href="#lem:lazy-simulation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:lazy-simulation">2</a> from the actual labeled tuple $s$. It yields a causal strongly lazy policy serving the same requests, with expected cost at most this simulated movement plus the initial matching mismatch. Thus Proposition <a href="#prop:finite-law" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:finite-law">3</a> holds with the absolute constant $$a=C_*C_P+C_2$$ and, for example, the finite nonnegative constant $$\label{eq:rounding-finite-additive}
 D_0=C_*\ell D_P+C_1D_A+40D_T+k\mathop{\mathrm{diam}}(Y).$$ All three remainders can be enlarged to nonnegative uniform bounds from the census above. Formula <a href="#eq:rounding-finite-additive" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:rounding-finite-additive">[eq:rounding-finite-additive]</a> consequently depends only on the fixed metric, $k$, the start and the fixed construction; it is independent of the input law and horizon. Repeated starting positions cause no change, since both simulated configurations and the matching simulation treat repeated points as separate copies. ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:transfer"></a>
### One policy on an arbitrary metric space

Proposition <a href="#prop:finite-law" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:finite-law">3</a> produces a policy for each input law on a fixed finite metric. We now remove that dependence in three steps. First, repeated independent episodes remove the additive term when the starting points are distinct. A finite separation argument then gives one policy for any finite collection of inputs. Finally, product compactness gives one policy for all finite inputs, even when the metric space is infinite. We spell out the finite separation argument and the closed constraints used in compactness.

<a id="eq:transfer-alpha"></a>
Throughout this section, let $a$ be the absolute constant in Proposition <a href="#prop:finite-law" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:finite-law">3</a>, and put $$\ell=1+\log(k+1),\qquad c_{\mathrm{tr}}=2a+1,
 \qquad \alpha=c_{\mathrm{tr}}\ell^2.
 \label{eq:transfer-alpha}$$ We may enlarge the additive constant of that proposition to ensure $D_0(Y,k,s)\ge0$. Its independence of the input law and horizon is essential below.

<a id="pm-h-64"></a>
#### Removing the finite-metric additive constant

<a id="stmt-lem-amplification"></a>
<div id="lem:amplification" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:additive-free-law"></a>
**Lemma 33** (Episode amplification). *Let $Y$ be a finite metric space, let $k\ge2$, and let $s=(s_1,\ldots,s_k)\in Y^k$ have distinct entries. For every finite-support probability law $\lambda$ on finite request sequences in $Y$, there is a causal randomized policy $A_\lambda$ starting from $s$ such that $$\mathbb E_{\sigma\sim\lambda}\mathbb E\bigl[\mathop{\mathrm{cost}}_{A_\lambda,s}(\sigma)\bigr]
 \le \alpha\,
      \mathbb E_{\sigma\sim\lambda}\bigl[\mathop{\mathrm{OPT}}_s(\sigma)\bigr].
 \label{eq:additive-free-law}$$ The policy can be strongly lazy. The constant $c_{\mathrm{tr}}$ in <a href="#eq:transfer-alpha" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:transfer-alpha">[eq:transfer-alpha]</a> is the same for all $Y$, $s$, and $\lambda$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-amplification"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-amplification</summary>

*Proof.* Write $Q=\mathbb E_{\lambda}\mathop{\mathrm{OPT}}_s(\sigma)$. We first make the episode length deterministic without changing $Q$. Let $$L=\max\bigl(1,\max_{\sigma\in\mathop{\mathrm{supp}}\lambda}|\sigma|\bigr).$$ Extend each nonempty sequence to length $L$ by repeating its last request; extend an empty sequence by $L$ repetitions of $s_1$. Denote the resulting law on $Y^L$ by $\bar\lambda$. For every original sequence $\sigma$ and its extension $\bar\sigma$, <a id="eq:padding-optimum"></a>$$\mathop{\mathrm{OPT}}_s(\bar\sigma)=\mathop{\mathrm{OPT}}_s(\sigma).
 \label{eq:padding-optimum}$$ Indeed, restriction of any service of $\bar\sigma$ to its original prefix gives the lower bound. An optimal service of a nonempty $\sigma$ ends with a server at its last request and can serve the extension at zero extra cost. An empty sequence and its extension both have optimum zero. A causal policy designed for $L$ requests can be run on the original sequence and stopped when that sequence ends; its actions on this prefix are identical to its actions on the extension. Thus it is enough to prove the claim for $\bar\lambda$ and then truncate. The policy need not be told the actual stopping time in advance.

If $Q=0$, every sequence of positive probability has optimum zero. An optimum is attained because there are only $k^L$ possible strings of server labels. A zero-cost service cannot move any server to a different point, so every request lies among $s_1,\ldots,s_k$. Any strongly lazy policy therefore has zero cost on the law, proving <a href="#eq:additive-free-law" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:additive-free-law">[eq:additive-free-law]</a>. We henceforth assume $Q>0$.

Set $$\Delta=\mathop{\mathrm{diam}}(Y),\qquad
 \delta=\min\{d(y,z):y,z\in Y,\ y\ne z\}>0,
 \qquad R=\left\lfloor\frac{k\Delta}{\delta}\right\rfloor+1.$$ The distinct starting points ensure $\Delta>0$. The reset word is $R$ consecutive copies of $(s_1,\ldots,s_k)$, of length $Rk$. For every $\rho=(x_1,\ldots,x_L)\in\mathop{\mathrm{supp}}\bar\lambda$, choose one optimal labeled service from $s$, resolving ties by the lexicographic order of its chosen server labels. Write $C_t(\rho)\in Y^k$ for its configuration after request $t$, and $j_t(\rho)$ for its chosen label. Here $C_0(\rho)=s$; a label is designated even when the move is zero. Define its reverse word to be <a id="eq:reverse-word"></a>$$\rho^{\leftarrow}
  =\bigl(C_{L-1,j_L}(\rho),C_{L-2,j_{L-1}}(\rho),
              \ldots,C_{0,j_1}(\rho)\bigr).
 \label{eq:reverse-word}$$ Starting at $C_L(\rho)$, this word can be served by moving labels $j_L,j_{L-1},\ldots,j_1$ in that order. Before undoing step $t$ the configuration is exactly $C_t(\rho)$, and its designated move returns it to $C_{t-1}(\rho)$. This verifies legality and returns the labeled tuple exactly to $s$, at total cost $\mathop{\mathrm{OPT}}_s(\rho)$. The assertion also covers zero moves. The reset word then has zero comparator cost.

Fix an integer $N\ge1$. Sample $N$ independent words $\rho_1,\ldots,\rho_N$ from $\bar\lambda$, and concatenate, for each $i$, its forward word $\rho_i$, its reverse word $\rho_i^{\leftarrow}$, and the reset word. This is a finite-support law on words of the fixed length $N(2L+Rk)$. All its requests are functions of the sampled forward words and the fixed optimal services; in particular, the law is fixed independently of the online policy’s randomness.
