Following the chosen optimal service and then its reverse in every episode gives <a id="eq:episode-comparator"></a>$$\mathbb E\mathop{\mathrm{OPT}}_s(\text{concatenated experiment})\le 2NQ.
 \label{eq:episode-comparator}$$ Proposition <a href="#prop:finite-law" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:finite-law">3</a>, followed if necessary by Lemma <a href="#lem:lazy-simulation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:lazy-simulation">2</a> with zero initial mismatch, gives a strongly lazy causal policy $\mathcal A_N$ for this experiment with <a id="eq:episode-total"></a>$$\mathbb E\mathop{\mathrm{cost}}_{\mathcal A_N,s}(\text{experiment})
       \le 2a\ell^2NQ+D_0(Y,k,s).
 \label{eq:episode-total}$$ As usual, specify arbitrary causal strongly lazy continuation rules at histories having probability zero under the experiment law.

<a id="eq:reset-mismatch"></a>
Let $F_i$, $H_i$, and $Z_i$ be the costs of $\mathcal A_N$ during the forward word, reverse word, and reset word of episode $i$, respectively. Let $A_i$ be its configuration at the end of that reset word, and define $$M_i=\mathsf M(A_i,s),\qquad M_0=0.$$ We claim, pathwise, that $$M_i\le Z_i\qquad(1\le i\le N).
 \label{eq:reset-mismatch}$$ If a complete reset cycle has zero movement, the configuration stays constant throughout that cycle and covers every one of the $k$ distinct points $s_1,\ldots,s_k$. Since there are exactly $k$ servers, its multiset of positions is the starting multiset. Strong laziness keeps this multiset fixed throughout all remaining reset cycles. Consequently, if $M_i>0$, every reset cycle contains a positive move. Each such move has length at least $\delta$, because all positions are in $Y$. Therefore $$Z_i\ge R\delta>k\Delta\ge\mathsf M(A_i,s)=M_i.$$ If $M_i=0$, <a href="#eq:reset-mismatch" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:reset-mismatch">[eq:reset-mismatch]</a> follows from nonnegativity. The argument requires equality only of multisets, not of the labels occupying the distinct starting points.

We extract a policy for one forward word. Before receiving any actual requests, choose $I$ uniformly from $\{1,\ldots,N\}$. Conditional on $I=i$, generate $i-1$ fictitious independent forward words from $\bar\lambda$, together with their fixed reverse and reset words. Using fresh internal randomness independent of these words, simulate $\mathcal A_N$ from $s$ through that entire fictitious prefix. This produces its internal state and a virtual configuration at the start of forward episode $i$. For each actual request, now give that request to the virtual policy as its next request. Follow its virtual configurations from the real initial tuple $s$ by the strongly lazy simulation in Lemma <a href="#lem:lazy-simulation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:lazy-simulation">2</a>.

This defines a causal policy: the fictitious prefix is internal randomness independent of the actual input, the boundary of episode $i$ is known from $i$, $L$, and $R$, and the virtual continuation uses only the actual requests already received. Neither the remainder of the actual word nor its reverse word needs to be generated. After $L$ actual requests, an arbitrary strongly lazy rule extends this policy to all longer inputs.

<a id="eq:finite-n-amplification"></a>
When the actual forward word is sampled from $\bar\lambda$, conditional on $I=i$, the virtual evolution through that word has the same law as the evolution of $\mathcal A_N$ through forward episode $i$ in the experiment. The true simulation starts with mismatch $M_{i-1}$ under this identification, where $M_0=0$ for $i=1$. Lemma <a href="#lem:lazy-simulation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:lazy-simulation">2</a> and <a href="#eq:reset-mismatch" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:reset-mismatch">[eq:reset-mismatch]</a> give $$\begin{aligned}
 \mathbb E\mathop{\mathrm{cost}}_{\mathrm{extracted},s}(\rho)
 &\le \frac1N\sum_{i=1}^N\mathbb E(F_i+M_{i-1}) \notag\\
 &\le \frac1N\left(\sum_{i=1}^N\mathbb EF_i
                    +\sum_{i=1}^{N-1}\mathbb EZ_i\right) \notag\\
 &\le \frac1N\mathbb E\mathop{\mathrm{cost}}_{\mathcal A_N,s}(\text{experiment}) \notag\\
 &\le 2a\ell^2Q+\frac{D_0(Y,k,s)}N.
 \label{eq:finite-n-amplification}
\end{aligned}$$ In the third line, the charged forward and preceding reset blocks are disjoint parts of the experiment; reverse-block costs and the last reset-block cost are nonnegative uncharged terms. Truncating to an original unpadded sequence can only reduce this expected cost.

Finally take $$N=\max\left(1,
      \left\lceil\frac{D_0(Y,k,s)}{\ell^2Q}\right\rceil\right).$$ Then <a href="#eq:finite-n-amplification" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:finite-n-amplification">[eq:finite-n-amplification]</a> is at most $(2a+1)\ell^2Q$, as asserted. This is a finite choice of $N$ for the given law; no limiting policy is used here. The metric-dependent numbers $R$ and $N$ affect only the auxiliary experiment and not the coefficient in <a href="#eq:additive-free-law" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:additive-free-law">[eq:additive-free-law]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-65"></a>
#### A single policy for a finite collection of inputs

For this and the next subsection fix an arbitrary metric space $X$ and a distinct starting tuple $s\in X^k$. Write $[k]=\{1,\ldots,k\}$. A decision history at time $t$ consists of the request prefix $(x_1,\ldots,x_t)$ and the previously chosen server labels $(j_1,\ldots,j_{t-1})$. These data determine the current labeled configuration. A behavioral policy assigns a probability vector on $[k]$ to every such history and samples its next label from that vector. It has no horizon parameter.

<a id="stmt-lem-finite-minimax"></a>
<div id="lem:finite-minimax" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:finite-family-bound"></a>
**Lemma 34** (Finite separation and behavioral conversion). *For every finite collection $\sigma_1,\ldots,\sigma_m$ of finite request sequences in $X$, there is a behavioral policy $p$ from $s$ satisfying $$\mathbb E\mathop{\mathrm{cost}}_{p,s}(\sigma_i)\le\alpha\mathop{\mathrm{OPT}}_s(\sigma_i)
       \qquad(1\le i\le m).
 \label{eq:finite-family-bound}$$ Its behavioral choices can be defined on all decision histories in $X$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-finite-minimax"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-finite-minimax</summary>

*Proof.* There is nothing to prove for an empty collection. Otherwise let $Y$ be the finite submetric containing all starting points and all points appearing in the sequences. It has positive diameter. Every server position during a legal service is an initial position or a previously requested point: each permitted move is to the current request. Therefore passing from $X$ to $Y$ changes neither the legal label strings nor their costs, and <a id="eq:finite-restriction-optimum"></a>$$\mathop{\mathrm{OPT}}_{Y,s}(\sigma_i)=\mathop{\mathrm{OPT}}_{X,s}(\sigma_i).
 \label{eq:finite-restriction-optimum}$$

Let $\mathcal H_0$ be the finite set of decision histories whose request prefix is a nonempty prefix of some $\sigma_i$, with every possible previous label string included. This is the finite distributional minimax principle associated with Yao (Yao 1977) <a id="pm-cite-31"></a>[[Yao77](#ref-Yao77)], applied to excess cost. A deterministic policy on this set is a map $\pi:\mathcal H_0\to[k]$. There are finitely many such maps; write their set as $\mathcal D$. For each $\pi\in\mathcal D$, put $$v(\pi)_i=\mathop{\mathrm{cost}}_{\pi,s}(\sigma_i)-\alpha\mathop{\mathrm{OPT}}_s(\sigma_i),
 \qquad
 K=\left\{\sum_{\pi\in\mathcal D}\theta_\pi v(\pi):
       \theta_\pi\ge0,\ \sum_{\pi\in\mathcal D}\theta_\pi=1\right\}.$$ The set $K\subseteq\mathbb R^m$ is compact, being the continuous image of a finite-dimensional compact simplex, and is convex.

<a id="eq:finite-separation"></a>
We claim $K\cap(-\mathbb R_{\ge0}^m)\ne\varnothing$. If not, minimize the continuous function $$f(z)=\sum_{i=1}^m\max(z_i,0)^2$$ over $K$, and let $z_*$ be a minimizer. Put $w_i=\max((z_*)_i,0)$. The supposed disjointness implies $w\ne0$. For every $z\in K$ the segment $z_*+t(z-z_*)$, $0\le t\le1$, lies in $K$. The right derivative of $f$ along this segment at $t=0$ is nonnegative. Since the scalar function $u\mapsto\max(u,0)^2$ is differentiable, including at zero, this says $$2w\mathbin{\cdot}(z-z_*)\ge0.$$ It follows that $$w\mathbin{\cdot}z\ge w\mathbin{\cdot}z_*
    =\sum_i w_i^2>0\qquad(z\in K).
 \label{eq:finite-separation}$$ Normalize $w$ to the probability weights $\lambda_i=w_i/(\sum_jw_j)$ on the target sequences. Lemma <a href="#lem:amplification" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:amplification">33</a>, applied on $Y$, supplies a randomized causal policy with nonpositive expected excess cost for this law.

Every randomized causal policy, restricted to this finite history set, has its vector of expected excess costs in $K$. To see this directly, fix its internal random tape, independent of requests. Run the fixed tape on each of the finitely many request prefixes. This determines a deterministic rule at histories compatible with those runs; assign arbitrary choices at incompatible histories. The resulting member of $\mathcal D$ reproduces its actions on every $\sigma_i$. There are only finitely many possible rules, so their probabilities form a convex combination of the vectors $v(\pi)$. Each event defining such a rule is a finite combination of events for choices on fixed finite inputs and is measurable. Consequently the expected excess for the law with weights $\lambda_i$ is strictly positive by <a href="#eq:finite-separation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:finite-separation">[eq:finite-separation]</a>, a contradiction. This proves the claim. Choose weights $\theta_\pi$ whose convex combination lies in the nonpositive orthant. Sampling $\pi$ with these weights at the start would give <a href="#eq:finite-family-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:finite-family-bound">[eq:finite-family-bound]</a>.

<a id="eq:behavioral-conversion"></a>
For later compactness we convert this finite mixture to behavioral choices, without placing any law on requests. This is the perfect-recall conversion of mixed to behavioral strategies (Kuhn 1953) <a id="pm-cite-32"></a>[[Kuhn53](#ref-Kuhn53)]; the formula below makes its request-independent meaning explicit. At a history $h=((x_1,\ldots,x_t),(j_1,\ldots,j_{t-1}))\in\mathcal H_0$, let $$\mathcal D(h)=\{\pi\in\mathcal D:
   \pi((x_1,\ldots,x_u),(j_1,\ldots,j_{u-1}))=j_u
                    \text{ for every }u<t\},
 \qquad Z(h)=\sum_{\pi\in\mathcal D(h)}\theta_\pi.$$ If $Z(h)>0$, define $$p_h(j)=\frac{\displaystyle
          \sum_{\substack{\pi\in\mathcal D(h)\\\pi(h)=j}}
               \theta_\pi}{Z(h)}\qquad(j\in[k]).
 \label{eq:behavioral-conversion}$$ If $Z(h)=0$, choose any probability vector, for example one concentrated on label $1$. Induction along a fixed request sequence shows that the probability of each label prefix under these choices is the total $\theta$-weight of policies consistent with that prefix. Indeed, when this weight is positive the next factor in <a href="#eq:behavioral-conversion" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:behavioral-conversion">[eq:behavioral-conversion]</a> is exactly the ratio of the extended prefix weight to the old weight; when it is zero the prefix is unreachable. Thus the complete label strings, and therefore their costs, have the same law as in the original mixture on each $\sigma_i$. Set $p_h$ arbitrarily at histories outside $\mathcal H_0$ to obtain the asserted policy on all of $X$. Formula <a href="#eq:behavioral-conversion" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:behavioral-conversion">[eq:behavioral-conversion]</a> conditions only on consistency of past choices along a specified request prefix, not on an assumed distribution of requests. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-66"></a>
#### Compactness over all finite histories

<a id="stmt-lem-compact-policy"></a>
<div id="lem:compact-policy" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:all-inputs-distinct"></a>
**Lemma 35** (One policy for all finite inputs). *For every metric space $X$ and every distinct tuple $s\in X^k$, there is a single randomized causal policy $A_s$ such that $$\mathbb E\mathop{\mathrm{cost}}_{A_s,s}(\sigma)\le\alpha\mathop{\mathrm{OPT}}_s(\sigma)
       \qquad\text{for every finite request sequence }\sigma\text{ in }X.
 \label{eq:all-inputs-distinct}$$ For each fixed input its decisions and cost are measurable functions of its internal randomness. Neither its rule nor its randomization depends on an eventual horizon.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-compact-policy"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-compact-policy</summary>

*Proof.* The set of all decision histories is $$\mathcal H=\bigcup_{t\ge1}\bigl(X^t\times[k]^{t-1}\bigr).$$ Let $$\Delta_k=\{(p(1),\ldots,p(k))\in[0,1]^k:
                       \textstyle\sum_{j=1}^kp(j)=1\},
 \qquad \mathcal P=\prod_{h\in\mathcal H}\Delta_k.$$ Thus a point of $\mathcal P$ specifies a behavioral policy at all histories. Each factor $\Delta_k$ is compact, so Tychonoff’s theorem makes $\mathcal P$ compact in the product topology (Kelley 1955, chap. 5, Theorem 13) <a id="pm-cite-33"></a>[[Kelley55](#ref-Kelley55)]; see also (Tychonoff 1935) <a id="pm-cite-34"></a>[[Tychonoff35](#ref-Tychonoff35)]. This application uses the usual set-theoretic choice principle and permits an arbitrary index set $\mathcal H$.

<a id="eq:cost-polynomial"></a>
First record the exact dependence of a fixed-input expected cost on these coordinates. For $\sigma=(x_1,\ldots,x_m)$ and a label string $j=(j_1,\ldots,j_m)\in[k]^m$, let $c_s(\sigma,j)$ be its deterministic service cost from $s$. Explicitly, begin with the tuple $s$, replace coordinate $j_t$ by $x_t$ at step $t$, and sum the distances replaced. These costs are finite, even when $X$ is unbounded, since a fixed input and the starting tuple contain only finitely many points. For $p\in\mathcal P$ the expected cost is $$C_\sigma(p)=\sum_{j\in[k]^m}c_s(\sigma,j)
       \prod_{t=1}^m
       p_{((x_1,\ldots,x_t),(j_1,\ldots,j_{t-1}))}(j_t).
 \label{eq:cost-polynomial}$$ For the empty sequence this is zero. For any fixed nonempty sequence, it is a polynomial in finitely many coordinates of $p$, hence a continuous function of those coordinates. Consequently the constraint <a id="eq:closed-cost-constraint"></a>$$C_\sigma(p)\le\alpha\mathop{\mathrm{OPT}}_s(\sigma)
 \label{eq:closed-cost-constraint}$$ is closed in those finitely many factors.

For each finite input $\sigma$, let $$E_\sigma=\{p\in\mathcal P:
 C_\sigma(p)\le\alpha\mathop{\mathrm{OPT}}_s(\sigma)\}.$$ These sets are closed by the finite-coordinate continuity just proved. Every finite subcollection has nonempty intersection by Lemma <a href="#lem:finite-minimax" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:finite-minimax">34</a>: that lemma supplies a behavioral policy defined on all histories which satisfies all the selected constraints. The finite-intersection property in the compact space $\mathcal P$ therefore gives $$\bigcap_\sigma E_\sigma\ne\varnothing.$$ Choose $p$ in this intersection. No bound on the diameter or cardinality of $X$ enters this argument. Boundedness is needed only for the cost of one fixed finite input, which was established before Equation <a href="#eq:cost-polynomial" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:cost-polynomial">[eq:cost-polynomial]</a>.

It remains to realize these choices as one randomized online policy. At each request use the current history $h$ and choose the next label with probabilities $p_h(1),\ldots,p_h(k)$. For example, take independent uniform random numbers in $[0,1)$ and choose the first label whose cumulative probability exceeds the next number. Only the revealed request prefix and past labels enter this rule. One can also implement all times on the single probability space $[0,1)$ with Lebesgue measure: subdivide its current interval into half-open pieces with relative lengths $p_h(1),\ldots,p_h(k)$, choose the piece containing the initial uniform point, and continue within that piece. Pieces of length zero are empty. On a fixed finite input, the set of seeds giving any specified label prefix is an interval whose length is the product of the corresponding probabilities. Therefore each label choice and the finite total cost are measurable, and their expectation is precisely <a href="#eq:cost-polynomial" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:cost-polynomial">[eq:cost-polynomial]</a>.

In particular, the policy is defined for every history before any input or horizon is chosen. Measurability here is exactly that required for each fixed oblivious input; no probability distribution on the request space, or joint measurability in a random request variable, enters the construction. The inequalities already proved for $p$ give <a href="#eq:all-inputs-distinct" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:all-inputs-distinct">[eq:all-inputs-distinct]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-67"></a>
#### Repeated starting positions and the final bound

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-compact-policy-2"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-compact-policy</summary>

*Proof of Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a>.* Fix $k\ge2$, a metric space $(X,d)$ with at least $k+1$ points, and any labeled starting tuple $s\in X^k$, allowing repetitions. Choose a tuple $u\in X^k$ of distinct points, and put <a id="eq:initial-matching-distance"></a>$$D=\mathsf M(s,u)
   =\min_{\pi\text{ a permutation of }[k]}
          \sum_{i=1}^k d(s_i,u_{\pi(i)}).
 \label{eq:initial-matching-distance}$$ Such a tuple exists by the cardinality assumption. The number $D$ is finite, even in an unbounded metric, since it is the minimum of finitely many finite sums.

For every finite input $\sigma$, choose an optimal legal trajectory from $s$. Its existence follows from the finitely many label strings for that input. Apply Lemma <a href="#lem:lazy-simulation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:lazy-simulation">2</a> to follow this trajectory from the initial tuple $u$. This gives a legal service from $u$ of cost at most $\mathop{\mathrm{OPT}}_s(\sigma)+D$. This use of the lemma is a pathwise comparison of offline services and does not require computing that trajectory online. Consequently <a id="eq:offline-start-comparison"></a>$$\mathop{\mathrm{OPT}}_u(\sigma)\le\mathop{\mathrm{OPT}}_s(\sigma)+D.
 \label{eq:offline-start-comparison}$$

<a id="eq:repeated-start-bound"></a>
<a id="eq:final-additive-constant"></a>
By Lemma <a href="#lem:compact-policy" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:compact-policy">35</a>, one online policy $A_u$ from $u$ satisfies <a href="#eq:all-inputs-distinct" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:all-inputs-distinct">[eq:all-inputs-distinct]</a> for every finite input. Run this policy virtually, and follow its configurations from $s$ by Lemma <a href="#lem:lazy-simulation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:lazy-simulation">2</a>. The resulting policy $A$ is causal and can be strongly lazy. Minimum matchings can be selected by a fixed order on the finite set of label permutations, so this simulation also preserves measurability on each fixed input. Pathwise it adds at most $D$ to the virtual cost, and therefore $$\begin{aligned}
 \mathbb E\mathop{\mathrm{cost}}_{A,s}(\sigma)
 &\le \mathbb E\mathop{\mathrm{cost}}_{A_u,u}(\sigma)+D \notag\\
 &\le \alpha\mathop{\mathrm{OPT}}_u(\sigma)+D \notag\\
 &\le \alpha\mathop{\mathrm{OPT}}_s(\sigma)+(\alpha+1)D.
 \label{eq:repeated-start-bound}
\end{aligned}$$ The explicit additive constant $$B=\bigl((2a+1)(1+\log(k+1))^2+1\bigr)\mathsf M(s,u)
 \label{eq:final-additive-constant}$$ depends only on the metric, $k$, and the fixed choices of starting tuples. It is independent of the input and its length. When $s$ already has distinct entries we may take $u=s$, giving $D=B=0$.

Finally, for $k\ge2$ we have $\log(k+1)\ge\log3>1$ and hence $$(1+\log(k+1))^2\le4(\log(k+1))^2.$$ Thus <a href="#eq:repeated-start-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:repeated-start-bound">[eq:repeated-start-bound]</a> proves the theorem with the single absolute constant $C=4(2a+1)$ and the finite $B$ in <a href="#eq:final-additive-constant" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:final-additive-constant">[eq:final-additive-constant]</a>. All policy choices were fixed for the specified metric and initial tuple, so the same policy serves every finite sequence and every horizon. ◻

</details>

  "figures": [
    {
      "caption": "A subtree amount is split between its children and a park at its own vertex. The park is included as one more child target during rounding. A fixed tree vertex can have a moving representative in the original metric, so its parked mass requires a separate travel estimate. The picture records conservation and does not assert that the original metric is a Euclidean tree.",
      "figure_id": "fig:parked-tree",
      "pdf_page": null,
      "source_evidence": [

      "caption": "Movement accounting. The partitions and the tree allocation each contribute one logarithm. Rounding has an absolute loss. Moving the representatives costs $O(V+ℓP+ℓQ)$, so it preserves the final squared logarithm. All additive terms shown are uniform in the finite input law and horizon.",
